# Sparkle Bureaucracy — the complete record

*One file, everything — assembled 7 August 2026 at Fatima's instruction as the single source of truth for everything that ever was Sparkle Bureaucracy: the prototype, the journey, the research, the evidence. **Part I** is the submission story (video script → criteria). **Part II** is the full record: every lore document inlined verbatim, headings adjusted to fit one hierarchy, provenance noted per section, cross-links resolved to in-file anchors. Not inlined: the raw Matrix export JSON (rendered instead as a readable transcript in the final appendix) and the diary's browsable wiki app (`index.html`/`app.js`/`styles.css` — code, not prose). Assessment begins 10 August 2026.*

## Part I — the submission story


*One file, everything. Drafted 6 August 2026 from the full [`lore/`](#part-ii) corpus for the assessment beginning 10 August (results 5 September; pass = ⅔ faculty vote on six criteria). Workflow: review each part here, paste into the submission Google Doc, add photos where the `> Add to the doc:` slots say so. Voice reference: [the raw speech capture](#doc-2026-08-06-the-story-of-sb-video-intro-draft).*

**Contents:** 1. Video intro script · 2. Introducing the project · 3. Timeline of projects and activations · 4. Learnings · 5. Roadmap · 6. Addressing the criteria · 7. Evidence shelf (quotes, testimonials, people) · 8. Thank you

#### A note on format, for faculty

This submission deliberately doesn't follow the Prototype Work Diary template's order. Sparkle Bureaucracy is an outlier against the template — a network and an experiment lab, not a single tool — so the submission is formatted as the story makes sense, starting from the video intro. The template's skeleton is fully preserved underneath; only the encounter is redesigned. (Which is the project's whole thesis.) Every kind of evidence the template asks for exists, here or one link away:

| Template section | Where it lives |
| --- | --- |
| 1. Project Summary | Part 2 below |
| 2. Project Map — features, risks, dependencies | Parts 2 & 5 below; risk registers in the [experiments log](#doc-experiments) and [template-shaped diary §2](#doc-submission-diary) |
| 3. Weekly Diary Entries | [`lore/weekly/`](#doc-weekly-readme) — nine dated entries, February–July |
| 4. Prior Art Log | [prior-art.md](#doc-prior-art) + the [100-case research corpus](#doc-research-longlist-readme) |
| 5. User & Community Engagement Log | [engagement-log.md](#doc-engagement-log), plus Parts 3 & 7 below |
| 6. Prototype & Experiments Log | [experiments.md](#doc-experiments) · [prospective-experiments.md](#doc-prospective-experiments) · [experiment-research-sprint.md](#doc-experiment-research-sprint) |
| 7. Design Notes | ritual/pipeline flows in the [template-shaped diary §7](#doc-submission-diary); live artifacts linked throughout Part 3 |
| 8. Technical Notes | [technical-notes.md](#doc-technical-notes) |
| 9. Faculty Feedback Tracking | [faculty-feedback.md](#doc-faculty-feedback), plus the mentor record woven through Parts 3–4 |
| 10. Readiness Checklist | Part 6 below — the six criteria addressed directly |
| 11. Final Reflection | Parts 4–6, especially the closing of Part 6 |

One more deliberate choice: the timeline counts cohort-and-house convening — Code Club, coworking days, co-creation Fridays — alongside the experiments. The prototype is technically not every contribution you make to the house; but what should actually be measured is **building your thing while building the cohort at the same time**. For a network prototype, the cohort-building isn't overhead — it's the practice. The same principle cuts the other way: the timeline ends with a short list of house contributions deliberately left *outside* the umbrella, because a project that claims everything measures nothing.

### 1. Video intro script (3–5 minutes)

*≈780 words ≈ 5 minutes at a natural pace. The three **[trim]** blocks are optional: dropping them gives a ≈3½-minute cut. Italics are b-roll suggestions.*

As AI continues to improve and permeate through everyday life, I find myself watching the gap between the people who have AI access and those who don't get wider — and that worries me.

Growing up in the US as the child of immigrants, I've seen what happens to skilled workers from other countries. I've seen neuroscientists and engineers from abroad working at gas stations and driving taxis to get their children educated in America — all because their degrees weren't legible to the system.

I believe AI is going to make immigrants out of us all. I believe it's going to widen this gap — make it more and more expensive to access, to learn, and to teach.

*[b-roll: hack night photos, the Hall]*

Sparkle Bureaucracy, as you heard earlier this year, was originally a network of people using AI to explore optimistic organisational and service futures — because SB is my life. It's working in and outside of government institutions. It's volunteering at hack nights from Boston to New York to Toronto, Ottawa, and London — the one in Ontario. It's understanding that digital access to good civic services is crucial to a democracy. That's where the name comes from: running experiments that illustrate the power of technology, and how these emerging innovations can help us envision the possibilities in govtech.

But SB evolved over the course of this fellowship. I came in thinking I wanted to run experiments on things like DMVs — what does a better licence renewal process look like? What does liquid democracy look like if the results are livestreamed while you can still change your vote? I ran an immigration scheme at my own birthday — passports, visa types, credentials — and watched the conversations it sparked.

**[trim 1]** And the experimentation went beyond bureaucratic processes. I ran our cohort reunion as a spy game — to get around the fact that nobody really wanted to answer "how is your prototype going?" one more time. It was a resounding success, because competition and games lower the bar to social interaction.

**[trim 2]** I started hosting Clawclub when agent tooling suddenly became something everyone could use — because I'd been testing it in ways that excited me, and I wanted a community to be excited with. Sometimes 10 or 12 people, sometimes 60 in the hall — building, sharing, even selling each other hardware.

*[b-roll: Clawclub, the reunion, Birthday Passports]*

And all these communities — the tech-for-good people, the emerging-technology builders, the ones I met in the Hall throughout the fellowship: the RightsCon folks, the Protocol for Publishers, the vTaiwan delegation, the technologist journalists — they helped me identify, collect, and narrow the characteristics of the community I want to build.

In parallel, this fellowship was about building confidence. I've grappled with *why me* — why am I the person to do this? *Why now* — why does yet another network need to exist? And *why not the others* — London is full of great networks doing things around AI. I still can't answer those to full confidence. But Hannah O'Rourke says legitimacy is constructed — so for now, I borrow the answers of the people around me, who reflect back that the overlap of sparkle × bureaucracy is unique, and that when I speak about agents at events, I am, apparently, the expert in the room.

Here's what I can say with confidence: now is the window of opportunity to close the gap. As models get more advanced, as our agents move to the cloud and get operated remotely, as compute gets cheaper, most of the population will end up using AI tools they don't understand. I want them to understand what they're using. And I want the communities aligned with civic tech, tech for good, open source, and govtech to be prepared for what's coming — and to use these tools to keep building new possibilities for themselves.

It's why the AI & Campaigning conference mattered so much to me. Campaigners weren't "my people" per se — but working with Hannah to contextualise their problems and build them Campaign Factory showed me how important it is to show people the power of these tools. Someone told me Campaign Factory built for her, in a session, what would have taken her five months of research.

**[trim 3]** Same with my keynote at the charity evaluators' festival — Evaluation 2030: shift your thinking from prompt-based to systems-based, before the vendors arrive asking you to purchase things. Fifteen people lined up afterwards to tell me their stories, how they work, and where agents could fit.

In the moment, these engagements didn't fit a single mandate. Reflecting back on the year, they were all pieces of the same puzzle.

*[b-roll: the diary / lore repo scrolling]*

And honestly — I don't know if "Sparkle Bureaucracy" is the right name anymore. Making bureaucracy more sparkly is my long-term life's mission. But as long as this window is open, I want to be here: someone who understands the technology sufficiently, who tests the latest of it, and who builds the bridge from the tech to the people I'm speaking to — translating what it does, what it means for their work, and how they can use it to amplify themselves.

I don't yet know what the network's new name will be. So for this prototype, I present the accumulation of experiments, projects, and ideas that led me here — and the roadmap towards what's next.

---

### 2. Introducing the project

**Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network for closing the AI gap for civic communities, run as an experiment lab.**

It started as a bet about bureaucracy: you can keep the entire procedural skeleton — the forms, the queues, the checkpoints, the stamps — and change only the intent, and the whole experience transforms. "Bureaucracy" carries the weight; it signals unexpected seriousness, the way "governance" does. "Sparkle" says it doesn't have to be miserable — it lowers threat, invites participation, and makes experimentation socially acceptable in environments that are usually defensive. The working ratio, on the record since the spring: **80% credible institution, 20% sparkle.**

Over the fellowship, that bet met a more urgent problem: AI is arriving like a new country arrives — suddenly skills need translating, credentials don't parse, and the people who can't afford the translation fall behind. The communities that should be shaping this technology — civic tech, tech for good, open source, govtech, the grassroots — are the ones most at risk of being priced out of it.

#### The problem statement

We are a network of people using AI to explore optimistic futures.

1. The state is failing in many countries.
2. AI offers an opportunity to fix this.
3. We as technologists need to make sure people working at the grassroots are not left behind.
4. We can use AI to prototype and imagine a different future.

#### How it works — three registers

There isn't one thing here; there are three, and they don't need collapsing ([What I actually care about](#doc-2026-07-31-what-i-care-about)):

- **The method — the fun thing.** Playful games, rituals, and working prototypes that let people *feel* how a system works instead of being told: a border regime at a birthday, a cohort reunion as a spy game, an evaluation run as public theatre.
- **The moment — the temporary opportunity.** Civic and political groups have an AI upskilling gap, especially around agents, and it's closing on someone else's terms. While the window is open, SB tests the newest technology and builds the bridge to the people who need it.
- **The horizon — govtech.** The long-term mission stays: digital access to good civic services is load-bearing for democracy, and bureaucracy can sparkle. 

It reduces to two questions anyone can ask: **"What can I use AI for?"** and **"What can AI do for me?"**

#### What "sparkle" means, precisely

After a 100-case research sprint across six lanes ([research-longlist/](#doc-research-longlist-readme)), the working definition:

> **Sparkle is the designed charge in a consequential procedure: rules and power become possible to feel and understand, people gain meaningful room to participate, question, or act, and the encounter leaves behind civic capability rather than a decorative veneer.**

And the bar an SB experiment must clear ([prospective experiments](#doc-prospective-experiments)): an **institutional skeleton** (a recognisable ritual — queue, permit, court, ledger, passport), an **affective re-skin** (participatory and legible without pretending power has disappeared), **decision pressure** (something is accepted, rejected, classified, delegated, or made contestable), and a **durable artifact** (a receipt, ledger, passport, constitution, or protocol that could harden into a reusable pattern). If an idea doesn't change who can act, how judgment is made, what becomes visible, or how a decision can be contested — it isn't strong enough yet.

#### What the prototype consists of

The network is the prototype; the experiments are its releases; the diary is the evidence.

- **The experiments and activations** — the dated sequence in [Part 3](#3-the-timeline--projects-and-activations-february-to-august-2026).
- **The working artifacts** — [Sparkle Border Authority](https://sparkle-border-authority.vercel.app/), [Project Mirror & the V1–V15 awards pipeline](https://2025.newspeak.house/awards), [Campaign Factory](https://campaign-factory.vercel.app), Conference Pulse, Topic — plus the site and mailing list at sparklebureaucracy.org.
- **The open diary** — [`lore/`](#part-ii): weekly entries, experiment cards with risks and ethics notes, engagement and prior-art logs, a 100-case research corpus, and the essays where the project interrogated its own assumptions in public.

One honest caveat, stated up front: the name "Sparkle Bureaucracy" may not survive the network's next phase — making bureaucracy sparkle is the founder's long-term horizon, but the urgent work is the gap. What's assessed here is the accumulated practice, the communities engaged, and the roadmap.

### 3. The timeline — projects and activations, February to August 2026

*Chronological, from [pivotal-moments.md](#doc-pivotal-moments) and the [weekly entries](#doc-weekly-readme). Each entry has an `Add to the doc` slot for the Google Doc pass.*

#### 14 December – 1 April — the field-notes room

Before any of the events: a public-by-design Matrix room, `field-notes: fatima`, with an explicit contract that anything posted could become public. 294 messages over three and a half months — heaviest in the December–January stretch (205 of them) — me thinking out loud, with a handful of others dropping in (including Gamithra's 29 March suggestion that became the conversational-cohort-agents idea). The rabbit holes weren't all on-mission, but power and AI kept surfacing: the surveillance-pricing investigation (a NYT dynamic-pricing video found via TikTok, followed all the way down to disclosure law and audit tooling — invisible algorithmic decisions as a legibility problem), shadow governance and needing permission in rule-light systems, AI system observability and the failures standard evals can't see, agentic workflow risk. Fifteen arXiv papers, a dozen GitHub repos, the Civic Tech Field Guide, gov.uk. When the room went quiet on 1 April, the export became the seed corpus: every fragment was catalogued into the [research inventory](#doc-research-inventory) (Part 4 is essentially this room, itemised), and the raw export lives in [`lore/data/`](#doc-field-notes-transcript). The diary didn't start as a diary — it started as a room where thinking was public.

> **Add to the doc:** a screenshot of the room, and one or two favourite rabbit holes told properly.

#### 2 February — Code Club (inherited, then evolved)

Code Club came to me from the previous cohort and the institution — its earlier run is on the house calendar through autumn 2025 — and I ran it every other week from 2 February: soliciting workshops people actually wanted, themed sessions on trending topics (openclaw among them), discussing and building together. Over the year it evolved with the cohort's needs: **Code Club** (2 Feb – 16 Mar) → **Prototype Club** (from 13 Apr) → **Prototype Coworking Day** (17 Jul). The clearest example of the thesis: building your thing while building the cohort at the same time.

> **Add to the doc:** themed-session list, photos, attendance range.

#### 17 February — Clawclub #1

The first hack night, started in response to the sudden arrival of usable agent tooling: I'd been testing openclaw in ways that excited me and wanted a community to share ideas with and be excited together. The series ran through the spring — sometimes 10–12 people, sometimes 60 filling the hall, building, sharing, occasionally selling each other hardware. The Claw agents (Moltbook, penpals, research pipelines) grew out of this community — playful multi-agent systems with real outputs, whimsy and rigour in the same hand.

> **Add to the doc:** Clawclub photos (a light night + a packed night), attendance range, one artifact built at a club night.

#### 11 March — Sparkle Border Authority: "Birthday Passports"

A full border-crossing ritual run live at my birthday — identity checks, purpose-of-visit declarations, secondary screening, visa classes with privileges, printed A6 visa stickers, checkpoints, admin overrides, and a live immigration stats dashboard ([the app](https://sparkle-border-authority.vercel.app/), still deployed). The language layer did real work: "sparkle compliance," "diplomatic glitter" — compliance felt like joining a story world, and even rejection flows felt narrative, not punitive. People co-created the fiction. Ed Saperia saw it and said: *that's Sparkle Bureaucracy.* The founding proof: same procedural skeleton, different intent, and the whole experience transforms.

> **Add to the doc:** party photos, a printed visa artifact, the kiosk screen, guest reactions.

#### 15 March — Clawclub #2

> **Add to the doc:** blurb + photo (Fatima).

#### 25 March — Lumina House takeover of Ration Club

Cross-community collaboration — social choreography at scale. Not a software project: a demonstration that the convening instinct works outside technical contexts, and an early data point for the alliance-building strategy.

> **Add to the doc:** event photos, a line on what the takeover involved.

#### 30 March — Project Mirror, at the PoliTech Awards showcase

The capstone of leading the fellowship's open awards process: 321 political-technology projects evaluated through a pipeline whose every version (V1–V15) was published on GitHub with rationale for every change — from random scoring, through structured dossiers (Civic Tech Field Guide taxonomy, OpenAlex citations, ProPublica financials), to values-as-constitutions and multi-jury agent systems. The framing: rankings are political; openness makes the trade-offs discussable. The cohort was pulled in — Davit on values taxonomy, Gamithra's ITN/A framework, Alexandra on aggregation, Jamie on synthetic users, Fran on People's Choice — and the audience ran its own live ranking (QR-code criteria → agent-generated ranking) before seeing the committee's.

Project Mirror asked: what if all 18 cohort members had been in the room? Eighteen synthetic evaluator constitutions inferred from public records — criteria plus value modifiers (boosts for community ownership and under-resourced civic contexts; reductions for VC funding; a popularity discount to counter LLM familiarity bias) — then a Borda-count grand jury with strategic reasoning enabled. Winner: Liquid Feedback, stable under every manipulation attempt (18-point lead, 13 deliberation arguments, no revisions). And the tension that stays: Asil reflecting live on what it feels like to have an agent built from your public record. The inference is not consent.

> **Add to the doc:** showcase photos, the 18-agents-on-one-screen screenshot, a constitution excerpt, [2025.newspeak.house/awards](https://2025.newspeak.house/awards).

#### 2 April — the prototype diary is founded; the Previews meet Sparkle Bureaucracy

The Matrix field-notes room went quiet on 1 April; the next morning the chat was exported (294 messages, December 2025 – April 2026) and the diary repo was born the same day — which was also the day of the fellowship's **Prototype Previews**. The checkpoint arrived, and the diary became the deliverable: research inventory, sensemaking map, synthesis, and a README rewritten six times in one afternoon until it was in my voice, with the liquid democracy experiment and named partners added by evening.

That evening (pitch at 21:50), the framing met the faculty for the first time — and the [feedback](#doc-2026-04-02-prototype-previews-feedback) shaped what followed: Ed — *"this will go very far… straightforward, generative, and accessible… an AI angle which makes it fresh."* Matt — *"an experiment lab for imagining how emerging tech can change the way our systems work without abandoning them"* — the phrase the prototype grew into. Hannah offered intros and pilots and pointed at Campaign Lab's [pipresist.uk](https://pipresist.uk/) — the seed of everything that followed with them. Chris Owen prescribed *Radical Help*. Emily Mayhew asked why public consultation is "such a dull awful bureaucratic cold process" and offered her digital-replica work as overlap with synthetic voice. Anouk Ruhaak demanded the sparkle passport be open-sourced — [it is](https://github.com/sugaroverflow/sparkle-border-authority). Every one of those got a response, tracked in [faculty-feedback.md](#doc-faculty-feedback).

> **Add to the doc:** screenshot of the first commits, a previews photo or slide; the diary lives on as [`lore/`](#part-ii).

#### 8 April — Clawcon London + Clawclub #3

Clawcon London, co-organised — the club scaled up to a conference for a day.

> **Add to the doc:** Clawcon photos, attendance, programme highlight.

#### 27 April — Clawclub #4

> **Add to the doc:** blurb + photo (Fatima).

#### 29 April — Red Team, Blue Team at Ration Club

A live team button game built for the Ration Club crowd (`nwspk/ration-club-button-game`) — the games-as-social-infrastructure thread, tested again between the border ritual and the spy game.

> **Add to the doc:** game photos, how the teams played out.

#### 8 May — Rights UnCon

The RightsCon-adjacent unconference at the Hall — one of the communities that helped narrow what SB's own community should be.

> **Add to the doc:** blurb + session notes (Fatima).

#### 9–24 May — sparklebureaucracy.org and the mailing list

The website goes from AI-generated design to real project: first build pass on 9 May, the Astro migration decision on 11 May (recorded in a decision doc, old version preserved), look-and-feel work, and on 24 May the Buttondown mailing list goes live behind a provider-adapter API — "a mailing list and eventually an event series," the diary's network-building commitment, made real.

> **Add to the doc:** site screenshot, mailing-list signup count to date.

#### 13 May — Cohort Reunion: Spy Edition (NHIFA)

Nobody in my cohort wanted to answer "how is your prototype going?" one more time — so the reunion became a spy agency. The NHIFA Field Console: each player's mission revealed in-app, keyword guesses, handler bonus codes, submissions locked at 21:45, a paced reveal on the projector at 22:00 — while Director and Handler missions lived on paper, with printed dossier mini-zines (an A6-onto-A3 imposition pipeline) and reset slips as physical artifacts. Then something SBA never had: a *post-event ritual* — personal debrief cards on a debrief page in the days after. Networking as a game on the intake → process → decision → artifact → reveal skeleton. A resounding success: competition and games lower the bar to social interaction.

> **Add to the doc:** dossier mini-zine photos, the Field Console, a mission card, a debrief card (with permission).

#### 24 May — OpenClaw 101 workshop for Campaign Lab

A workshop run for Campaign Lab — "agents, skills, factories, and the future of AI workflows" — the hack-night teaching thread formalised, and the visible start of the partnership that produced Campaign Factory and Conference Pulse in July. The translation model in miniature: meet a community inside its own work, show what the tools can do, build with them rather than at them.

> **Add to the doc:** workshop photo, a slide from the deck.

#### 12 June — Agent Craft 2026, "AI for the Rest of Us" keynote: "The Agent Hype Is Real. So Is the Mess"

The translation work for a general audience — enthusiasm and honesty in the same talk, and the framing arc I keep teaching: prompt → system → loops. The day produced two live leads: Tom Fieldman of mySociety — intrigued, offering ideas for experiments I could run, and promising an intro to a practitioner using AI agents to fill out government forms — and Paul Silcock, a CTO building production AI agents, who thought Sparkle Bureaucracy was amazing and asked me to come present to his team.

> **Add to the doc:** blurb + photo/slide (Fatima).

#### 15 June — CHEW Festival keynote: "Evaluation 2030"

The keynote that opened the charity evaluators' festival: shift from prompt-based to systems-based thinking before agents arrive — articulate your work as a system first, so when vendors show up selling things, you know where agents actually fit. Fifteen people lined up afterwards to tell me their specific stories, how they work, and where they could implement agents. And the demand didn't stop at the door: over the following two months the organisers wrote three times — attendees kept giving great feedback and requesting more — asking for a follow-up blog to share the insights with people who couldn't attend.

> **Add to the doc:** keynote photo, a slide, the "15 people" moment attributed if possible; the festival blog is a pending output.

#### 19 June — the lab gets its operating system

In one day: the diary is reorganised around the fellowship template (submission diary, experiment cards, engagement log, faculty feedback tracker, prior-art log), and an [experiment research sprint](#doc-experiment-research-sprint) compares candidate first experiments — concluding: keep Sparkle Bureaucracy itself as the assessed prototype, run lab infrastructure quietly, and pick one public-facing flagship (top candidate: a verification/exception-handling ritual, the direct successor to Sparkle Border Authority).

> **Add to the doc:** nothing needed — link the docs.

#### 23 June — the nine-experiment shortlist, and Topic begins

The same evening, two things: the [prospective experiments idea bank](#doc-prospective-experiments) lands — nine ranked candidates (Public Voice Customs, Mirror Commons, The Exception Window, Permission Passport Office, The Value Court, Claw Civil Service, Shadow Labour Claims Office, Liquid Mandate Assembly, Sparkle Border Field Kit) with the four-part "what counts" bar — and the **Topic** build kicks off with 43 commits in a day. Topic (topic.forum) is collaborative forum software — propose topics, vote with hearts, share availability, shape a schedule together — "a Newspeak House × Sparkle Bureaucracy production," finished like a product through July (QA sprints, feeds, CI, docs).

> **Add to the doc:** Topic screenshots, deployment status, who's using it.

#### 16 July — Campaign Factory + Conference Pulse, at the AI & Campaigning Conference

Built with Campaign Lab in an intense ten-day sprint (~122 commits): give [Campaign Factory](https://campaign-factory.vercel.app) a UK local or public-policy problem and a place, and it researches the decision route, maps power and pressure, proposes strategy and tactics, and drafts campaign materials — *showing the work as it happens*. Conference Pulse ran alongside as the live companion for the conference at Newspeak House: schedule, anonymous per-panel comments, votes, an organiser dashboard, a big-screen room view. The week after, a free-access code went onto the house key so "bring your own API key" stopped being the price of admission — a deliberate accessibility move with a kill-switch as the safety valve. Someone told me Campaign Factory built for her, in a session, what would have taken her five months of research.

And the room's verdict was measured live, by our own instrument: Conference Pulse's end-of-panel vote on "Agents: working tools or over-hyped?" (with Hannah and Ed on the panel) came back 19 ballots — **9 could be useful with work, 7 already useful and real, 3 just hype** — over a comment wall running from "Incredible! would love to see a write up of how this was built" to the sharpest question in the room: how do we stop people's first experience being slop campaign plans? That question is the quality bar Campaign Factory carries forward. [Full feedback capture](#doc-2026-07-16-campaign-factory-panel-feedback).

> **Add to the doc:** conference photos, a Campaign Factory output, the Pulse big-screen view, the "five months" quote attributed if she's willing, free-access usage numbers.

#### 17 July — Prototype Coworking Day

Code Club's final form, via Prototype Club: the day after the AI & Campaigning Conference, the inherited convening reshaped around what the cohort actually needed as prototypes came due — sit together, push the assessed work forward.

> **Add to the doc:** whether it recurred, photos, who came.

#### 24–31 July — the reckoning

A session with Ed and James on how networks are born (mission + taste, inspirations, let people show up); a weekend where the advice wouldn't settle; the essay that worked out why ([The resistance is information](#doc-2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information)); a 100-case research sprint on inspirations ([research-longlist/](#doc-research-longlist-readme)); then an hour with Hannah that turned the tangle into [a problem statement and three registers](#doc-2026-07-31-what-i-care-about), and Peyman's nudge toward a tangible flagship. The week the project understood itself — in public, in the diary.

> **Add to the doc:** nothing to photograph — quote the essays.

#### Throughout — the Ed thread (roughly 7,400 messages)

The mentorship didn't happen in sessions; it happened in a near-daily WhatsApp thread with Ed Saperia running from before the fellowship to this week — summarised here, raw chat kept private. The dated spine: **16 March**, the brand session — SB as an update of civic tech for AI, "bureaucracy" as unexpected seriousness, unthreatening on purpose, the vTaiwan ↔ pol.is model, Sparkle Bureaucracy Camp and Awards as future formats — the framing that became [research inventory Part 1](#doc-research-inventory). **26–27 March**, calendar-as-curation, and the awards-eve reassurance that "something sparkle bureaucracy themed" was enough. **14 April**, the observation that Prototype Club is itself an SB project — the building-the-cohort thesis, seen from outside. **28 April**, the artifact principle: every interaction with SB should leave you holding something — a certificate, a key, a title. **13 July**, the reframe that set up the endgame: SB is a container; list the projects that are SB-coded — and my answer, that I love SB but it needs a tangible project headlining it. **17 July**, Ed arriving with his own researched list of twenty AI-powered civic projects for the constellation.

> **Add to the doc:** nothing — the chat stays private; the dated rows in [faculty-feedback.md](#doc-faculty-feedback) are the citable record.

#### Fridays since 12 December — Content Co-Creation Fridays

Co-founded with Francesca Galli, with Matt Stempeck's sponsorship — because I was inspired to work in the open. A weekly "shut up & write"-style focus session at the house for faculty and fellowship candidates, first on the house calendar 12 December 2025: set a goal, pick focused or collaborative mode, check in on progress — research write-ups, blog posts and explainers, decks, demo scripts, videos, visuals ([Luma](https://luma.com/o7t15995)). The working-in-public method, made into a shared weekly ritual.

> **Add to the doc:** how many sessions ran, a session photo, one thing that shipped out of it.

#### Ongoing through the year — the AI safety study group

A reading-and-discussion group on AI safety materials, with people from neighbouring institutions invited in — the "considering the implications" thread running underneath all the building.

#### 20 July – 6 August — the pull begins

The latest evidence arrived unprompted, in the inbox, after the year's work. Oxfam GB's campaign engagement manager, after seeing Campaign Factory at the conference — "amazing to see the platform you have built" — asked for a team demo and Q&A; a September workshop is being organised with Asil Sidahmed, pairing her humanitarian-aid and digital-rights leadership with the agentic workflows. Matt Stempeck offered theory-of-change material for Campaign Factory's strategy section, asked to share it with colleagues ("and then, the world"), and with the Civic Tech Field Guide's new manager invited Hannah and me to demo at a CTFG vibe-code show & tell — plus a content-creation edition slot. The full diary — weekly entries, experiment cards, essays, research corpus, and the raw field-notes export — merged into the public site repo as [`lore/`](#part-ii); the original prototype-diary repo archived with a moved-notice. This submission is built directly on top of it.

#### What's deliberately *not* in this timeline

Not every contribution to the house belongs under the Sparkle Bureaucracy umbrella, and the line is drawn on purpose: organizing **Funding 101 for Civic & Political Tech** with Georgia Bullen (7 April) to help fellows understand funding; organizing the **PoliTech Awards committee meetings** (the evaluation methodology and Project Mirror are SB; the committee logistics were house service); co-hosting the **Censorship & Surveillance Resistant Comms** discussion with Nicholas Botti (1 March, [Luma](https://luma.com/f6u4f3pz)). They're named here once because they're part of building the cohort — but claiming them as the project would inflate it. Knowing what *isn't* the project is part of the method.

### 4. The learnings

#### 1. Same skeleton, different intent

The founding insight, proven live at Birthday Passports and repeated everywhere since: every one of these systems is a pipeline with a ritual layer on top — intake → process → decision → artifact → handoff. What varies is the intent: control or experience, compliance or engagement, opacity or legible ritual. You can change the intent without dismantling the skeleton, and the structure stays rigorous while the experience transforms. The two built proof points cover both layers: Sparkle Border Authority redesigns how procedure *feels* in lived encounter; Project Mirror redesigns how institutional *judgment* is articulated and applied.

#### 2. Games lower the bar

The reunion-as-spy-game, the button game, the border at the birthday: competition and play reliably got people into interactions they'd have avoided as networking, feedback, or compliance. The fun thing isn't decoration on the method — it *is* the method. (And the spy game added what the border never had: a post-event ritual. Debrief cards extended the experience past the room.)

#### 3. Transparency isn't trust — but it makes trade-offs discussable

Publishing all fifteen versions of the awards algorithm didn't make evaluation neutral, and people's first instinct was to find the flaw. That's the point: rankings are political, and openness moves the argument from "trust us" to "argue with this specific choice." Re-legitimisation requires redesigning the encounter, not only the disclosure. Small details carry legitimacy too — tie semantics and display choices in a ranking materially change how fair it feels.

#### 4. The inference is not consent

Project Mirror was methodologically careful — "evaluative stance," never "beliefs" — and the discomfort was still real. Asil's live reflection set a permanent ethical line: synthetic representation scales participation and undermines it in the same motion. The lab's answer is already specified in the idea bank: Mirror Commons — synthetic stand-ins people *manage themselves*, with editable evaluator passports, red lines, expiry, and revocation. From estimating people, to people governing their own estimates.

#### 5. Translation beats evangelism

The engagements that landed hardest — Campaign Factory with Campaign Lab, Evaluation 2030 with the charity evaluators — met a community *inside its own work*: their problems, their vocabulary, a system they could see themselves in. "Five months of research in a session"; fifteen people queueing after a keynote; a manager hearing about synthetic agents and saying "please build some for the team." That's what the upskilling gap closing looks like at small scale — and it reduces to the two questions: *what can I use AI for?* and *what can AI do for me?*

#### 6. Affect is broader than delight — and it has a named failure mode

The 100-case research sprint sharpened "sparkle" well past whimsy: relief, dignity, moral discomfort, certainty, empathy, and productive disagreement all count as designed affect. Status is affective infrastructure — acknowledgement, tracking, and closure matter as much as a friendly front door. Sometimes sparkle *removes* a ritual rather than decorating it (Clear My Record). Technology often works best behind a trusted person, not as the sole civic interface (Haqdarshak). And the clearest warning pattern across all the prior art: **affective legitimacy without recourse** — a beautiful, friendly process that leaves power untouched and unchallengeable. The thing SB must never build.

#### 7. The resistance is information

The hardest learning was about the founder, and it's in the diary on purpose. An experiment says *judge the thing I made*; a network says *trust my judgment about what matters* — and I trusted my execution far more than my right to originate authority. So I concealed the programme (nine experiments already shortlisted, fourteen moments already run) out of fear it would sound frivolous, which made the concept look vague, which earned advice to go lighter, which made it feel more insubstantial. Naming the loop broke it. On *why me*: legitimacy is constructed — borrow the answers of the people around you until your own catch up.

#### 8. The three registers don't need collapsing

The final week produced the structure everything now hangs on: the fun thing is the **method**, the AI upskilling window is the **audience and the moment**, govtech is the **horizon**. Six months of thrash came from trying to compress three cares into one mission statement; holding them as three registers resolved it in an afternoon.

#### 9. The lab's own bureaucracy is an experiment — and it ran

Experiment 1 in the [experiments log](#doc-experiments) proposed an agent-assisted diary: notes, commits, and logs turned into structured, human-approved diary entries. The reconstructed weekly entries and this submission package are its first real run — every reconstructed entry marked as such, gaps listed for human correction, voice preserved by keeping the founder's raw text as the reference. The lab practices the bureaucracy it preaches: legible process, visible provenance, human judgment kept in the loop.

#### What the communities around the work taught me

The video says these communities "helped me identify, collect, and narrow the characteristics of the community I want to build." Specifically:

- **vTaiwan (the delegation at the Hall)** — the power of co-creation when facing bureaucratic change; the movement-plus-tool precedent the whole roadmap leans on.
- **Protocols for Publishers (showcase + stakeholder summit at the house, 4–5 February)** — democracy dies when truth is behind a paywall and misinformation is free.
- **CADA** — there are different strategies for AI powers: infrastructure, cutting-edge research, or vertical knowledge — industry-specific models. Positioning is a choice, not a default.
- **A Bohm Dialogue on human, more-than-human, and artificial intelligences (21 February) / Thinking Gym** — people truly do not understand AI, and personify it to a degree that enrages me. And showing up as the technical expert can make people insecure enough to get defensive and stop hearing me. The bridge has to lower the temperature before it can translate — expertise announced is expertise resisted.
- **My birthday experiment** — birthdays are dictatorships: the one day you hold absolute authority and everyone consents to play along. Which is exactly why it was the safest place to run a border regime — temporary, consented power is the perfect sandbox for studying how authority feels.
- **The Majority Myth book launch, 20 May (Edmund Lewis Maklouf)** — the author found SB really interesting and very much needed if people are to get better government services; and he pointed me at the immigrant mindset in the US — what the literature calls the **model minority myth**: the belief that if you do good, work hard, and follow the rules, you'll be noticed for being "good" and rewarded. It made me ask what my true motivations for this project actually are — a question I'm still sitting with.
- **Lumina House** — cross-pollination: different communities talk about the same things in completely different languages. And a realization about the "why me" question: the people I look up to are ML/AI researchers who know far more than I do — but for the people around me, I'm the closest to that they have. That's not imposter syndrome resolved; that's what being the bridge means.
- ***Are You a Software Update?* (book launch, 30 April)** — not all forms of power announce themselves. Infrastructure built for scale carries a totalising logic, and there's a fascist tendency in it. A sharper edge on the warning pattern from the research: friendliness at scale still needs recourse.
- **Campaign Lab hack nights & the AI & Campaigning Conference** — the partner-community rhythm that produced Campaign Factory and Conference Pulse (see the timeline): show up repeatedly, build *with*, not *at*.
- **The bookshelf people handed me** — *The Utopia of Rules* (David Graeber, from Anouk Ruhaak) · *Radical Help* (Hilary Cottam, from Chris Owen) · *Incorruptible* (Eric Ries, from Theodore Keloglou) · *Who Thought This Was a Good Idea?* (Alyssa Mastromonaco, from Hannah) · *The Employees* (Olga Ravn, from Six Silberman). A few chapters into each, honestly — inspiration doesn't require completion. The list with what each changed lives in [reading-list.md](#doc-reading-list).

### 5. The roadmap

#### Near term (August–September 2026)

- **AI agents for good — hackathon.** Prototype a different future, with suggested civic topics and challenges. The network's first event under its own banner.
- **Demo night.** Projects from the hackathon, plus anyone else who has something to show.
- **Booked and queued engagements** — evidence already turning into calendar: the Oxfam GB Campaign Factory workshop with Asil Sidahmed (late September); the Lovelace brown-bag on AI optimism, in debate with their researchers (Octavia Field Reid's invitation); Paul Silcock's team presentation; the Civic Tech Field Guide content-creation show & tell (August or September — date owed to Matt); and the CHEW festival follow-up blog (three invitations outstanding).
- **A faculty brainstorm on the flagship project.** The network needs its tangible artifact — its pol.is, in the vTaiwan analogy.

#### The flagship candidates

From the Hannah session: **reimagining grant-making** · **Campaign Factory, grown up** (already live, already partnered, already testimonialed) · **a game** · and Peyman's nudge, **"What can AI do for me?" as a product** — an agent that shadows your working patterns, RescueTime-style, and suggests the tools, workflows, and skills that would amplify you: an upskilling diagnostic that meets people inside their actual work, and the artifact-between-me-and-the-audience the July reflection said the network needs.

Behind these sit the [nine specified experiments](#doc-prospective-experiments) with their two recommended routes: the **canonical tool route** (Public Voice Customs — "pol.is for synthetic-voice-era consultations"; Mirror Commons — consentful synthetic representation; The Exception Window) and the **fellowship evidence route** (The Exception Window first — the verification/exception ritual that inherits Sparkle Border Authority's grammar and keeps enforcement visible — with Claw Civil Service running quietly as lab infrastructure). Each candidate already has a smallest-credible-run spec, artifacts, evidence lists, and named risks.

#### The rhythm (determined with the community)

- **Hackathon → demo night** as the anchor cycle.
- **2× socials a year, built as experiences** — game nights, Xmas and summer; the experiments are the social infrastructure.
- **Hear-from-experts sessions** — a domain at a time, when new things ship (Andreas Varotsis and others).
- **Problem discovery** — gatherings that bring in people *with problems*, not technologists: cross-pollination, coffee chats. The network's intake ritual.
- **An organiser** to hold the rhythm.
- The supporting strategy from the mentor record: small, high-density events over big panels; a public calendar that curates the ecosystem without needing permission; "easy yes" asks (visibility, co-hosting, lightweight sponsorship, demo slots); a portfolio that includes other people's work — alliances, not just outputs.

#### Scope — so the network stays aimed

**In:** redesigning refugee services · mapping all of London's community centres · election disinformation · govtech · trade unions · work against hate and authoritarianism.

**Out:** working for a political party, or for one side winning an election.

#### Open questions carried forward

- **The name.** "Sparkle Bureaucracy" may not be the network's next-phase name; the mission is settled even if the label isn't. The rename decision follows the flagship decision.
- **The flagship.** To be chosen with faculty input against the selection criteria already in the [experiments log](#doc-experiments): clearest civic community, smallest credible version, strongest evidence path.
- **The seven founder questions** the research sprint left open ([website-selection.md](#doc-research-longlist-website-selection)) — e.g. is emerging technology part of sparkle's definition or just SB's current material? Can moral discomfort count as sparkle? Must power be transferred, or is making it legible enough?
- **The horizon.** Govtech. The window work of the next year builds the network and credibility that the horizon work will need.

---

### 6. Addressing the criteria

Sparkle Bureaucracy is not a single tool with a user base, and this submission doesn't pretend it is. The claim: **the network is the prototype, the experiments are its releases, and the diary is the evidence.** The module guidance prioritises judgement, commitment, and creativity demonstrated over time through open working practices — which is precisely what [`lore/`](#part-ii) documents, week by week, including the weeks where the project questioned itself.

#### 1. Qualifies as political technology

The lab's artifacts are civic/political technology end to end: Sparkle Border Authority (bureaucratic ritual as live software), Project Mirror and the open V1–V15 awards pipeline (participatory evaluation of 321 political-tech projects), Campaign Factory (campaign strategy research for UK civic problems), Conference Pulse (live conference participation), Topic (community scheduling/forum software). The lab is infrastructural — it serves multiple civic communities rather than one — and the [prior-art log](#doc-prior-art) already frames it for a Civic Tech Field Guide entry (librarian question queued).

#### 2. Public web presence

sparklebureaucracy.org with a live mailing list (Buttondown, since 24 May); the [site repo](https://github.com/sugaroverflow/sparkle-bureaucracy-site) with the complete open diary at `lore/`; live deployed prototypes (SBA, Campaign Factory, awards site, topic.forum); the archived original diary repo preserving full history.

#### 3. Originality

Every prototype named above is original work, built during the fellowship, in public — with collaborations credited (Campaign Lab on Campaign Factory; cohort members' contributions to the awards pipeline named individually in [the synthesis](#doc-synthesis)).

#### 4. Non-redundancy

The [prior-art log](#doc-prior-art) and the [100-case research corpus](#doc-research-longlist-readme) map the neighbours honestly — vTaiwan/pol.is (the movement-plus-tool precedent), LiquidFeedback, Creative Bureaucracy Festival, OneTeamGov, Papers Please, Project Re:form, Talk to the City, the Habermas Machine — and name the differentiator: **designed affect on top of real procedure, AI-native, aimed at the upskilling window, with under-resourced civic contexts as a stated boost.** The corpus classifies neighbours as exemplars, ancestors, infrastructure-awaiting-sparkle, and warning cases — non-redundancy demonstrated by taxonomy, not assertion.

#### 5. Active user community

The inversion at the heart of the submission: instead of one tool with users, **communities engaged through experiments**, logged in the [engagement log](#doc-engagement-log) and dated in the [timeline](#3-the-timeline--projects-and-activations-february-to-august-2026):

- **Ration Club guests** went through a live border ritual and co-created the fiction (11 March, 29 April).
- **The cohort** was inside Project Mirror, ran its own live ranking at the showcase, and played the spy reunion (30 March, 13 May) — and cohort input shaped the work (Gamithra's synthetic-agents suggestion is in the Matrix record, 29 March).
- **Clawclub** drew 10–60 people a night, February through April, plus Clawcon London.
- **Campaign Lab** co-built Campaign Factory, ran it at their conference, and subsidises free access on the house key; a campaigner reported five months of research done in a session (24 May → 16 July). The conference audience voted on the spot via Conference Pulse — 19 ballots: 16 on the useful side (9 "could be useful, with work", 7 "already useful & real"), 3 "just hype" — [feedback preserved in full](#doc-2026-07-16-campaign-factory-panel-feedback).
- **Charity evaluators**: fifteen people queued after the Evaluation 2030 keynote to map agents onto their work (15 June) — and the festival organisers wrote three times over the following two months requesting a follow-up blog, because attendees kept asking for more.
- **Inbound pull, unprompted (July–August)**: Oxfam GB's campaign engagement team requesting a Campaign Factory team demo after seeing it at the conference — a September workshop is being organised with Asil Sidahmed; the Civic Tech Field Guide inviting a show-and-tell demo and a content-creation edition; theory-of-change material offered for Campaign Factory's strategy section.
- **Endorsements from the field**: the author of *The Majority Myth* calling SB really interesting and very much needed for people to get better government services (his book launch, 20 May); Tom Fieldman of mySociety intrigued, offering experiment ideas and an intro to a practitioner using AI agents to fill out government forms (Agent Craft 2026, 12 June); Paul Silcock — a CTO building production AI agents — calling SB amazing, the prompt → system → loops framing "so valuable," and asking me to present to his team (same day); and the website travelling on its own — *"I've shared your website with family and friends today!"* (Dr Eugenie Regan, August). The CHEW keynote alone converted twice more: Octavia Field Reid (Lovelace) inviting a brown-bag on AI optimism in debate with their researchers, and Rosario Piazza — a data-and-AI policy researcher — subscribing to the list.
- **Demand signals beyond the fellowship**: a manager's "please build some for the team" on hearing about synthetic agents.
- **The cohort as ongoing infrastructure**: Code Club inherited and run biweekly from 3 February, evolving into Prototype Club and Prototype Coworking Day; Content Co-Creation Fridays co-founded with Francesca Galli under Matt Stempeck's sponsorship — recurring rituals the cohort built inside all year.

The community formed around the practice before the network had a landing page — which, per the network-formation advice in [the diary](#doc-2026-07-31-network-formation-and-mission-rethink), is how networks are born.

#### 6. No clear methodological failure

The failure-mode discipline is visible in the record: every candidate experiment carries hypothesis, method, evidence-to-collect, risks, ethics notes, and faculty questions ([experiments log](#doc-experiments), [research sprint](#doc-experiment-research-sprint)); the umbrella risk register predates this submission ([submission diary](#doc-submission-diary)); the prior-art research names the category's predictable failure — affective legitimacy without recourse, cute-washing coercion, exception theatre — and designs against it explicitly. Most importantly, the record shows the approach *adapting*: the June experiment bar ("if it doesn't change who can act… it isn't strong enough"), the July rethink documented in public, and the diary's own infrastructure run as a monitored experiment with a human approval gate. Expert critique is logged with responses — every piece of [Prototype Previews feedback](#doc-faculty-feedback) (2 April) got an action: *Radical Help* read, the sparkle passport confirmed open, Emily Mayhew's consultation question folded into the synthetic-voice cards, Hannah's pilot offer grown into the Campaign Lab partnership, Matt's experiment-lab phrase adopted as the prototype's spine. The predictable failure of this category — a vibe with no programme — is answered by the dated record: fourteen pivotal moments run, nine experiments specified to smallest-credible-run level, one flagship decision queued with faculty.

- **Pivotal moments:** [`pivotal-moments.md`](#doc-pivotal-moments) · **Reading list:** [`reading-list.md`](#doc-reading-list)

---

#### What is Sparkle Bureaucracy

Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network of people prototyping optimistic organisational futures for the age of AI.

The idea is pretty simple: what if the systems we're stuck with — the forms, the queues, the approvals, the checkpoints — didn't have to feel hostile? What if you could keep all the structure and change what it feels like to go through it? Same rituals, different intent.

I'm not trying to fix policy. I'm trying to run experiments that show what's possible and leave something behind — a pattern, a prototype, an artifact that gets hardened into something useful inside the systems it touches. The outputs are meant to illustrate, not prescribe.

The name matters: "bureaucracy" carries weight — it signals that this is serious, not just playful. "Sparkle" signals that it doesn't have to be miserable. That's the whole bet.

What makes it different from other things in this space: **no prescribed outcome**. I don't arrive with an answer. I show up with curiosity, run the experiment, publish what happened, and see what crystallises.

---

#### Why I care about this

AI reminds me of what happened when immigrants came to the US and took low-paid jobs — taxi drivers, gas stations — despite being physicists and doctors whose degrees didn't hold up in a new country. That displacement of skill and dignity is what AI is going to do to people, at scale, quickly.

I have a technical gift and I've spent most of my career using it in places where it didn't come easy for most people. I care about those communities. Not to sell them AI tools — but because they're the ones who'll feel this the most, and I think they should also be the ones who get to shape what it becomes.

I also know that having a moral high ground won't stop AI from moving forward. So the question I keep coming back to isn't "should we?" — it's "how do we make sure the people these systems are built for actually benefit from them?" Sparkle Bureaucracy is one attempt at an answer: make the systems more legible, more participatory, and more human — starting with the places where they're most broken.

---

#### What I've built so far

These are the prototypes I've already run. Each one taught me something different about what the experiment lab could look like.

**[Sparkle Border Authority](https://sparkle-border-authority.vercel.app/)** — A full border-crossing ritual built for a live party. Forms, screening, visa printing, checkpoints, admin overrides, live stats. The procedural skeleton of a border regime, wrapped in "sparkle compliance" and "diplomatic glitter." What it proved: you can preserve bureaucratic structure entirely and change only the intent, and the whole experience transforms. People played along fully. They co-created the fiction.

**[Project Mirror](https://2025.newspeak.house/awards)** — A multi-agent evaluator that inferred value constitutions from each cohort member's public record, then ran 321 project rankings and a Borda-count deliberation across 18 synthetic agents. What it proved: AI can produce surprisingly stable evaluative results when constitutions are explicit and aggregation methods are varied. It also raised genuinely unsettling questions about synthetic representation — what does it mean to have your values inferred?

**PoliTech Awards showcase** — Led the narrative and evaluation architecture for the fellowship's open awards process. V1–V15 versioned algorithms, all public on GitHub. Pulled the whole cohort in. What it proved: making evaluation methodology public doesn't make it neutral — it makes the trade-offs discussable, which is better.

**Claw agents** (Moltbook, penpals, research pipelines) — A set of playful and practical multi-agent systems built inside the Claw Club community. Whimsy and rigour held in the same hand.

**Lumina House × Ration Club** — Cross-community collaboration; social choreography at scale. Not a software project but proof that the convening instinct works outside technical contexts too.

**Community × governance writing (unpublished)** — Field notes on shadow governance, permission structures, and what it means to occupy a role that doesn't exist. The most honest thinking I've done this year.

---

#### The tensions I'm sitting with

These aren't problems to solve before I start — they're the interesting part. They're what the experiments are for.

**Playfulness vs enforcement.** Sparkle Border Authority was theatrical but it still gated access. You could still be rejected. Sparkle bureaucracy doesn't abolish enforcement — it changes what enforcement feels like. The question I haven't answered: where is the line between meaningful reframing and trivialising something that should stay serious?

**Visibility vs trust.** More transparency doesn't automatically rebuild institutional trust. Sometimes it just makes failures more visible. I published every version of the PoliTech Awards algorithm, and people's first reaction was often to find the flaw. Re-legitimisation requires redesigning the encounter, not only the disclosure.

**Synthetic representation vs authentic voice.** Project Mirror was methodologically careful — it said "evaluative stance," not "beliefs." But the discomfort was real. Asil reflected at the awards showcase on what it felt like to see an AI agent built from her public record. The inference is not consent. What does it mean to scale participation through synthetic stand-ins?

**Rigor vs accessibility.** There's a version of Sparkle Bureaucracy that becomes its own administrative burden — 15 algorithm versions, a methodology doc nobody reads, a process heavier than the thing it's trying to fix. How do you keep the ritual light enough to actually participate in?

**Open participation vs unequal permission capacity.** "No one needs permission" is a design intention, not a lived reality. Some people can act without a mandate; others can't, and it's not about confidence — it's structural. The experiment lab has to grapple with who it's actually for.

---

#### Experiments in progress

These are the systems I'm looking at next. For each one, the question is the same: can you redesign the felt experience without touching the underlying policy? What does a sparkle bureaucracy version look like, and what does it leave behind?

**DMV / queuing and classification rituals**
Government service encounters at their most frustrating. Waiting, classification, gating, identity checks. The interesting thing about the DMV isn't that it's bad — it's that everyone has been through it, everyone has feelings about it, and the procedural logic is actually quite legible if you look at it. Good candidate for a prototype that makes the ritual visible and slightly more bearable.

**Digital identity**
Verification rituals, trust documents, what it means to prove you are who you say you are — and who gets left out when those systems are designed without them in mind. Connects directly to the synthetic voice problem: if identity is already hard to verify, what happens when AI makes spoofing trivial?

**Synthetic voice in participatory channels**
Representatives get feedback from constituents. That feedback is increasingly easy to flood, spoof, or manufacture — by good actors and bad ones. The system needs to be hardened for a world where synthetic voice is cheap. The Sparkle Bureaucracy angle: we have to redesign this anyway. Let's be intentional about how.

**Liquid democracy + a hybrid election**
What does voting look like when people can delegate their vote to someone they trust, and take it back? Liquid democracy is a compelling idea that rarely gets tested in practice. The experiment: run a real election — something with actual stakes inside a community — using both paper and digital channels simultaneously. What does the ritual feel like? Where does trust live — in the paper or the screen? What does delegation look like when it's made tangible? The interesting part isn't the technology; it's what the side-by-side comparison reveals about how people relate to legitimacy, representation, and the act of choosing.

---

#### Theories of change

I'm working with a few models simultaneously. I don't think I have to pick one yet.

**The experiment lab.** Commit to running regular experiments in clearly defined spaces. For each one, state the theory of change up front: *how does doing X create result Y, and why does it matter?* Publish what happened, including what didn't work. Build evidence over time.

**The insiders.** There are people inside broken systems who want them to be better and are demotivated. Enlisting them — starting small, giving them something to hold — is its own theory of change. The experiments aren't just for outsiders looking in; they're for the person inside who's been waiting for someone to say "this could be different."

**The gem.** The prototype isn't the output. The output is what it crystallises into inside an institution — a process someone keeps using, a pattern someone names, an artifact that outlasts the experiment. The sparkles have to get hardened into gems and left behind.

**Inspiration as mechanism.** When you do the experiments with rigour and openness — no prescribed outcome — and publish them honestly, you attract the people who want to make things better. The theory of change is the constellation of people who find this and feel something.

---

#### How it gains legitimacy

Honestly, by doing the work and being honest about it. But more specifically:

- **Keynotes and demos** that show the experiments in action — not pitching, showing
- **Publishing results**, including the failures and the unresolved tensions
- **Collaborations** with people who care about the same systems (not everything has to be mine)
- **A mailing list and eventually an event series** — small, high-density, genuinely curious

##### Organisations and people I want to work with

**Public sector AI and civic tech**
- [Faculty](https://faculty.ai) — public-sector AI consultancy; potential structured collaborator for experiment design and scope
- [TPXimpact](https://www.tpximpact.com) — digital transformation for public and social impact organisations
- [MHCLG Local AI](https://mhclgdigital.blog.gov.uk/2026/03/16/introducing-local-ai/) — local government AI framing from the Ministry of Housing, Communities and Local Government

**Creative bureaucracy and civic imagination**

- [Studio Sanshin](https://www.studio-sanshin.com/) — design practice with thematic and aesthetic alignment

**Network and theory**

**Potential funders**

- Faculty and TPXimpact (above) — also potential sponsors, not only collaborators

---

#### For the prototype check-in

If any of the experiments above land for you — as a research interest, a lived experience, a project you know about, or something you'd want to work on — I'd love to hear it.

And if you have a bureaucratic system you find maddening, fascinating, or ripe for redesign, tell me that too. The experiment lab only works if the experiments are chosen well.

For structured critique, use [`faculty-feedback.md`](#doc-faculty-feedback). The prototype is **Sparkle Bureaucracy as an experiment lab**; the current decision is which first experiments the lab should run, what evidence they need, and how faculty feedback should shape the sequence.

### Research Inventory

Combined and comprehensive source of record across all field notes, chat exports, project explorations, and sensemaking work. Use `sensemaking-map.md` for the current cluster/tension map and `synthesis.md` for the strategic/mentor-facing layer built on top of this.

---

#### Part 1: Sparkle Bureaucracy — Definitions, Framing, and Network Logic

##### Definitions (multiple in use, all useful)

- **Ritual + intent definition:** Sparkle bureaucracy = bureaucracy that keeps the **rituals and structure of official systems** but makes them **playful, whimsical, or delightful instead of oppressive**. Think: forms, stamps, checkpoints, procedures — but redesigned to create joy, theatre, and shared experience. Typical traits: keeps bureaucratic rituals (forms, approvals, stamps); adds aesthetic charm (stickers, colorful documents, funny titles); turns processes into participatory theatre; uses light-hearted rules rather than strict enforcement. The structure mimics government bureaucracy, but the goal is delight and immersion, not control.

- **Organisational futures definition:** Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network of people **prototyping optimistic organisational futures for the age of AI** — reimagining how institutions feel and decide, not only claiming to improve delivery.

- **Brand positioning definition (Ed Saperia):** Talent-focused, not delivery-focused. About education, creativity, younger people. An update of "civic tech" but for AI. About using AI to explore organisational/service futures — outputs meant to **illustrate** the power of technology and what new possibilities it brings, not to be applied directly. Deliberately sidesteps strategic policy, which makes it less threatening in political environments.

- **Vehicle definition (James):** A vehicle for carrying a particular approach into places that are otherwise "unsexy," boring, or banal. Not a single product — a frame that lets a lot of different work travel together.

- **Working design principle (Fatima):** 80% credible institution, 20% Fatima-coded sparkle bureaucracy. The word "bureaucracy" signals unexpected seriousness (similar to "governance") — without it, the concept might seem too silly.

- **Related terms / adjacent vocabulary:** playful bureaucracy; ritualized administration; bureaucratic theatre; administrative cosplay. Appears in art installations, immersive events, civic design experiments, and playful governance prototypes where administrative systems are reimagined as social experiences.

##### Core transformation pattern

- Control → experience
- Compliance → engagement
- Opacity → legible ritual

##### Strategic thesis (from sensemaking integration)

Sparkle Bureaucracy works because it combines institutional seriousness with emotional accessibility. "Bureaucracy" signals credibility and system-level ambition. "Sparkle" lowers threat, invites participation, and makes experimentation socially acceptable in environments that are typically defensive. This enables practical exploration of AI-era service and organisational futures without triggering immediate policy conflict.

Trust in institutions is low. A common response is to demand more transparency, but transparency alone can intensify distrust by making institutional shortcomings more visible without changing lived experience. Sparkle Bureaucracy proposes a re-legitimisation pathway: redesign procedural encounters so they are legible, humane, participatory, and culturally resonant. The goal is not to trivialise administration, but to repurpose administrative ritual from fear/friction toward trust/connection.

##### Brand framing 

1. **Talent-focused, not delivery-focused.** This is about education, creativity, younger people — not about "effective, trustworthy, efficient, cheap."
2. **Brings together AI and the operational side of policy** — like an update of civic tech for AI. Outputs are not meant to be applied directly, but to illustrate what technology makes possible and what new possibilities it brings. Deliberately sidesteps strategic policy.
3. **The word "bureaucracy" is unusual** — unexpected seriousness, similar to "governance." Gives the brand weight without being corporate.
4. **The brand makes it unthreatening** — suggests cool ideas, fun people, a good time. Especially important in bureaucratic environments where people's spirits are usually crushed.
5. **Feminist + "for good" + AI angles make it easy to support and sponsor.** Feels fresh.
6. **Hinges on Fatima being an amazing ambassador.** Asks that are easy to say yes to: from the tech side this is sponsorship, from the civic side this is visibility. Good photos and cute branding help. Background is strong enough to stay credible.
7. **Build a portfolio of projects that encapsulate the brand.** Can take time — don't need them at launch. Shouldn't all be things Fatima made — it's also a way to make alliances with people doing cool stuff.
8. **A way to promote concrete values** — like how vTaiwan is associated with pol.is. Movement + associated tool.
9. **Important new projects don't exist yet; they'll be AI-native.** When tech is made with AI it's much cheaper, so making it "sparkly" is cheap — might as well have things be fun and beautiful as well as functional. Custom Matrix channels as an example: tech is so cheap a chat channel can be a whole custom project.

##### Additional observations

- The evaluation keynote thing can be Sparkle Bureaucracy branded.
- People are hungry for optimistic futures.
- People want to understand what post-AI jobs look like.
- One very powerful outcome: helping make post-AI team compositions palatable to public sector. This is a big part of what the service design movement did.
- Calendar strategy: put other people's events in the Sparkle Bureaucracy calendar without needing permission — it's just highlighting things you find interesting. Another way of thinking about the project: institutionalising your personal interests to give them more weight.
- Code review agents feel important — "feels of the same universe as Sparkle Bureaucracy." (Theme: AI-native governance tools.)
- Speculative future formats: Sparkle Bureaucracy Camp; Sparkle Bureaucracy Awards.
- "Something sparkle bureaucracy themed is good enough." Low-bar asks are powerful.
- "Sparkle Bureaucracy as a brand alone is really good — for some sort of AI/civic tech programme/project/articulation."
- "He's advocating for a vehicle called sparkle bureaucracy that is trying to carry forward a particular approach into places where it is otherwise'boring,' or banal, to engage with."

##### Possible sponsors and collaborators (named explicitly)

- **[Faculty](https://faculty.ai)** — public-sector AI consultancy. Explicitly suggested by Ed.
- **[TPXimpact](https://www.tpximpact.com)** — digital/public impact org. Explicitly suggested by Ed.
- **Creative Bureaucracy Festival** — [creativebureaucracy.org](https://creativebureaucracy.org/) — anchor event in the ecosystem.
- **Studio Sanshin** — [studio-sanshin.com](https://www.studio-sanshin.com/) — stylistically and thematically aligned collaborator.
- **Martin Dittus** — [martindittus.info](https://martindittus.info/) — "got it immediately"; early network signal.
- **OneTeamGov** — oneteamgov.uk — "radical reform through practical action" — adjacent positioning.
- **Google.org AI Government Innovation** — [link](https://www.google.org/impact-challenges/ai-government-innovation/) — funder-facing alignment.
- **MHCLG Local AI / digital public services** — [MHCLG blog](https://mhclgdigital.blog.gov.uk/2026/03/16/introducing-local-ai/) — policy-side ecosystem signal.
- **UKAuthority digital public services event** — [link](https://www.ukauthority.com/events/powering-digital-public-services-2026) — visibility/speaking opportunity.
- **Re-legitimisation discussion (James Plunkett / Kinship Works)** — [LinkedIn post](https://www.linkedin.com/posts/james-plunkett-a1472827_new-from-kinship-works-ending-the-institutional-activity-7440019395415957504-ut0M/) — theoretical / social backing for the trust-rebuilding angle.
- **vTaiwan / pol.is** — precedent/analogy for movement + associated tool.

##### Portfolio and operating model

- Portfolio approach: many small prototypes, not one grand solution. Not all things Fatima made — also a way to form alliances.
- "Easy yes" asks: visibility, co-hosting, lightweight sponsorship, demo participation.
- Convening as strategy: calendar, events, shared practice community.
- Network Development module: build a visible network around shared practice; use anchors and adjacencies; institutionalise personal interests into a recognisable platform with collective gravity.
- Knowledge Production module: convert prototypes into reusable knowledge assets (case notes, design patterns, failure notes); produce evidence around trust, service experience, and organisational learning; publish short, high-frequency outputs.

##### Positioning language (options in use)

1. Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network of people prototyping optimistic organisational futures for the age of AI.
2. Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network reimagining public-sector organisational futures for the AI age.
3. Sparkle Bureaucracy prototypes optimistic public-sector futures for the AI age, focused on institutional re-legitimisation.
