> **Source:** `lore/experiment-research-sprint.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Experiments

### Experiment Research Sprint

Date: 2026-06-19

Purpose: explore candidate first experiments for Sparkle Bureaucracy as an experiment lab, using parallel subagent research plus source checks.

Conclusion: keep **Sparkle Bureaucracy** as the assessed prototype, and run the first experiments as the lab's evidence-producing programme. The strongest near-term shape is: private lab infrastructure first, then one public/civic-facing flagship experiment with clear evidence, risks, and faculty critique.

---

#### Recommended Sequence

##### 1. Run the internal lab infrastructure first

Use the **Agent-Assisted Lab Clerk** and Google Doc feedback loop to keep the diary, evidence, and faculty critique moving.

This should not be the main faculty-facing experience. It is the lab's operating infrastructure: a way to turn notes, links, experiments, and feedback into structured public evidence.

Smallest test: 10 days.

Success criteria:
- one weekly diary entry drafted and human-approved
- one experiment card improved
- one prior-art list improved
- one faculty feedback prompt improved
- a visible log of what the agent got wrong, what was rejected, and what changed

##### 2. Pick one first public/civic-facing flagship

Best current candidate: **The Verification Queue**.

Reason: it is closest to Sparkle Border Authority while moving toward a real public-service surface. It keeps the core ritual pattern, `intake -> process -> decision -> artifact -> handoff`, but shifts from border theatre to classification, exception-handling, and dignity under verification.

##### 3. Keep two second-wave experiments warm

Second strongest: **Consultation Customs**, about synthetic voice in participatory channels.

Third strongest: **Hybrid Delegation Assembly**, about liquid democracy, delegation, paper, and legitimacy.

Both are strong, but each has heavier legitimacy risk. Consultation Customs risks becoming too adversarial or AI-detection-focused. Hybrid Delegation Assembly risks becoming a voting-system legitimacy problem unless carefully scoped.

---

#### Ranking

| Rank | Experiment | Best role | Why it is strong | Main risk |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | The Verification Queue | First flagship | Concrete civic/public-service ritual, strong continuity with Sparkle Border Authority, measurable evidence, clear dignity/exception-handling question | Could become service-design theatre without practitioners or affected users |
| 2 | Consultation Customs | Second flagship or parallel shadow test | Extremely timely AI-era participation problem, strong political technology fit, reusable protocol output | Can become bot detection, surveillance, or anti-AI culture war |
| 3 | Hybrid Delegation Assembly | Community legitimacy event | Easy to run in a bounded community, strong ritual artifacts, concrete participation data | Delegation, secrecy, clique power, and vote legitimacy are hard to handle ethically |
| 4 | Agent-Assisted Lab Clerk | Internal infrastructure | Directly improves diary, evidence, prior art, and feedback loops | Too meta if treated as the main civic-facing experiment |

---

#### Experiment 1: The Verification Queue

##### Core question

Can a public-service verification ritual become more legible, humane, and contestable without hiding the fact that classification and exclusion are still happening?

##### Why this fits Sparkle Bureaucracy

This is the closest successor to Sparkle Border Authority. It keeps the procedural skeleton, but tests whether the pattern transfers from a party border ritual into a public-service scenario: queueing, eligibility, identity checks, document evidence, edge cases, decisions, receipts, and appeals.

##### Smallest credible experiment

Run a 90-minute live simulation with 12-20 participants and 2-4 observers or practitioners.

Scenario: participants need to complete a fictional but plausible public-service task, such as renewing a licence, claiming an event access credential, proving eligibility for a concession, or updating a civic record.

Run two versions:

1. **Baseline ritual**
   - opaque queue number
   - ambiguous eligibility categories
   - pass/fail document check
   - generic rejection message
   - unclear appeal route

2. **Sparkle Bureaucracy ritual**
   - visible queue state
   - clear classification tokens
   - "why am I in this queue?" explanations
   - transparent verification steps
   - explicit exception route
   - decision receipt
   - humane handoff

Use only synthetic personas and fake documents.

Include edge cases:
- name mismatch
- no photo ID
- expired document
- low digital access
- accessibility need
- synthetic or deepfake concern
- legitimate user who fails standard checks

##### Output artifact

A **decision receipt** showing:
- classification
- evidence used
- reason
- next step
- appeal or exception route

##### Evidence to collect

- before/after comprehension: what happened, why, and what can you do next
- perceived fairness, dignity, trust, and discomfort
- time spent in waiting, intake, verification, exception, and decision states
- false accept and false reject rates using synthetic cases
- whether participants can explain the classification categories afterward
- whether the exception route feels real or decorative
- practitioner critique: plausible, naive, risky, useful
- accessibility notes: phone, camera, language, document, and visual dependency
- anonymised quotes about humiliation, relief, confusion, agency, and legitimacy
- service map, queue-state diagram, classification cards, and decision receipt template

##### Failure modes

- Cute-washing coercion: the ritual feels nicer but hides an unjust rule.
- Simulation weakness: participants know it is fake, so emotional stakes are lower.
- Practitioner absence: without public-service designers or frontline staff, operational constraints may be missed.
- Identity harm: do not collect real IDs, biometrics, immigration status, financial information, or benefits details.
- Accessibility harm: do not make digital identity mean smartphone plus good camera plus stable documents.
- Exception theatre: an appeal route that cannot change outcomes may be worse than no appeal route.

##### Prior art to investigate

- GOV.UK Service Standard: https://www.gov.uk/service-manual/service-standard
- GOV.UK One Login: https://www.gov.uk/using-your-gov-uk-one-login
- GOV.UK identity proofing guidance: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/identity-proofing-and-verification-of-an-individual/how-to-prove-and-verify-someones-identity
- NIST SP 800-63-4 Digital Identity Guidelines: https://pages.nist.gov/800-63-4/
- W3C Verifiable Credentials Data Model v2.0: https://www.w3.org/TR/vc-data-model-2.0/

##### Faculty questions

- Is this strong enough as Sparkle Bureaucracy's first flagship civic/public-service experiment?
- Is the core research question about queues, classification, identity verification, exception handling, or dignity?
- Does combining queueing with digital identity make the experiment richer, or should they be split?
- What evidence would convince you that this is more than theatrical service design?
- What minimum practitioner involvement is needed for credibility?

---

#### Experiment 2: Consultation Customs

##### Core question

How should public consultations, constituent inboxes, petitions, or planning comments handle participation when submissions may be duplicated, AI-assisted, paid, impersonated, or synthetic?

##### Why this fits Sparkle Bureaucracy

This is a bureaucratic ritual failure: "the public has spoken" becomes hard to interpret when volume, authenticity, affectedness, and substance come apart.

The goal should not be AI detection. The goal should be a participation ritual that helps reviewers distinguish **volume**, **substance**, **affectedness**, and **uncertainty** without punishing legitimate AI-assisted participation.

##### Smallest credible experiment

Run a bounded shadow consultation inside Newspeak House or another trusted civic community.

Setup:
- choose a real but low-risk decision, such as event policy, space use, experiment prioritisation, community norm, or small budget allocation
- publish a one-page consultation brief with 3 options and clear questions
- recruit 15-30 real participants
- allow AI-assisted submissions, but ask people to disclose whether AI helped draft, edit, translate, or structure their response
- add a labelled adversarial test set: duplicate form responses, AI-generated persona responses, synthetic but plausible objections, and AI-assisted genuine responses
- ask 3-5 reviewers to process the dataset twice: once as a normal inbox, once through the Sparkle Bureaucracy protocol

##### Output artifact

A **Public Voice Ledger** or **Consultation Customs protocol** with:
- original submission preserved
- claimed relationship to issue
- AI assistance disclosure
- duplicate / campaign / unique marker
- evidence or lived-experience marker
- confidence note, not authenticity verdict
- "do not penalise accessibility assistance" rule
- final response memo showing which themes changed the decision

##### Evidence to collect

- number and type of submissions
- triage time: baseline inbox vs protocol
- reviewer confidence before and after protocol
- false positives: genuine or accessibility-assisted comments treated as suspicious
- false negatives: synthetic or fabricated comments treated as authentic
- number of distinct issues surfaced
- whether minority or unusual views survive summarisation
- participant trust rating
- reviewer usefulness rating
- final decision trace: which comments or themes affected the outcome
- consultation brief, submission schema, anonymised ledger, reviewer notes, decision memo

##### Failure modes

- The synthetic test set may be too obvious or too artificial.
- Reviewers may over-focus on detecting AI rather than extracting civic substance.
- The protocol may reward polished writing and punish messy but authentic comments.
- AI-assisted accessibility, translation, or neurodivergent support may be misclassified as manipulation.
- Identity verification could slide into surveillance or exclusion.
- Publishing adversarial prompts could teach people how to flood consultations.

Design constraint: do not make human-written text the gold standard. Make the standard accountable, situated, consentful, interpretable participation.

##### Prior art to investigate

- New York Attorney General fake comments report: https://ag.ny.gov/sites/default/files/oag-fakecommentsreport.pdf
- Regulations.gov API: https://open.gsa.gov/api/regulationsgov/
- GOV.UK Consultation Principles: https://www.gov.uk/government/publications/consultation-principles-guidance
- vTaiwan: https://info.vtaiwan.tw/
- Polis: https://compdemocracy.org/polis/
- Decidim: https://decidim.org/
- C2PA / Content Credentials: https://c2pa.org/

##### Faculty questions

- Is this strong enough as the first public/civic-facing Sparkle Bureaucracy experiment?
- Should the experiment optimise for consultation officials, elected representatives, civic organisations, or affected residents?
- Is a bounded shadow consultation credible enough, or does it need a real institutional partner?
- What should count more: verified identity, affectedness, originality, evidence, or deliberative value?
- How do we avoid excluding people who use AI for accessibility, translation, confidence, or structure?

---

#### Experiment 3: Hybrid Delegation Assembly

##### Core question

How do paper and digital rituals affect perceived legitimacy, participation, and trust in a delegated community decision?

##### Why this fits Sparkle Bureaucracy

It turns voting mechanics into visible ritual: delegation, revocation, paper ballots, seals, witnessed counting, audit notes, and dispute handling.

It is strong if treated as a bounded community legitimacy experiment. It is weak if framed as solving voting.

##### Smallest credible experiment

Run a Hybrid Delegation Assembly with 15-40 people in a bounded community.

Decision options:
- which Sparkle Bureaucracy experiment should run first
- how to allocate a small event budget
- which collective question the group should investigate next

Protocol:
1. Publish 4-6 proposals in advance.
2. Participants can vote directly or delegate their vote to another participant.
3. Delegations are digital before the session and revocable.
4. At the session, each participant receives a paper ballot.
5. Paper ballot can confirm direct vote, override delegation, or abstain.
6. Delegates cast visible bundle votes only for unresolved delegations.
7. Public tally happens twice: quick spreadsheet tally, then paper count.
8. Publish an audit note.

##### Output artifact

A **delegation audit note** with:
- turnout
- direct votes vs delegations
- largest delegate weight
- revocations and overrides
- tally mismatches
- disputes
- participant trust scores

##### Evidence to collect

- turnout and completion rate
- direct votes vs delegated votes
- delegation concentration
- unused or revoked delegations
- paper overrides of digital delegation
- digital tally vs paper tally errors
- time required to explain the system
- participant trust before and after the ritual
- participant comprehension: can they explain how their vote counted?
- perceived legitimacy: would they accept the result if they disliked the outcome?
- qualitative reactions to paper, witnessing, signatures, seals, and visible counting

##### Failure modes

- Small sample cannot prove liquid democracy works.
- Low-stakes decision may inflate trust.
- Delegation can create social pressure or clique power.
- Public delegation graphs may reveal social or political affinities.
- Weighted delegate votes can weaken secrecy.
- Paper rituals can launder legitimacy if the underlying process is bad.

##### Prior art to investigate

- LiquidFeedback: https://liquidfeedback.org/
- Decidim: https://decidim.org/
- Polis: https://compdemocracy.org/polis/
- vTaiwan: https://info.vtaiwan.tw/
- Helios Voting: https://heliosvoting.org/
- Voting behaviour and power in LiquidFeedback: https://arxiv.org/abs/1503.07723
- Liquid democracy experiments on delegation: https://arxiv.org/abs/2212.09715

##### Faculty questions

- Is the strongest research question trust, participation, delegation power, or bureaucratic ritual?
- What is the safest real decision to use without making the experiment trivial?
- Should the first version include delegation, or start with paper-vs-digital voting only?
- Does the paper ritual create meaningful accountability, or just aesthetic confidence?
- How should secrecy work when delegation itself may need visibility?

---

#### Experiment 4: Agent-Assisted Lab Clerk

##### Core question

Can agent-assisted lab bureaucracy make Sparkle Bureaucracy more legible, accountable, and feedback-responsive without flattening the research voice or laundering uncertainty?

##### Why this fits Sparkle Bureaucracy

The lab itself needs bureaucracy: intake, classification, evidence, decision logs, handoff, publication. The agent-assisted diary tests whether that bureaucracy can be useful, humane, and auditable.

This should be internal infrastructure, not the primary faculty feedback interface.

##### Smallest credible experiment

Run a 10-day pilot.

Inputs:
- Matrix notes
- meeting notes
- 5-10 links
- one weekly reflection
- one faculty comment thread
- current repo docs

Agent tasks:
- classify notes into diary, prior art, experiment, feedback, and evidence
- draft one weekly entry
- propose one experiment-card update
- produce a collaborator/prior-art shortlist

Human tasks:
- approve, reject, or edit every output
- log why
- keep Google Doc as the faculty surface

Comparator: manually produce one equivalent weekly update from the same source material before or after the agent-assisted pass.

##### Evidence to collect

- time spent manually vs with agent assistance
- number of raw notes captured, classified, discarded, and published
- traceability rate: public claims linked back to a note, source, conversation, or decision
- agent error log: hallucinated sources, bad classifications, overconfident summaries, missed nuance
- human edit distance: what had to be rewritten and why
- faculty feedback quality: comments that become concrete changes
- prior-art yield: sources found, sources rejected, and why
- collaborator yield: people or organisations suggested, contacted, responded, useful
- privacy/redaction log
- before/after diary readability

##### Failure modes

- The agent optimises for legibility rather than truth.
- The agent makes the project look more coherent than it was.
- Sensitive or relational material gets laundered into public evidence.
- Collaborator scouting becomes extractive.
- The agent becomes a public-facing gimmick and alienates AI-sceptical faculty.

Controls:
- no autonomous publishing
- no autonomous outreach
- no ranking people by inferred usefulness without human review
- redact private conversations by default
- keep rejected agent outputs as evidence of limits
- separate raw notes, interpreted evidence, and public diary
- mark AI-assisted sections when relevant
- keep faculty interface non-AI unless explicitly invited

##### Prior art to investigate

- Nielsen Norman Group diary studies: https://www.nngroup.com/articles/diary-studies/
- DiaryHelper paper: https://arxiv.org/abs/2404.19738
- Elicit: https://elicit.com/
- ResearchRabbit: https://www.researchrabbit.ai/
- Google NotebookLM: https://notebooklm.google/
- GitHub Issues: https://docs.github.com/en/issues/tracking-your-work-with-issues/about-issues
- GitHub Discussions: https://docs.github.com/en/discussions
- Civic Tech Field Guide: https://civictech.guide/

##### Faculty questions

- Does this infrastructure count as part of Sparkle Bureaucracy, or only as support?
- What evidence would convince you that it improves research quality rather than polish?
- Which parts of the diary should remain human-authored?
- What should be explicitly out of scope for the agent?
- Which first public-facing experiment should this infrastructure support?

---

#### Cross-Cutting Lessons

##### The best first flagship is not necessarily the most AI-native

The Verification Queue may be strongest because it is legible, concrete, and directly connected to Sparkle Border Authority. Its AI relevance can remain contextual: AI increases pressure on identity, classification, spoofing, and automation, but the first workshop does not need to include a live AI classifier.

##### Synthetic voice is the most timely public problem

Consultation Customs is probably the strongest AI-era civic-tech experiment. It has a clear public problem and sharp prior art, but it needs careful framing to avoid becoming an AI-detection or identity-verification project.

##### Liquid democracy is a good event, but a risky first flagship

Hybrid Delegation Assembly would be memorable and easy to stage. It also produces clean metrics. But voting legitimacy, delegation power, secrecy, and clique dynamics can overwhelm the core research question unless the decision is carefully bounded.

##### The agent diary should be internal infrastructure

Given faculty AI-scepticism, the agent should help maintain the lab, not mediate faculty feedback. Google Docs should remain the primary faculty surface.

---

#### Proposed Faculty Ask

Use this framing in the Google Doc:

> Sparkle Bureaucracy is the prototype: an experiment lab. I am choosing the lab's first formal experiment sequence. The strongest current options are The Verification Queue, Consultation Customs, and Hybrid Delegation Assembly. The Agent-Assisted Lab Clerk is proposed as internal research infrastructure. I would like feedback on which public/civic-facing experiment should run first, what evidence would make it credible, and what risks I am underestimating.

Ask faculty to rank:

1. Which should run first?
2. Which has the clearest political technology fit?
3. Which has the clearest civic community?
4. Which has the strongest evidence path?
5. Which has the largest ethical risk?
6. Which prior art is missing?

<a id="doc-prospective-experiments"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/prospective-experiments.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Experiments

### Prospective Sparkle Bureaucracy Experiments

This is the working idea bank for future Sparkle Bureaucracy experiments.

The earlier list was useful, but too many candidates read like generic civic workshops. This version uses the project source material more strictly: Sparkle Border Authority, Project Mirror, the PoliTech Awards process, Claw agents, Newspeak governance field notes, and the 80% credible institution / 20% sparkle principle.

#### What Counts

A Sparkle Bureaucracy experiment should have all four parts:

1. **Institutional skeleton:** a recognisable bureaucratic ritual, such as a queue, permit, customs desk, warrant, court, audit, ledger, passport, hearing, register, or receipt.
2. **Affective re-skin:** the procedure feels participatory, playful, legible, or ceremonial without pretending power has disappeared.
3. **Decision pressure:** something is accepted, rejected, classified, authorised, appealed, represented, delegated, scored, or made contestable.
4. **Durable artifact:** the experiment leaves behind a receipt, ledger, passport, warrant, field kit, constitution, register, dashboard, or protocol that could harden into a reusable pattern.

If an idea does not change who can act, how judgment is made, what becomes visible, or how a decision can be contested, it is probably not strong enough yet.

#### Selection Criteria

- Does it preserve a serious procedural skeleton?
- Does the sparkle layer change participation, not only aesthetics?
- What artifact survives the event?
- What evidence would show that the artifact mattered?
- What is the coercive or exclusionary part of the ritual, and is it still visible?
- Could this become Sparkle Bureaucracy's `pol.is`-like canonical tool or pattern?
- Could faculty critique the method, ethics, and political technology claim?
- Can it be run with fictional or synthetic cases before touching real people or sensitive data?

#### Shortlist

| Rank | Experiment | Format | Canonical potential | Why it matters |
| ---: | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| 1 | Public Voice Customs | Mechanism + live consultation | Very high | A synthetic-voice-era consultation ritual. |
| 2 | Mirror Commons | AI mechanism + calibration game | Very high | Consentful synthetic representation for collective judgment. |
| 3 | The Exception Window | Live experience + procedural mechanism | High | The strongest successor to Sparkle Border Authority for public services. |
| 4 | Permission Passport Office | Governance mechanism + role-played office | Medium-high | Newspeak-native test of unequal permission capacity. |
| 5 | The Value Court | Game + evaluation mechanism | Medium-high | Turns PoliTech Awards methodology into a reusable evaluation ritual. |
| 6 | Claw Civil Service | Agent mechanism + lab operating system | High inside the lab | AI agents as warranted public servants, not vibes. |
| 7 | Shadow Labour Claims Office | Mechanism + claims ceremony | Medium | Makes shadow governance claimable and redistributable. |
| 8 | Liquid Mandate Assembly | Governance experience + audit mechanism | Medium | Tangible delegation, revocation, and legitimacy under visible power concentration. |
| 9 | Sparkle Border Field Kit | Deployable experience kit | High as origin pattern | Turns Sparkle Border Authority into a portable grammar. |

---

#### 1. Public Voice Customs

**Working question:** How should public consultations handle civic voice when submissions may be human, AI-assisted, duplicated, synthetic, coordinated, affected, performative, or genuinely urgent?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Public consultation, petitions, comment inboxes, casework triage, submission review.

**Sparkle move:** Re-skin "the public has spoken" as a customs process for civic voice. Every submission passes through counters that stamp different properties separately: volume, affectedness, duplication, AI assistance, uncertainty, evidence, and civic substance.

This must not become bot detection. The point is not to decide whether a comment is "real." The point is to preserve participation while making uncertainty legible.

**Format:** Mechanism plus live consultation experience.

**Smallest credible run:**

- Pick one low-risk Newspeak or community decision.
- Collect 20-40 submissions.
- Add labelled test cases: duplicates, AI-assisted genuine responses, synthetic objections, campaign text, translated or accessibility-assisted comments.
- Have reviewers process the set once as a normal inbox and once through Public Voice Customs.
- Publish a decision trace memo showing what changed.

**Artifacts left behind:** Public Voice Ledger, submission passports, uncertainty stamps, decision trace memo, "do not penalise accessibility assistance" rule.

**Evidence:** Reviewer confidence, false positives and negatives, minority-view preservation, whether the final decision cites themes that came through the process.

**Risks:** Surveillance creep, authenticity policing, punishing AI-assisted access needs, giving officials a language for discounting inconvenient participation.

**Canonical potential:** Very high. This could become "pol.is for synthetic-voice-era consultations" if the ledger is strong enough.

---

#### 2. Mirror Commons

**Working question:** What does consentful synthetic representation look like when people manage their own evaluator agents?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Jury selection, conflict-of-interest declarations, representative mandates, value constitutions, deliberation records.

**Sparkle move:** Turn Project Mirror's inferred constitutions into editable evaluator passports. An agent may only speak, rank, summarise, or deliberate inside the warrant a person has declared.

This is not "build a better clone." It is a consular process for deciding what a synthetic stand-in is allowed to do.

**Format:** AI mechanism plus constitutional calibration game.

**Smallest credible run:**

- Recruit 8 people.
- Each person creates or reviews an evaluator passport: criteria, modifiers, values, red lines, forbidden claims, expiry.
- Agents or manually simulated stand-ins rank 20 proposals.
- Participants audit where the stand-in misrepresented them.
- Run one aggregation comparison: Borda, pairwise, or average.

**Artifacts left behind:** Evaluator passports, modifier ledgers, dissent notes, agent-behaviour diff reports, aggregation receipts, revocation form.

**Evidence:** Human-agent agreement and disagreement, correction frequency, what participants refuse to delegate, comfort and discomfort notes, ranking stability after corrections.

**Risks:** False authority, flattened values, consent ambiguity, treating public records as permission, making synthetic judgment seem more precise than it is.

**Canonical potential:** Very high if Sparkle Bureaucracy becomes about managed synthetic selves for collective judgment.

---

#### 3. The Exception Window

**Working question:** Can verification, eligibility, and queueing systems make rejection contestable without hiding exclusion or increasing surveillance?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Queues, eligibility checks, identity verification, denial notices, appeals, exception handling.

**Sparkle move:** The exception route becomes the main object. Classification, rejection, override, and appeal are visible, named, receipted, and testable.

This is not a nicer DMV. It is a public service simulation where the question is whether the rejected person understands what happened and has a real route to challenge it.

**Format:** Live experience plus procedural mechanism.

**Smallest credible run:**

- Use 12 synthetic cases.
- Run a baseline queue, then a Sparkle queue.
- Include edge cases: name mismatch, no photo ID, expired documents, low digital access, accessibility need, synthetic media suspicion.
- Roles: applicant, queue marshal, verifier, exception officer, receipt clerk, observer.

**Artifacts left behind:** Queue tokens, classification cards, decision receipts, appeal stamps, exception ledger, service map.

**Evidence:** Participant comprehension, false reject and false accept handling, perceived dignity, whether the appeal route feels real, whether repeated exceptions reveal a broken rule, practitioner critique.

**Risks:** Cute-washing exclusion, decorative appeals, identity and accessibility harms, making a bad rule easier to endure instead of easier to contest.

**Canonical potential:** High as a portable pattern: decision receipt plus exception ledger.

---

#### 4. Permission Passport Office

**Working question:** How can a rule-light community make permission to act explicit without reproducing hierarchy?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Mandate seeking, role assignment, authorisation, sponsor letters, expiry, revocation.

**Sparkle move:** Make Newspeak's shadow permission structure visible through temporary warrants, stamps, sponsors, expiry dates, scope limits, and revocation slips.

The point is not to help people "feel confident." The point is to reveal unequal permission capacity: who can act without asking, who needs a document, and who silently carries legitimacy work.

**Format:** Governance mechanism plus role-played office.

**Smallest credible run:**

- 6-10 participants bring 3 blocked actions.
- The office issues, denies, narrows, or amends mandates.
- Every mandate names what it unlocks: room, budget, mailing list, calendar slot, role, or decision right.
- Review after one week.

**Artifacts left behind:** Permission passports, mandate registry, role charters, revocation slips, refusal reasons.

**Evidence:** Actions unblocked, contestations raised, who felt more authorised, whether mandates survived the week, whether the process reduced invisible negotiation or added another gate.

**Risks:** Formalising hierarchy, permission theatre, strengthening already-powerful actors, turning structural permission inequality into a personal confidence problem.

**Canonical potential:** Medium-high. This could become the permission scaffold pattern.

---

#### 5. The Value Court

**Working question:** Can a group make evaluative values explicit before using them to judge people, projects, grants, tenders, or proposals?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Awards judging, grant panels, procurement scoring, conflict declarations, appeals, method changes.

**Sparkle move:** Turn the PoliTech Awards process into public theatre: every scoring system is a constitution, every version change is a political act, every winner gets a stability receipt.

The experiment does not pretend to make evaluation neutral. It makes the trade-offs discussable.

**Format:** Game plus evaluation mechanism.

**Smallest credible run:**

- 8 jurors.
- 12 projects or proposals.
- 2-3 scoring constitutions.
- Compare Borda, pairwise, and average aggregation.
- Hold one objection round and one method-change round.

**Artifacts left behind:** Value constitutions, score passports, method-change receipts, winner-stability report, dissent appendix.

**Evidence:** Ranking volatility across methods, objections and amendments, participant understanding of value trade-offs, whether people accept an unwanted winner because the method is legible.

**Risks:** Method theatre, reputational harm if real people or projects are judged, synthetic judgment seeming more authoritative than it is, too much process weight for a small decision.

**Canonical potential:** Medium-high as an evaluation constitution kit.

---

#### 6. Claw Civil Service

**Working question:** What happens when AI agents are treated as tiny civil servants with warrants, duties, refusal rules, audit logs, and decommission notices?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Civil-service staffing, job descriptions, task dockets, warrants, supervision, audit logs, error reports.

**Sparkle move:** Claw-style agents become named officeholders, not magic helpers. Each agent has a job card, jurisdiction, source duties, refusal conditions, escalation route, and retirement procedure.

This is the lab's AI-native organisational futures experiment.

**Format:** Agent mechanism plus lab operating system.

**Smallest credible run:**

- Run 3 agents for 10 days:
  - Lab Clerk: classifies notes and drafts weekly diary updates.
  - Prior-Art Scout: finds adjacent work and flags confidence.
  - Skeptical Reviewer: challenges overclaims, ethics, and generic ideas.
- Every output is human-approved before publication.
- Keep rejected outputs.

**Artifacts left behind:** Agent warrant cards, task docket, source audit, error log, rejected-output file, decommission notice.

**Evidence:** Time saved, errors caught, outputs accepted, edited, or rejected, whether Fatima's voice is preserved, whether the warrant prevented scope creep.

**Risks:** Too meta for faculty, privacy leakage, anthropomorphic authority, flattening field notes or making the diary feel generated.

**Canonical potential:** High inside the lab. Lower as the public-facing canonical tool unless paired with Mirror Commons.

---

#### 7. Shadow Labour Claims Office

**Working question:** How can invisible governance labour become visible, claimable, and redistributable without punishing the people who made it visible?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Timesheets, expense claims, minutes, service credit, role registers, rota handoff.

**Sparkle move:** Translation, lobbying, emotional buffering, context carrying, access brokering, and legitimacy repair become claimable receipts. Shadow governance becomes inspectable without pretending it is neutral.

**Format:** Mechanism plus claims ceremony.

**Smallest credible run:**

- Use fictional cases first.
- Later, after one event or project week, 8-12 trusted participants file shadow-labour receipts.
- The group decides what becomes a named role, rotation, budget line, refusal, or stopped task.
- Review after two weeks.

**Artifacts left behind:** Shadow-labour receipts, invisible org chart, role-debt ledger, denied-claim reasons, transfer docket.

**Evidence:** Hidden work surfaced, redistribution decisions, role clarity, follow-through after two weeks, whether the people doing the work feel more or less burdened.

**Risks:** Making care transactional, social exposure, burdening already-burdened people, turning vulnerability into performance.

**Canonical potential:** Medium. More pattern than platform, but very specific to the governance field notes.

---

#### 8. Liquid Mandate Assembly

**Working question:** How do paper, digital delegation, revocation, and visible mandate concentration affect legitimacy in a bounded community decision?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Voting, delegation, revocation, ballot counting, audit notes, mandate registers.

**Sparkle move:** Delegation becomes tangible: mandate cards, revocation stamps, witnessed counts, paper overrides, concentration charts.

The point is not to solve voting. The point is to make legitimacy under visible power concentration observable.

**Format:** Governance experience plus audit mechanism.

**Smallest credible run:**

- 20-40 people decide a bounded real question: first experiment, small event budget, event priority, or community norm.
- Participants can vote directly or delegate.
- Paper ballot can confirm or override digital delegation.
- Publish an audit note.

**Artifacts left behind:** Delegation passports, sealed paper ballots, override receipts, concentration chart, audit note, dispute log.

**Evidence:** Direct vs delegated votes, revocations, tally mismatches, participant comprehension, willingness to accept a disliked outcome, discomfort around visible power concentration.

**Risks:** Clique power, secrecy leaks, social pressure, inflated legitimacy.

**Canonical potential:** Medium. Memorable, but riskier than Public Voice Customs or Mirror Commons.

---

#### 9. Sparkle Border Field Kit

**Working question:** Can Sparkle Border Authority become a portable grammar for transforming access control, credentialing, and event entry into participatory ritual?

**Bureaucratic ritual:** Border crossing, access control, event check-in, credentialing, permit issuance, checkpoint override.

**Sparkle move:** Package the original Sparkle Border Authority pattern as a reusable field kit: intake, screening, decision, artifact, checkpoint, override.

This is the origin proof. The question is whether it travels.

**Format:** Deployable experience kit.

**Smallest credible run:**

- One event with 30+ entrants.
- Two checkpoint staff.
- Live override handling.
- Printed credentials.
- Dashboard screenshots.

**Artifacts left behind:** Visa stickers, checkpoint ledger, override report, rejection narratives, dashboard screenshots, host setup guide.

**Evidence:** Participation rate, override demand, emotional response to gating, artifact retention, accessibility issues, co-created lore.

**Risks:** Trivialising border violence, accessibility failures, exclusion through play, becoming the only thing people think Sparkle Bureaucracy is.

**Canonical potential:** High as brand origin and demo. Lower as the wider civic canonical tool unless translated into less border-specific contexts.

---

#### Current Recommendation

There are two credible routes.

##### Route A: Canonical Tool Route

If Sparkle Bureaucracy needs its `pol.is`-like anchor, prioritise:

1. Public Voice Customs.
2. Mirror Commons.
3. The Exception Window.

These have the strongest chance of becoming portable tools or protocols.

##### Route B: Fellowship Evidence Route

If the goal is a strong, feasible fellowship prototype in the next few weeks, prioritise:

1. The Exception Window.
2. Claw Civil Service.
3. Permission Passport Office.

These best match existing assets, can be run with synthetic cases, and can produce assessable evidence quickly.

##### Suggested First Run

Run **The Exception Window** as the first public-facing experiment:

- It inherits the Sparkle Border Authority grammar.
- It keeps enforcement visible.
- It produces durable artifacts: decision receipts and exception ledgers.
- It can be tested with fake cases.
- It lets faculty critique the ethics of making exclusion feel humane.

Run **Claw Civil Service** quietly in parallel as lab infrastructure:

- It keeps the diary moving.
- It tests AI-native organisational futures.
- It produces an error log and warrant system.
- It should not be the only public-facing prototype.

Keep **Public Voice Customs** and **Mirror Commons** as the bigger canonical candidates for the next phase.

> **Source:** `lore/faculty-feedback.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Feedback & engagement

### Faculty Feedback Tracker

This is the repo-side tracker for faculty critique. The commentable faculty surface should be a Google Doc generated from [faculty-feedback-brief.md](#doc-faculty-feedback-brief); this file records what was asked, what came back, and how the project changed.

Use this file before and after faculty conversations:

1. State the decision.
2. Name the options.
3. Say what I currently believe.
4. Say what would change my mind.
5. Record the feedback.
6. Record what changed.

---

#### Current Decision Needing Feedback

##### Decision

Sparkle Bureaucracy is the assessed prototype: an experiment lab. Which first experiments should the lab run, in what order, and what evidence would make the lab credible against the fellowship criteria?

##### Options

| Option | Role in the experiment lab | Why it might be strong | Main concern |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Agent-assisted prototype diary | Lab research infrastructure | Directly supports diary requirements and shows AI-native knowledge production | May be seen as meta unless connected to public learning and community validation |
| Faculty feedback loop | Lab governance / critique process | Produces clear evidence of expert feedback changing the lab's experiment sequence | Needs a Google Doc surface and a disciplined response log |
| Collaborator and prior-art scout | Lab network development | Fits the Sparkle Bureaucracy network thesis | Needs careful evidence and avoids superficial outreach |
| Synthetic voice in participatory channels | Civic-facing experiment | Strong political technology fit around AI-era public participation | More complex, higher ethics and scope risk |
| Hybrid liquid democracy election | Civic-facing experiment | Concrete community test with real engagement evidence | Requires a willing community, dates, and legitimacy guardrails |
| DMV / queue ritual | Civic/public-service-facing experiment | Easy to understand and explain | Needs access to real users or practitioners to avoid pure theatre |
| Digital identity / verification ritual | Civic/public-service-facing experiment | Strong AI governance connection | High sensitivity and exclusion risk |

##### Current Belief

The strongest near-term path is:

1. Define Sparkle Bureaucracy as the experiment lab prototype.
2. Use the agent-assisted diary and faculty feedback loop as the lab's operating infrastructure.
3. Use faculty feedback to choose and harden the first public/civic-facing experiment sequence.
4. Turn each candidate experiment into a comparable card before booking dates.

##### What Would Change My Mind

- Faculty say an experiment lab is too broad unless one public/civic-facing experiment is clearly primary.
- A bounded community offers a concrete date for a liquid democracy or synthetic voice test.
- Prior art shows that one candidate is obsolete or predictably doomed.
- A mentor identifies a sharper civic community and user need for one option.

---

#### Google Doc Feedback Surface

Primary faculty feedback should happen in a Google Doc, because faculty can comment inline and respond without navigating the repo. Use [faculty-feedback-brief.md](#doc-faculty-feedback-brief) as the source text for that document.

After each round of comments:

1. Add a row to the feedback log below.
2. Update the relevant experiment card in [experiments.md](#doc-experiments).
3. Add any new evidence requirement to [submission-diary.md](#doc-submission-diary) or [engagement-log.md](#doc-engagement-log).
4. Record unresolved questions here.

<a id="doc-faculty-feedback-brief"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/faculty-feedback-brief.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Feedback & engagement

### Sparkle Bureaucracy - Faculty Feedback Brief

This is the Google-Doc-friendly feedback surface for faculty and mentors.

The prototype is **Sparkle Bureaucracy**: an experiment lab for prototyping optimistic organisational futures for the age of AI. The open question is not "what is the prototype?" The open question is which first experiments the lab should run, what evidence those experiments need, and how to make the lab credible against the fellowship criteria.

---

#### 1. What I Am Building

Sparkle Bureaucracy is an experiment lab for testing whether bureaucratic rituals can be redesigned to be more legible, participatory, and humane without pretending the underlying power dynamics disappear.

It starts from a pattern I have already built and observed:

> intake -> process -> decision -> artifact -> handoff

That pattern appears in borders, evaluations, voting, public consultation, queues, permissions, digital identity, and AI-mediated governance. The lab runs small experiments in these systems, publishes what happened, and turns the results into reusable patterns, artifacts, and evidence.

---

#### 2. Why This Is Political Technology

The project sits at the intersection of:

- Civic technology and public-sector AI.
- Participatory democracy and collective judgment.
- Bureaucratic experience design.
- Synthetic voice, identity, and legitimacy in AI-mediated systems.
- Research infrastructure for public learning.

The goal is not to produce policy advice. The goal is to run concrete experiments that make institutional procedures more inspectable, discussable, and redesignable.

---

#### 3. What Already Exists

- **Sparkle Border Authority:** a full border-crossing ritual for a live event, including forms, screening, visa printing, checkpoints, admin overrides, and live stats.
- **Project Mirror:** a multi-agent evaluator that inferred value constitutions from cohort members' public records, then ran project rankings and social-choice aggregation.
- **PoliTech Awards process:** public, versioned evaluation of political technology projects.
- **Field notes and research inventory:** ongoing notes on legitimacy, permission, synthetic representation, public-sector AI, and civic infrastructure.

---

#### 4. Current Lab Components

- Public narrative: `README.md`
- Template-shaped diary: `submission-diary.md`
- Experiment log: `experiments.md`
- Faculty feedback tracker: `faculty-feedback.md`
- Prior art log: `prior-art.md`
- Engagement log: `engagement-log.md`
- Technical notes: `technical-notes.md`
- Weekly entries: `weekly/`

---

#### 5. Candidate First Experiments

##### A. Agent-Assisted Prototype Diary

Role: lab research infrastructure.

Question: Can an agent help turn scattered field notes, links, conversations, and feedback into structured weekly diary drafts, experiment cards, prior-art candidates, and collaborator suggestions?

Risk: may be seen as too meta unless connected to public learning and community validation.

##### B. Faculty Feedback Loop

Role: lab governance / critique process.

Question: Does structured feedback produce more actionable faculty critique than asking people to review the whole project at once?

Risk: may be too small unless paired with a public/civic-facing experiment.

##### C. Collaborator and Prior-Art Scout

Role: lab network development.

Question: Can an agent help identify relevant collaborators and prior art from the lab's field notes without producing shallow or extractive outreach?

Risk: needs strong human review and evidence-backed suggestions.

##### D. Synthetic Voice in Participatory Channels

Role: civic-facing experiment.

Question: How should representatives and civic organisations handle constituent feedback when synthetic voice is cheap to generate?

Risk: hardening channels against synthetic feedback could also make participation harder for real constituents.

##### E. Hybrid Liquid Democracy Election

Role: civic-facing experiment.

Question: What happens when a bounded community runs an election using both paper and digital channels, with visible delegation and revocation?

Risk: voting experiments create legitimacy expectations even when framed as exploratory.

##### F. DMV / Queue and Classification Ritual

Role: civic/public-service-facing experiment.

Question: Can a frustrating service encounter be made more legible and humane without changing the underlying policy?

Risk: making a bad process feel better may obscure the need to change the underlying policy.

##### G. Digital Identity / Verification Ritual

Role: civic/public-service-facing experiment.

Question: What does it mean to prove who you are when identity verification, synthetic media, and AI agents collide?

Risk: identity systems can easily become exclusionary.

---

#### 6. What I Need Feedback On

1. Does Sparkle Bureaucracy work as the assessed prototype if framed as an experiment lab?
2. Which first experiment has the strongest political technology fit?
3. Which first experiment has the clearest civic community and evidence path?
4. Which experiment is most original relative to prior art?
5. Which experiment has the most serious methodological risk?
6. What would you need to see by the next check-in to believe this is on track?
7. Should the agent-assisted diary count as part of the lab, or only as supporting infrastructure?
8. Where does the playfulness become ethically risky?
9. What kind of engagement evidence would be strongest?

---

#### 7. My Current Belief

The strongest near-term structure is:

1. Treat Sparkle Bureaucracy as the assessed experiment lab prototype.
2. Use the agent-assisted diary and faculty feedback loop as the lab's operating infrastructure.
3. Choose one public/civic-facing experiment as the first flagship lab test.
4. Turn every candidate experiment into a comparable card with hypothesis, method, evidence, risks, and date.

What would change my mind:

- Faculty say the experiment lab is too broad unless one public/civic-facing experiment is clearly primary.
- A bounded community offers a concrete date for a liquid democracy or synthetic voice test.
- Prior art shows that one candidate is obsolete or predictably doomed.
- A mentor identifies a sharper civic community and user need for one option.

---

#### 8. Suggested Commenting Style

Please comment directly on:

- The strongest first experiment.
- The weakest or riskiest assumption.
- Missing prior art.
- Evidence that would make the project credible.
- Communities or collaborators I should talk to.
- Anything that feels ethically thin, overclaimed, or too broad.

#### The pitch (21:50)

🎉 Introducing Sparkle Bureaucracy (2026 Fellowship Prototype Previews)

Looking for feedback and engagement on experiment ideas and how to expand on some of the existing projects that I've worked on, now within this framing.

My prototype diary exists in two forms:

1. The Field Notes Fatima channel on Matrix is a place where I share readings, interesting projects I've come across, field notes - which are reflections on readings and projects I've encountered. You're welcome to join this channel and engage in discussions as well - with the note that I plan on publishing all of my research notes to some public website 🙂 via the (2) so your discussions/comments may also become public.
2. A public prototype diary: https://github.com/nwspk/sugaroverflow-prototype-diary - I've pushed some early research ideas and my matrix chat log, but I'll continue to build on this Work In Progress and make it a "dashboard" of my prototype progress. I'd like to challenge myself to work in the open as much as possible! And oops I was supposed to use the template so I'll do that.

#### The feedback

**Hannah:** I can intro you to the ai in gov people when you want to - also can help connect you to some people who are thinking about optimistic political narratives. As someone who worked in casework - can also see how this might help MP offices and how they do things. We will have people up for piloting things. Tyrone from campaign lab made this which is in this: voice notes to gov consultation: https://pipresist.uk/, can also help with experiments and evaluation frameworks.

**Edward Saperia:** I think this will go very far - very contemporary, hits very relevant questions, in a way that's quite straightforward, generative, and accessible. Links directly into humanistic tech and design of public services, but with an AI angle which makes it fresh. I have already given Fatima a load of links and contacts for this!

**Chris:** You should read Radical Help from Hilary Cottam (https://www.hilarycottam.com/radical-help/), she's coming at this from a different perspective but I think with somewhat similar goals. The general idea is that we have architected our systems poorly and can do better if we radically re-imagine how they could work. She's coming from a humanistic/inter-personal [angle]

**James:** I liked optimistical.

**Matt:** experiment lab for imagining how emerging tech can change the way our systems work without abandoning them
"if you can't dance it's not my revolution"

**Sinead:** I really enjoy the aesthetic of your slides ✨
**Edward:** you are basically the target audience for this project :)

**Emily:** so fun! It made me think of policy lab, would be happy to talk about use cases / take part in experiments! Questions like - why is public consultation such a dull awful bureaucratic cold process? Also: would you license a digital replica of you (image/video/voice) under what terms, and what would that involve - I'm starting to work on this, might be some overlap with your questions about synthetic voice;

**Anouk:** Please open source the sparkle passport / visa!!

- "Incredible! would love to see a write up of how this was built"
- "Needs quite a few more prompts / work but could be really brilliant!"
- "Really exciting. Being able to set up a campaign from scratch is great!"
- "How do we stop people having bad first experiences with stuff where they just get slop campaign plans dumped on them full of problems?"
- "type shit"
- "I think be more likely to use for smaller number of the specific elements (eg helping do a TOC or content pack) then all of it at once"
- "Getting activists to do things is an issue - making more campaigns packs we need to think about risk of overwhelm"
- "Fable IS good at architecture! what thinking mode did u use"
- "But also it's not gonna take ur job don't worry"
- "We want one affordable house!"

#### Private feedback to organisers (anonymous)

- "This is such a cool demo and explanation! Love the energy and way it is pitched"
- "Can the hack nights be more open to people who are not in london?"

#### AI live takes shown on the room screen

Excitement about end-to-end campaign setup · preference for narrow, specific tasks over full generation · concern about low-quality outputs eroding trust early · "needs refinement but the potential is clear."

#### What it feeds

The vote is the record's first hard audience metric (16 of 19 on the useful side). The comment wall's sharpest question — bad first experiences from slop campaign plans — plus the narrow-tasks preference are the quality bar Campaign Factory's next iteration carries. The non-London hack-nights request is a network-design input.

### Pivotal moments — SB-coded activities during the fellowship

A dated register of the things done at (and around) Newspeak House during the fellowship that count as Sparkle Bureaucracy activity. Dates and names supplied by Fatima (31 July 2026); each entry has a **Blurb TBD** placeholder for her to fill in — a lil paragraph on what it was, who was there, and how it landed. Where a weekly diary entry or lore doc already covers the event, it's cross-linked. *Extended 7 August 2026 with the inherited-convening thread (Code Club, Content Co-Creation Fridays, Prototype Coworking Day) and the umbrella-boundary note at the end.*

Related stub for future diary notes: [`reading-list.md`](#doc-reading-list) — books read as a result of founding SB.

##### 14 December – 1 April — the field-notes room

The public-by-contract Matrix room `field-notes: fatima`: 294 messages (heaviest December–January), rabbit holes on surveillance pricing, shadow governance, permission in rule-light systems, AI observability and eval blind spots — 15 arXiv papers, a dozen repos, the Civic Tech Field Guide. Went quiet 1 April; exported 2 April; catalogued into [`research-inventory.md`](#doc-research-inventory) Part 4. Raw export: [`data/matrix-field-notes-export-2026-04-02.json`](#doc-field-notes-transcript).

> **Blurb TBD (Fatima)**

##### 2 February — Code Club (inherited; biweekly)

Inherited from the previous cohort and the institution (earlier run visible on the house calendar through autumn 2025), run every other week from 2 February: solicited workshops on topics people actually wanted (openclaw among them), themed sessions, discussing and building together. Evolved over the year — Code Club (2 Feb–16 Mar) → Prototype Club (from 13 Apr, per the house calendar) → Prototype Coworking Day (17 Jul).

> **Blurb TBD (Fatima)**

##### 17 February — Clawclub #1

First of the Clawclub gatherings — the hack-night thread from the synthesis: openclaw made AI agents usable for everyone, so start hosting nights where people build. The Claw agents (Moltbook, penpals, research pipelines) grew out of this community.

> **Blurb TBD (Fatima)**

The passport-control border-patrol game, run at a birthday. The full SBA record is in [`research-inventory.md` Part 2](#doc-research-inventory) — identity checks, declarations, screening, visa printing, checkpoints, admin overrides, live stats. This is the "Birthday Passports" event named in the July site outline; the software is [`sparkle-border-authority`](https://sparkle-border-authority.vercel.app/) on GitHub.

> **Blurb TBD (Fatima)**

##### 15 March — Clawclub #2

> **Blurb TBD (Fatima)**

The Lumina House × Ration Club crossover — "social choreography at scale," the demonstration that the convening instinct works outside technical contexts (see [`synthesis.md`](#doc-synthesis)). This dates the "Lumina House takeover" event from the July site outline.

> **Blurb TBD (Fatima)**

The 18-agent evaluator-estimator presented at the PoliTech Awards showcase — constitutions inferred from public records, 321 projects ranked, Borda-count grand jury, Liquid Feedback stable winner. Full record in [`research-inventory.md` Part 3](#doc-research-inventory). Three days later the field-notes chat went quiet and the diary repo was founded ([weekly entry](#doc-weekly-2026-04-02)).

<a id="doc-weekly-2026-04-02"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/weekly/2026-04-02.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

### Week of 2 April — the diary repo is founded

*Reconstructed 31 July 2026 from the git record. Nine commits, all on Thursday 2 April between 13:26 and 16:34.*

#### What happened

The Matrix field-notes chat went quiet on 1 April; the chat was exported at 10:26 on 2 April, and the prototype diary repo was born the same day. In one afternoon session:

- **Initial import** — `research-inventory.md` (the combined source of record), `sensemaking-map.md`, `synthesis.md`, and the raw Matrix field-notes export (329 events, 14 Dec 2025 – 1 Apr 2026) into `assets/`.
- **README built up in six passes**, visible commit by commit:
  1. First README generated from the synthesis.
  2. Rewrite around the **Matt Stempeck mentor framing** — theories of change, the gems metaphor (sparkles hardened into gems and left behind), the experiment lab model, and a values statement.
  3. A **prototype check-in section** added — experiments framed as invitations for cohort and faculty input.
  4. SB definition section rewritten in Fatima's own voice.
  5. Full README rewrite — Fatima's voice throughout, tensions pulled in from the sensemaking map, experiments and theories of change made explicit.
  6. The **liquid democracy + hybrid paper/digital election experiment** added to the experiments list, followed by named organisations, partners, and funders (Faculty, TPXimpact, Creative Bureaucracy Festival, MHCLG, Knight Foundation, and others) in the legitimacy section.
- A final sync commit aligned README, research inventory, and synthesis.

The same nine commits exist in a sibling `research/` repo — the diary appears to have been mirrored or worked on in two checkouts.

#### Decisions visible in the record

- The diary's public voice is Fatima's, not a neutral report register.
- Theories of change and named potential partners belong in the public diary, not a private strategy doc.
- The liquid democracy / hybrid election idea enters the experiments list for the first time.

#### Gaps — for Fatima to fill

- ~~What prompted founding the repo that day~~ — answered 7 Aug 2026 via the house calendar: 2 April was the fellowship's **Prototype Previews** day. The checkpoint arrived; the diary became the deliverable. (Confirm, Fatima.)
- Which parts of the Matt Stempeck framing came from a live conversation versus his published mentoring material.

**Sources:** `sugaroverflow-prototype-diary` commits `1195c36`…`457978b` (2 Apr); Matrix export filename timestamp; duplicate history in `research/`.

<a id="doc-weekly-2026-05-13"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/weekly/2026-05-13.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

### Week of 11 May — Cohort Reunion: Spy Edition

*Reconstructed 31 July 2026 from the git record.*

#### What happened

The **NHIFA Field Console** (`nwspk/nhifa-spy-game`) was built in a fast arc around the **Newspeak House Cohort '24 × '25 Ration Club Reunion — Wednesday 13 May, 19:00–22:00**:

- **12 May** — "setting up the spy game."
- **13 May (event day)** — a dense run of commits right up to doors: agent dossiers and codes, exfil mechanics, reset slips, handler bonus codes, an A6-panels-onto-A3 **mini-zine imposition pipeline** (printed dossiers as physical artifacts), pytest scaffolding for the dossier builder, and an admin scoreboard.
- **14–15 May** — the aftermath: highlights, personal **debrief cards** on a debrief page, README brought in line with what actually shipped, repo prepped for going public, debrief deploys.
- **12 May**, in the Newspeak `web/` repo: "Add May 13 override for Ration Club special event" — the house site pointed at the event.

The design: the app reveals each player's mission, stores keyword guesses, accepts handler bonus codes, locks submissions at 21:45, and runs a paced reveal on the projector at 22:00 — while Director and Handler missions live **on paper**. Networking as a game, run on the intake → process → decision → artifact → reveal skeleton.

#### Sparkle Bureaucracy reading

This is the "Cohort Reunion: Spy Edition" later listed in the July site outline's events section. Pattern-wise it's a sibling of Sparkle Border Authority: procedural fiction (missions, codes, handlers, submission locks) wrapped around a social event, with printed artifacts (dossier zines, reset slips) and a live dashboard — and this time a *post-event ritual* too (debrief cards), which SBA never had.

#### Gaps — for Fatima to fill

- How the night actually went; how many players; what the debrief cards said.
- Whether any reunion attendees became SB collaborators afterwards (engagement evidence for the assessment diary).

**Sources:** `cohort-reunion-project` (21 commits, 12–15 May) and its README; `web/` commit of 12 May.

<a id="doc-weekly-2026-06-19"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/weekly/2026-06-19.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

### Week of: 19/06

*Written 19 June 2026 (previously uncommitted as `weekly/2026-06-19.md` in the diary repo); imported verbatim on 31 July 2026. The scaffold it describes — `submission-diary.md`, `experiments.md`, `experiment-research-sprint.md`, faculty-feedback docs, prior-art, engagement and technical logs — now lives alongside this entry in `lore/`.*

#### 3.1 What I Did This Week

- Reviewed the existing prototype diary repo against the Newspeak House prototype work diary template.
- Identified that the repo had strong narrative synthesis, but needed more explicit assessment evidence: weekly entries, experiment logs, prior art, engagement evidence, technical notes, risks, and faculty feedback tracking.
- Reorganised the repo around a template-shaped [submission diary](#doc-submission-diary) while keeping the more personal public narrative in [README.md](#doc-readme).
- Added first-pass support logs for experiments, faculty feedback, prior art, engagement, and technical notes.

#### 3.2 Decisions Made

- Keep GitHub as the canonical project diary and source of record.
- Use a separate commentable surface, likely Google Docs, when faculty feedback needs inline comments or lower friction.
- Treat the agent-assisted diary as a possible prototype experiment, but constrain it to drafting, triage, and suggestions unless human-approved.
- Treat Sparkle Bureaucracy itself as the assessed prototype: an experiment lab whose first experiments need clear methods, dates, risks, and evidence.
- Use a Google Doc as the primary faculty feedback surface, with the repo preserving the source text and response log.

#### 3.3 What Changed in My Understanding

- The diary cannot only be a compelling narrative. It needs to show adaptation over time.
- The strongest assessment evidence will come from dated experiment cards, weekly reflections, faculty feedback responses, and engagement logs.
- The research infrastructure itself might be part of the prototype: a system that helps turn field notes and community input into public learning.

#### 3.4 Evidence of Civic Use / Engagement Attempt

Existing evidence to consolidate:

- Sparkle Border Authority was used by guests at a live Ration Club event.
- PoliTech Awards involved a public ranking / evaluation process and audience participation.
- Project Mirror created cohort-facing questions about synthetic representation and evaluative constitutions.
- Existing mentor and community feedback is scattered through [research-inventory.md](#doc-research-inventory), [synthesis.md](#doc-synthesis), and the Matrix export in [assets/](#doc-field-notes-transcript).

New engagement action:

- Created [faculty-feedback.md](#doc-faculty-feedback) to make feedback requests more structured and easier to respond to.

#### 3.5 What Broke / Failed / Felt Wrong

- The previous repo structure made the project feel more polished than it was from an assessment-evidence perspective.
- The public narrative did not yet make it obvious that Sparkle Bureaucracy itself is the prototype, with the experiments acting as the lab's programme.
- Faculty feedback did not have a dedicated capture surface.
- Prior art and engagement evidence were present in the research inventory but not easy to scan.
- The Matrix field notes are useful raw evidence, but not yet transformed into weekly diary entries.

#### 3.6 Next Steps

- Prepare the faculty-facing Google Doc from [faculty-feedback-brief.md](#doc-faculty-feedback-brief).
- Choose the strongest first experiment in the Sparkle Bureaucracy lab sequence.
- Turn each candidate prototype into a one-page experiment card with a date, method, evidence plan, and risk section.
- Fill in the prior-art log with verified sources and librarian feedback.
- Gather screenshots, quotes, metrics, and links from Sparkle Border Authority, PoliTech Awards, and Project Mirror.

<a id="doc-weekly-2026-06-23"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/weekly/2026-06-23.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

### Week of 22 June — prospective experiments, and Topic begins

*Reconstructed 31 July 2026 from the git record.*

#### What happened

- **23 June (evening)** — `prospective-experiments/README.md` lands in the diary repo (uncommitted, 18KB): the working idea bank for future SB experiments. It tightens the definition of what counts — an experiment needs all four of **institutional skeleton, affective re-skin, decision pressure, durable artifact** — and ranks a shortlist of nine: Public Voice Customs, Mirror Commons, The Exception Window, Permission Passport Office, The Value Court, Claw Civil Service, Shadow Labour Claims Office, Liquid Mandate Assembly, and the Sparkle Border Field Kit. Top canonical-potential candidates: Public Voice Customs ("pol.is for synthetic-voice-era consultations") and Mirror Commons (consentful synthetic representation).
- **23 June (same day)** — the **Topic** build kicks off: 43 commits to `sugaroverflow/timetable` in one day, continuing through the end of the month (~66 commits by 30 June). Topic (topic.forum, formerly "Timetable") is collaborative forum software for proposing topics, voting with hearts, sharing availability, and shaping a schedule together — billed in its README as *"a Newspeak House × Sparkle Bureaucracy production."*

#### Decisions visible in the record

- The experiment bar was raised: "if an idea does not change who can act, how judgment is made, what becomes visible, or how a decision can be contested, it is probably not strong enough yet."
- Rather than one of the nine shortlisted experiments, the thing that actually got built next was **Topic** — participatory scheduling infrastructure. The record doesn't say whether that was a deliberate choice over the shortlist or a response to a concrete Newspeak need that arrived with its own momentum.

#### Gaps — for Fatima to fill

- What triggered Topic on that exact day (the same evening as the experiment shortlist!) — a request, an event need, an itch?
- Whether Topic is understood as an SB experiment (it plausibly passes all four "what counts" tests: scheduling ritual, hearts-and-availability re-skin, collective decision pressure, a durable forum artifact) or as adjacent infrastructure.
- Whether the nine shortlisted experiments are still live candidates or superseded by the July research direction.

**Sources:** `prospective-experiments/README.md` (file timestamp 23 Jun); `timetable` commit history (23–30 Jun); `timetable` README.

<a id="doc-weekly-2026-07-16"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/weekly/2026-07-16.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

### Weeks of 6 & 13 July — Campaign Factory and the AI & Campaigning Conference

*Reconstructed 31 July 2026 from the git record.*

#### What happened

- **6–11 July** — Topic (timetable) development continues at a low hum (~9 commits).
- **12–17 July** — a very intense sprint (~122 commits, 60 of them on 15 July alone) builds **Campaign Factory** (`CampaignLab/campaign-factory`): give it a UK local or public-policy problem, a named place, and your own Anthropic/OpenRouter API key, and it researches the decision route, maps power and pressure, proposes strategy and tactics, and drafts campaign materials — *showing the work as it happens*. Live at campaign-factory.vercel.app.
- **15–16 July** — **Conference Pulse** (`CampaignLab/conference-pulse`) built in parallel: the live companion site for Campaign Lab's **AI & Campaigning Conference, 16 July at Newspeak House** — schedule, anonymous per-panel comments, votes, an organiser dashboard, and a big-screen room view.

#### Sparkle Bureaucracy reading

Both projects are the SB pattern applied outside the fellowship bubble, with a partner community (Campaign Lab): a serious procedural skeleton (campaign planning's decision routes and power maps; a conference's schedule and Q&A) made legible and participatory, with the work shown rather than hidden. Campaign Factory later headlines the "projects" list in the July site outline. This is also the calendar/alliances strategy in action — building *with* an adjacent community rather than only under the SB banner.

#### Gaps — for Fatima to fill

- How the conference went; whether Campaign Factory was demoed or used live in the room; reactions.
- The Campaign Lab relationship goes back at least to the OpenClaw 101 workshop run for them on 24 May — how it started before that is still unrecorded.
- Whether Campaign Factory outputs have been used on a real campaign yet (the "gem" test).

**Sources:** `ai-campaigning-conference` (Campaign Factory) commit history 12–22 Jul and README; `conference-pulse` commits 15–16 Jul and README; `timetable` commits 6–16 Jul.

<a id="doc-weekly-2026-07-27"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/weekly/2026-07-27.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

### Weeks of 20 & 27 July — hardening, the network question, and the rethink

*Reconstructed 31 July 2026 from the git record. The 24–31 July thread is covered in depth in the narrative entry [How networks are born — and second thoughts](#doc-2026-07-31-network-formation-and-mission-rethink).*

#### What happened

- **20–22 July** — Campaign Factory hardening: architecture simplified, a shared model-error taxonomy, type-safety batches, deploy fixes, and a **free-access code running on the house key** (`FREE-CAMPAIGN-BUILD`, kill-switch off) — lowering the barrier from "bring your own API key" to "just try it."
- **21–25 July** — Topic keeps moving (~37 commits).
- **Friday 24 July** — the session with **Ed and James on how networks are born**: mission + brand/taste, inspired-by examples, people show up and build together. (Narrative entry has the detail.)
- **Monday 27 July** — a 32-commit **Topic QA sprint** (PRs #139–146): Atom feeds per forum, social preview cards, invite-email fixes, shuffle-seed URL handling, docs and dead-code cleanup, CI bumps. Topic is being finished like a product, not a prototype.
- **30 July** — the `research-longlist/` inspiration sprint in the diary repo (see [`../research-longlist/`](#doc-research-longlist-readme)), plus an uncommitted diary README edit.
- **31 July** — the lore is imported into the site repo (PR #1), the week is written up, and the **mission rethink** begins: not comfortable taking the network formulation forward as-is; session with Hannah on what the work is actually for. Ideas to follow.

#### Decisions visible in the record

- Campaign Factory shifted from BYOK-only to subsidised free access — a deliberate accessibility move, with a kill-switch as the safety valve.
- Topic got a quality bar (QA sprint, CI, docs) that signals intent to hand it to real users.
- The diary's canonical home moved from the standalone repo to `lore/` in the site repo.

#### Gaps — for Fatima to fill

- Where Topic is deployed and who is using it now (topic.forum status).
- Free-access usage of Campaign Factory since 21 July, and cost on the house key.
- ~~The Hannah session output~~ — now recorded in [What I actually care about](#doc-2026-07-31-what-i-care-about), alongside the Sunday reflection [The resistance is information](#doc-2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information) (26 July).

**Sources:** `ai-campaigning-conference` commits 20–22 Jul; `timetable` commits 21–27 Jul (PRs #139–146); `sugaroverflow-prototype-diary` commit `d828efc` and file timestamps; site repo PR #1; this conversation (24 & 31 Jul sessions, as told by Fatima).
