<a id="doc-execution-journal-2026-06-19-diary-structure"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/execution-journal/2026-06-19-diary-structure.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

#### 2026-06-19T15:51:57Z - Prototype Diary Structure

##### Goal

Reorganise the repository so Sparkle Bureaucracy remains readable as a public narrative while also satisfying the Newspeak House prototype work diary template.

##### Changes

- Added `submission-diary.md` as the template-shaped assessment diary.
- Added separate logs for experiments, faculty feedback, prior art, engagement evidence, technical notes, and weekly entries.
- Added `weekly/2026-06-19.md` as the first structured weekly diary entry.
- Updated `README.md` so reviewers can find the new diary and feedback surfaces.
- Fixed a stale `research-inventory.md` note that said `sensemaking-map.md` was deleted.

##### Decisions

- Keep `README.md` as the public narrative and orientation page.
- Use `submission-diary.md` as the formal assessment surface.
- Keep evidence in focused logs rather than overloading the README.
- Treat the agent-assisted diary as a candidate experiment and research infrastructure layer, not as an unbounded autonomous publishing system.
- Preserve human approval boundaries for any generated diary synthesis.

##### Tradeoffs

- The repo now has more files, but each file maps more directly to a diary template requirement.
- The assessed prototype is now Sparkle Bureaucracy as an experiment lab; the structure intentionally keeps multiple candidate first experiments alive until faculty feedback narrows the lab sequence.
- No interactive website was built yet because markdown plus a possible commentable Google Doc is lower-friction for faculty review.

##### Risks

- The project may still look broad until one first experiment is selected as the lab's initial public/civic-facing test.
- Engagement evidence and prior art are seeded but need stronger source links, quotes, screenshots, and dates.
- The agent-assisted diary could be perceived as too meta unless connected to civic use and community validation.

##### Verification

- Reviewed the local repository structure before editing.
- Fetched the Newspeak House prototype diary template text and mapped repository files to its sections.
- Planned a follow-up markdown/link consistency pass after edits.

##### Demo Impact

The project can now be shown in three layers:

1. Public story: `README.md`.
2. Assessment diary: `submission-diary.md`.
3. Evidence and adaptation logs: experiment, feedback, prior art, engagement, technical, and weekly files.

##### Customer-Facing Context

The new structure makes the project easier to explain to faculty, mentors, collaborators, and future civic/public-sector partners because it separates vision, evidence, feedback, and implementation notes.

##### Next Recommended Step

Use the Google Doc feedback brief to ask faculty which candidate experiment should become the lab's first public/civic-facing test, then turn the selected experiment into a dated test plan with evidence and risk criteria.

#### 2026-06-19T15:59:37Z - Experiment Lab Framing Confirmed

##### Goal

Update the diary structure after confirming that the assessed prototype is Sparkle Bureaucracy itself, framed as an experiment lab.

##### Changes

- Added `faculty-feedback-brief.md` as the Google-Doc-friendly faculty review surface.
- Updated `submission-diary.md` so Sparkle Bureaucracy is explicitly the prototype and the open question is the lab's first experiment sequence.
- Updated `faculty-feedback.md` so it tracks faculty critique from the Google Doc rather than acting as the primary comment surface.
- Updated `experiments.md` and `weekly/2026-06-19.md` to distinguish lab infrastructure from first public/civic-facing experiments.
- Updated `README.md` so the feedback ask is about shaping the lab's first experiments, not choosing a different prototype.

##### Decisions

- The prototype is Sparkle Bureaucracy: an experiment lab.
- Faculty feedback should happen primarily in a Google Doc, with the repo maintaining the source text and response log.
- The agent-assisted diary and faculty feedback loop are lab infrastructure; they may still be experiments, but the lab also needs a first public/civic-facing experiment sequence.

##### Tradeoffs

- The lab framing is more accurate to the project, but it requires discipline: each experiment still needs dates, methods, risks, and evidence.
- A Google Doc lowers faculty feedback friction but creates a second surface that must be reconciled back into the repo.

##### Risks

- Faculty may still ask for one experiment to be clearly primary for assessment.
- Feedback may remain in Google Doc comments unless it is actively copied back into `faculty-feedback.md`.
- The distinction between lab infrastructure and public/civic-facing experiments needs to stay visible.

##### Verification

- Planned a markdown consistency and diff check after updates.

##### Demo Impact

The demo narrative can now open with: "Sparkle Bureaucracy is the prototype: an experiment lab. These are the first experiments it can run, and this is how faculty feedback shapes the lab."

##### Customer-Facing Context

The lab framing makes Sparkle Bureaucracy easier to explain to collaborators and sponsors because it is not a single tool looking for a problem; it is a repeatable method for turning bureaucratic rituals into testable civic experiments.

##### Next Recommended Step

Create the faculty Google Doc from `faculty-feedback-brief.md`, collect comments, and reconcile them into `faculty-feedback.md` and `experiments.md`.

#### 2026-06-19T16:14:09Z - Experiment Research Sprint

##### Goal

Explore the strongest candidate experiments for Sparkle Bureaucracy as an experiment lab and turn the findings into a reusable memo for faculty conversations.

##### Changes

- Spawned four research subagents covering synthetic voice, hybrid liquid democracy, public-service verification/identity, and lab infrastructure.
- Added `experiment-research-sprint.md` with ranked experiment recommendations, smallest credible experiment designs, evidence plans, risks, prior-art leads, and faculty questions.
- Linked the research sprint memo from `README.md` and `experiments.md`.

##### Decisions

- Recommend **The Verification Queue** as the strongest first public/civic-facing flagship experiment.
- Keep **Agent-Assisted Lab Clerk** as internal lab infrastructure rather than a primary faculty-facing surface.
- Keep **Consultation Customs** and **Hybrid Delegation Assembly** as strong second-wave experiments.
- Continue using Google Docs as the primary faculty feedback surface.

##### Tradeoffs

- The memo is a synthesis, not a complete literature review.
- Some prior-art leads need deeper verification before being treated as settled evidence.
- Ranking experiments helps focus faculty discussion, but the final sequence should still respond to faculty feedback and available participants.

##### Risks

- The first flagship may still need a practitioner or affected-user group to avoid feeling like theatre.
- Synthetic voice is timely but could become too adversarial or AI-detection-focused.
- Liquid democracy is memorable but can be overwhelmed by voting legitimacy and secrecy issues.

##### Verification

- Used parallel subagent briefs and direct source checks.
- Planned markdown consistency checks after writing the memo.

##### Demo Impact

The project now has a concrete experiment sequence to discuss: internal lab clerk first, Verification Queue as likely flagship, Consultation Customs and Hybrid Delegation Assembly as follow-ons.

##### Customer-Facing Context

The research sprint makes Sparkle Bureaucracy easier to explain as a lab with a programme, not a loose collection of ideas. It also gives faculty a practical way to critique scope, evidence, and ethics.

##### Next Recommended Step

Use the Google Doc feedback brief to ask faculty whether The Verification Queue should be the first flagship and what practitioner/community involvement is required for credibility.

#### 2026-06-23T12:28:46Z - Interactive Public Lab Wiki Prototype

##### Goal

Turn the public diary repo into a more creative, navigable public lab surface without replacing the private faculty feedback and outreach workflow.

##### Changes

- Added `index.html` as a static public lab wiki entry point.
- Added `styles.css` for the responsive wiki layout, experiment cards, evidence ledger, and feedback sections.
- Added `app.js` for experiment search, status filters, selected experiment detail view, and a local-only private note queue.
- Updated `README.md` so the interactive wiki is the first public starting point.

##### Decisions

- Use a dependency-free static site so the repo remains easy to host on GitHub Pages or open locally.
- Keep faculty feedback in Google Docs and repo markdown, not in an AI-first chat surface.
- Treat the local note queue as a sketch of the agent-assisted diary workflow, not as a real published feedback system.
- Make the public/private boundary visible in the interface so collaborator prospecting and raw faculty notes are not accidentally presented as public evidence.

##### Tradeoffs

- The site duplicates a small amount of experiment summary content from markdown files so it can work without a build step.
- The note queue uses browser `localStorage`; it is useful for demonstrating the workflow but is not collaboration infrastructure.
- No framework was added, which limits future content automation but keeps the prototype light.

##### Risks

- If the wiki becomes the main source of truth, duplicated experiment copy could drift from `experiments.md` and `experiment-research-sprint.md`.
- The site is a prototype shell and still needs real feedback links, dates, and artifact uploads.
- The static note queue must not be mistaken for secure or shared storage.

##### Verification

- Planned syntax checks for the JavaScript and static asset references.
- Planned repository diff review after the files were added.

##### Demo Impact

The repo can now be shown as a public lab notebook rather than a folder of markdown files. Faculty and collaborators can scan the current experiment sequence, see the public/private boundary, and understand why the first decision is about experiment selection.

##### Customer-Facing Context

The wiki demonstrates the core Sparkle Bureaucracy operating pattern: public evidence, clear decision receipts, visible risks, and private trust-preserving working layers.

##### Next Recommended Step

Promote the strongest experiment cards into dated run plans and add a real faculty Google Doc link once the feedback doc exists.

#### 2026-06-23T19:05:13Z - Sparkle Bureaucracy Experiment Reframing

##### Goal

Sharpen the prospective experiment list so it reads as Sparkle Bureaucracy rather than generic civic workshop design.

##### Changes

- Added `prospective-experiments/README.md` as a dedicated idea bank for future experiments.
- Linked the prospective experiments directory from `README.md`.
- Reframed the experiment standard around institutional skeleton, affective re-skin, decision pressure, and durable artifact.
- Added a sharper shortlist: Public Voice Customs, Mirror Commons, The Exception Window, Permission Passport Office, The Value Court, Claw Civil Service, Shadow Labour Claims Office, Liquid Mandate Assembly, and Sparkle Border Field Kit.

##### Decisions

- Treat Sparkle Bureaucracy experiments as serious institutional rituals with a theatrical layer and durable artifacts, not generic workshops.
- Prefer naming and formats that feel like offices, customs desks, windows, courts, warrants, passports, ledgers, and field kits.
- Keep The Exception Window as the strongest near-term fellowship evidence route.
- Keep Public Voice Customs and Mirror Commons as higher-upside canonical candidates.
- Keep Claw Civil Service as lab infrastructure, not the sole public-facing prototype.

##### Tradeoffs

- The new document is more opinionated and may retire some generic ideas.
- The strongest canonical candidates may be harder to run quickly than the near-term fellowship route.
- Several ideas still need faculty/practitioner critique before scheduling.

##### Risks

- The experiment names may become too internally stylised if not paired with clear methods and evidence.
- Public Voice Customs and Mirror Commons have higher ethical and methodological burdens than The Exception Window.
- The repo now has overlapping experiment lists that need consolidation after faculty feedback.

##### Verification

- Reviewed `README.md`, `synthesis.md`, `sensemaking-map.md`, `research-inventory.md`, and prior experiment docs.
- Ran a synthesis-lens subagent over the repository material and incorporated the resulting framing.
- Planned markdown/link checks after edits.

##### Demo Impact

The project can now explain why a candidate is or is not a Sparkle Bureaucracy experiment. This makes faculty discussion sharper: the question becomes which ritual and artifact should become canonical, not just which topic is interesting.

##### Customer-Facing Context

The new framing supports sponsor and collaborator conversations by making each experiment legible as a repeatable institutional pattern: customs, mirror, exception window, passport, court, civil service, claims office, assembly, or field kit.

##### Next Recommended Step

Consolidate the public wiki experiment cards around the new shortlist and turn The Exception Window into a dated run plan with roles, materials, evidence, and faculty questions.

<a id="doc-execution-journal-2026-07-30-sparkle-longlist-research"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/execution-journal/2026-07-30-sparkle-longlist-research.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: The dated record

#### 2026-07-30T00:00:00Z - Sparkle Inspiration Longlist Research

##### Goal

Build an evidence-aware research engine for explaining Sparkle Bureaucracy through projects, organisations, source directories, people, and networks, while keeping projects as the primary unit.

##### Changes

- Added `research-longlist/` with a reusable research method, evidence model, and website-card format.
- Calibrated six known cases against emerging technology, bureaucracy, affect, civic capacity, power, and durable capability.
- Created linked, deduplicated longlists of 25 projects, 25 organisations, 25 directories or archives, and 25 people or networks.
- Retained the wider discovery pool and full evidence profiles in three lane-based research files.
- Proposed 10 website inspirations, 6 valuable projects where Sparkle is absent or unproven, and a 10-project private reserve including a founder-nominated priority challenger.
- Added the research directory to the repository README.
- Added a separate movements-and-tactics research layer, seeded with Mexico's Glitter Revolution and Portland's Operation Inflation, so collective affective practices are not misrepresented as authored projects.

##### Decisions

- Treat the raw 40–50-project pool as discovery material and the 25-project set as the evidence-enriched longlist.
- Treat emerging technology as Sparkle Bureaucracy's current arena, not yet as an essential property of Sparkle itself.
- Define affect broadly enough to include relief, dignity, discomfort, certainty, empathy, and productive disagreement, not only delight.
- Treat designed affect and experienced response as separate evidence questions. A work's form can establish its affective ambition without an event, survey, publication, or participant quotation; reception evidence is required only for claims about what an audience actually felt.
- Classify projects by their relationship to the research question: exemplar, ancestor, infrastructure awaiting Sparkle, or warning case.
- Make power, contestability, and institutional follow-through visible in the editorial judgment rather than rewarding friendly interfaces by default.
- Treat affective presentation as part of a project's operative design when it is the mechanism that makes otherwise inaccessible systems and technical power perceptible. Following founder review, `Models All the Way Down` entered the inspired set and `Cart Life` moved to the reserve.
- Do not turn the inclusion rubric into a pass/fail definition of inspiration. `Models All the Way Down` has an indirect bureaucratic relationship—the dataset is an administrative artifact rather than a bureaucracy—but remains valuable because it demonstrates affective legibility exceptionally well.
- Keep movements beside the project portfolio rather than forcing them into it. Research the political demand, institutional power, affective tactic, replication, risk and attribution separately.

##### Tradeoffs

- The sprint prioritised breadth and editorial usefulness over exhaustive literature review.
- Evidence depth is uneven: the selected website set is supported well enough for drafting, but every public card should still receive a final source audit.
- Some strong civic projects remain in the reserve because the first public set would otherwise overrepresent legal access or digital deliberation.
- Interviews and original participant research were intentionally deferred for the later events phase.

##### Risks

- The proposed definition remains a working hypothesis until the founder resolves whether technology, hopefulness, power transfer, and under-resourced civic use are requirements or programme commitments.
- Several affect claims rely on project-hosted case studies rather than independent evaluation.
- Warning cases can be misread as endorsements unless the website's classification and unresolved tensions are visually explicit.
- Dynamic project pages, programme status, and organisational ownership may change and need checking immediately before publication.

##### Verification

- Three research subagents independently covered affect and organisational ritual, public-service and under-resourced civic capacity, and AI and participatory governance.
- The primary agent reviewed and synthesised all three discovery files.
- Verified that each final longlist contains exactly 25 entries.
- Checked the repository diff for whitespace errors and reviewed the local cross-links among method, calibration, longlists, evidence profiles, and selection.
- No interviews, participant outreach, or empirical testing were conducted.

##### Demo Impact

Sparkle Bureaucracy can now explain itself through a small, varied public portfolio rather than an abstract rubric. The larger linked corpus remains available as a private research engine for future newsletters, events, and substitutions.

##### Customer-Facing Context

The research makes the project's central trust claim more concrete: an affective public-service encounter should reveal rather than disguise power, create meaningful room to act or contest, and leave behind civic capability. It also records where evidence is missing instead of converting aspiration into a user-experience claim.

##### Next Recommended Step

Resolve the seven founder questions in `research-longlist/website-selection.md`, then source-audit and edit the eight strongest cards into the redesigned website.

#### 2026-07-30T12:10:18Z - Movements and Affective Tactics Deep Research

##### Goal

Use Mexico's Glitter Revolution and Portland's inflatable anti-ICE protests as anchors for a deeper, globally varied investigation of movements whose material, ritual, costume, choreography or humour offers a useful account of Sparkle.

##### Changes

- Added `research-longlist/movements-deep-research.md` with a calibrated movement-specific rubric and six discovery lanes.
- Built a sourced longlist of exactly 25 movements and affective tactics across multiple geographies and institutional targets.
- Completed twelve deeper evidence profiles that separate designed affect, experienced response, strategic effect and political outcome.
- Recommended eight cases for a first public “Movements we're inspired by” section and retained thematic pairings as a private reserve.
- Expanded the website selection from two movement seeds to eight concise, evidence-linked cards.
- Recorded seven findings about Sparkle and eight design principles for Sparkle Bureaucracy's own work.

##### Decisions

- Research the **movement** and the **tactic** as linked but distinct units: the former carries demand, people and political context; the latter carries the affective mechanism and reusable repertoire.
- Define movement durability broadly enough to include a score, garment, cadence, gesture, collective role or sound protocol.
- Require an institutional target and an inhabitable participant action; visual memorability by itself is insufficient.
- Treat virality as evidence of circulation, not civic impact.
- Keep both founder anchors in the website set because they do different work: glitter marks authority, while inflatables reorganise participant roles and the evidentiary record.
- Use Holograms for Freedom as a productive near-miss: its technological concept clearly explains a coercive law, but evidence for durable civic capability is much weaker than evidence for spectacle.
- Treat culturally grounded forms such as haka as sources of principles, not reproducible formats.

##### Tradeoffs

- Breadth was prioritised across the full 25; twelve cases received deep profiles and the remaining thirteen received source-backed longlist records.
- Policy outcomes are not attributed to individual symbols or performances where a larger coalition and political process produced the change.
- Current movement research relies on public reporting and published accounts; no activists were contacted, consistent with the decision to reserve interviews for Sparkle Bureaucracy's own events.

##### Risks

- Short website cards can aestheticise danger or strip a tactic from its demand, geography and movement authorship.
- Collective tactics rarely have clean singular authorship; attribution must be checked again before publication.
- Repressive political contexts and legal status can change, so current cases need a fresh safety and source check at publication time.
- The movement selection currently draws heavily from feminist, queer and democracy struggles because their affective repertoires are unusually well documented; future newsletters should keep widening disability, labour, Indigenous, migration and mutual-aid coverage.

##### Verification

- Checked that the movement/tactic table contains exactly 25 numbered records.
- Used movement, archive, institutional, scholarly and reputable reporting sources; lead databases were not treated as final evidence where stronger sources were available.
- Reviewed each website candidate for a distinct portfolio role rather than selecting multiple variants of the same visual tactic.
- Rechecked that designed affect remains independent of formal audience-evaluation requirements.

##### Demo Impact

The website can now show that Sparkle is not limited to friendly public services or polished projects. It can be oppositional, grief-bearing, ridiculous or accusatory while still building civic agency, procedural understanding and reusable capacity.

##### Next Recommended Step

Choose whether the public section launches with all eight movement cards or four paired cards, then source-audit image rights and write final 80–120 word versions in the site's voice.

<a id="doc-2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Essays & captures

### The resistance is information

*Diary entry, Sunday 26 July 2026 — two days after the network-formation session with Ed and James.*

I couldn't settle after Friday. The advice was clear and generous — mission, taste, inspirations, let people show up — and something in me kept refusing it. I spent part of the weekend working the resistance through with an AI thinking partner, and it turned out not to be disagreement with the strategy at all.

The distinction that unlocked it: an experiment says *judge the thing I made*. A network says *trust my judgment about what matters* — subscribe to my perception and taste before I can prove them. There's no artifact standing between me and the audience. I trust my ability to execute far more than I trust my right to originate authority.

Underneath that, three fears — and a network-first launch activates all three at once:

1. **Taking up space as a leader.** I'm used to authority arriving because I was demonstrably useful. Convening a field means exercising authority prospectively — *I see a field here and I'm qualified to name it* — before the field confirms I was right.
2. **Being read as unserious.** "Sparkle" touches an old pattern: being read as warm, playful, and approachable first, and technically deep only on discovery. Launching the playful thing before the rigor is visible risks confirming exactly the misreading I've worked against. Experiments aren't just research — they're armour. They let me prove the sparkle contains rigor before anyone can dismiss it as decorative.
3. **Failing visibly.** A failed experiment can be analysed — wrong format, wrong hypothesis, iterate. An empty signup form feels like a verdict on whether anyone wants to be led by me. Keeping the network unlaunched keeps it potentially brilliant; publishing makes it measurable, and therefore rejectable.

The sentence that stung the most: **I know I can build something worthy of attention. I do not yet trust that my way of seeing is itself worthy of attention.** That's not insecurity about ability. It's insecurity about *generative authority* — whether I'm allowed to name a field before the field confirms I was right.

There was also an authorship bind that explains why an ordinary strategy conversation could leave me in tears. I don't just want to do what Ed thinks is best — I want to *agree*. If I follow the plan without metabolising it, Sparkle Bureaucracy becomes a performance of his conviction instead of an expression of mine. And his certainty, which comes from real institutional experience, makes it harder — not easier — to feel like the author.

Then the picture changed. Talking to Ed again, I realised he isn't against experiments at all. He thought I didn't have concrete ideas to implement. But I do — I've been scared to articulate them, because saying them aloud makes me accountable for becoming their leader. So we've been in a loop: I fear the ideas sound unserious → I don't share them → he reads the concept as vague → he recommends the lighter, network-first approach → it feels more insubstantial → I get more afraid.

The way out isn't to prove the full project. It's to show the concealed middle layer: *I have experiments in mind. I haven't shared them because I'm afraid they'll sound frivolous — not because I lack ideas.* And the record backs this up — the [nine-experiment shortlist from June](#doc-prospective-experiments) exists, and [fourteen pivotal moments](#doc-pivotal-moments) are dated in this diary. The programme was never missing. It was concealed.

*Next: [What I actually care about](#doc-2026-07-31-what-i-care-about) — the session with Hannah.*

---

<a id="doc-2026-07-31-network-formation-and-mission-rethink"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/2026-07-31-network-formation-and-mission-rethink.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Essays & captures

### How networks are born — and second thoughts

*Diary entry, 31 July 2026*

This entry records a fast week: a session with Ed and James on network formation, a site restructure plan, a research sprint on inspirations, and then the moment where I stopped and questioned the formulation itself.

---

#### The session with Ed and James (Friday 24 July)

We talked about how networks are born. Their model, roughly:

You don't start a network by building an institution. You start with an idea and a specific brand — a style, a taste — and you put it into the world. People recognise something in it, show up, and you organically start building things together. The network forms around the sensibility, not around an org chart.

The homework that came out of it:

1. **Really think about SB's mission.** Get it to something I can stand behind and say in one breath.
2. **Find the example projects we're inspired by.** A constellation of existing work explains the idea better than a definition can — and it signals the taste that people would be showing up for.

---

#### The plan that followed: rework the site

I sketched a new structure for the website, built around that logic — mission first, then the why, then the constellation, then our own projects and events.

**Working mission draft:** *Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network of people using AI to explore optimistic organisational and service futures.*

The structure:

- **Mission statement** + newsletter sign-up
- **Why this mission** — we are doing this, for this reason, at this time, and we are inspired by these projects
  - **Organisations we're inspired by**
  - **Projects we're inspired by** — e.g. *Papers, Please* (Lucas Pope, 2013): you play the border officer; documents, stamps, discrepancies, quotas, and a family to feed. It preserves the ritual of border crossing but affects the audience in a way that implies these processes are not humane — it puts you in the seat of power. Also *Cart Life*.
  - **Projects that do inspiring work, but lack sparkle** — Document Assembly Line / Court Forms Online / Spot (Suffolk LIT Lab); genagents (Stanford, Park et al. 2024); Pathfinder (Mobile Pathways); Talk to the City (AI Objectives Institute)
- **Projects** — campaign factory, timetable, Sparkle Border Authority, Project Mirror ("I built you a digital twin!")
  - With an **inclusion rubric**: (1) use of emerging technology (AI); (2) bureaucratic ritual or process; (3) designed affect — how did people feel; (4) was it sparkly?; (5) SB's stated boost for under-resourced civic contexts
- **Events, past and upcoming** — Lumina House takeover (community crossover), Cohort Reunion: Spy Edition (networking as game), Birthday Passports
- **About** — the dictator

---

#### The research sprint (30 July)

To fill the "inspired by" sections with real evidence rather than vibes, I ran a desk-research sprint. The full output is in [`research-longlist/`](#doc-research-longlist-readme) — method, calibration, four linked longlists of 25 (projects, organisations, directories/archives, people/networks), deep evidence profiles across three lanes, a movements-and-tactics companion lane anchored on Mexico's Glitter Revolution and Portland's inflatable anti-ICE protesters, and a proposed website portfolio in [`website-selection.md`](#doc-research-longlist-website-selection). The change record is in [`docs/execution-journal/2026-07-30-sparkle-longlist-research.md`](#doc-execution-journal-2026-07-30-sparkle-longlist-research).

The provisional definition the research landed on:

> Sparkle is the designed charge in a consequential procedure: rules and power become possible to feel and understand, people gain meaningful room to participate, question, or act, and the encounter leaves behind civic capability rather than a decorative veneer.

Findings I want to keep regardless of where the framing goes: affect is broader than delight (relief, dignity, moral discomfort, certainty, empathy, productive disagreement all count); status and acknowledgement are affective infrastructure; sparkle may *remove* a ritual rather than decorate it; technology often works best behind a trusted person, not as the sole civic interface; and the clearest warning pattern is **affective legitimacy without recourse** — a friendly process that leaves power untouched and unchallengeable.

The research also left [seven questions it says only I can answer](#doc-research-longlist-website-selection) — whether emerging technology is part of Sparkle's definition or just SB's current arena, whether discomfort and grief can count as sparkle, whether power must be transferred or only made legible, and so on.

---

#### Second thoughts (31 July)

After sitting with all of this for a week, I'm not comfortable taking this formulation forward as a movement/project/network. I can't fully articulate why yet — that articulation is the next piece of work. But the discomfort is real enough that I don't want to build the site on top of it as-is.

So today I sat down with Hannah to go a level deeper than mission statements: what do I actually care about, and what kind of impact do I want to have? We put together some ideas — recorded in [What I actually care about](#doc-2026-07-31-what-i-care-about). The weekend reflection that explains the discomfort itself is in [The resistance is information](#doc-2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information).

**Status:** the site restructure is paused until the mission question is resolved. The research stands either way — the longlist doesn't stop being useful because the frame is shifting, and the seven founder questions are probably part of *why* the frame is shifting.

---

<a id="doc-2026-07-31-what-i-care-about"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/2026-07-31-what-i-care-about.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Essays & captures

### What I actually care about

*Diary entry, 31 July 2026 — an hour with Hannah, then a follow-on chat with Peyman. Continues from [The resistance is information](#doc-2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information).*

I brought Hannah the whole tangle: how to *own* Sparkle Bureaucracy in a way that feels legitimate and exciting rather than scary and overwhelming. What we found: there isn't one thing I care about — there are several, and Ed found a 50,000-feet way of encapsulating them. But that encapsulation isn't in the format I'd want, because it has **no clear problem statement**. So we built one.

#### Three registers of caring

- **The fun thing:** playful games that encourage you to think about how the state runs — the birthday border control, the cohort reunion, the pirate ship.
- **The temporary opportunity:** political and civic groups have an AI upskilling gap, especially around agents — and it's closing on someone else's terms if we don't act. CHEW Festival / Project Mirror and Campaign Factory live here.
- **The long-term goal / career:** gov tech.

These don't need collapsing into one thing. The fun thing is the *method*, the opportunity is the *audience and moment*, gov tech is the *horizon*.

#### A problem statement, finally

We are a network of people using AI to explore optimistic futures.

1. **The state is failing in many countries.**
2. **AI offers an opportunity to fix this.**
3. **We as technologists need to make sure people working at the grassroots are not left behind.**
4. **We can use AI to prototype and imagine a different future.**

Candidate flagship projects: reimagining grant-making · Campaign Factory · a game.

#### A rhythm (determined by the community?)

- **Event 1 — hackathon:** an agents hackathon to prototype a different future, with suggested topics/challenges.
- **Event 2 — demo night:** projects from the hackathon plus anyone else who has one.
- **2× socials / game nights a year:** build an experience — Xmas, Summer.
- **Hear from experts:** a particular domain each time — Andreas, others; new things come out, people teach them.
- **Problem discovery (?):** bring in people *with problems* — not technologists — cross-pollinate, coffee chats.
- **An organiser** to hold the rhythm.

#### Scope

**In:** redesigning refugee services · mapping all the community centres in London · election disinformation · gov tech · Alex Vince · Tech for Palestine · Together Against Hate · trade unions · against authoritarianism.

**Out:** working for a political party or one side winning an election · Reform watch.

#### The two questions

The whole upskilling thread reduces to a pair of questions anyone can ask: **"What can I use AI for?"** and **"What can AI do for me?"**

#### Peyman's nudge: a tangible project

Afterwards I tried to explain to Peyman where the prototype stands, and how I'm struggling to articulate the movement without a tangible project. His idea: if the thing I care about is closing the gap between people who use AI and people who don't know how — build something like **howcaniuseai.com**: it takes information about your tasks and suggests automations. My variant: **an agent that tracks your RescueTime-equivalent, studies your patterns, and suggests tools, workflows, skills, and plugins** — an upskilling diagnostic that meets people inside their actual work.

That would be an artifact between me and the audience — the thing the [Sunday reflection](#doc-2026-07-26-the-resistance-is-information) said I needed. Judge the thing I made.

---

*Status update to [the network-formation entry](#doc-2026-07-31-network-formation-and-mission-rethink): the mission question now has a working problem statement. The site restructure can restart from it — problem first, not brand first.*

<a id="doc-2026-08-06-the-story-of-sb-video-intro-draft"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/2026-08-06-the-story-of-sb-video-intro-draft.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Essays & captures

### The story of SB — video intro draft (raw voice capture)

*Captured 2026-08-06 from Fatima's draft; spelling corrected 2026-08-06 at her instruction, phrasing untouched — this is the tone, language, and style reference for the prototype submission. The polished, timed cut lives in [`../submission/outline.md`](#part-i) (Part 1).*

---

#### The speech

As AI continues to improve and permeate through everyday lives, I find myself watching the gap between the people that have AI access and those that don't increase - and that worries me. Growing up in the US as the child of immigrants, I've seen what happens to skilled workers from other countries. I've seen the neuroscientists and engineers from abroad work at gas stations or drive taxis to get their children educated in America and get them better opportunities for success - all because their degrees weren't legible to the system.

I believe AI is going to make immigrants out of us all. I believe it's going to widen this gap, make it more and more expensive to access, to learn, and to teach. Sparkle Bureaucracy, as you heard earlier this year, was originally about a network of people using AI to explore optimistic organisational and service futures - because SB is my life. It's working in and outside of different government institutions, it's volunteering at hack nights across the US from Boston, to New York City, to Toronto, Ottawa, and London (the one in Ontario), to understanding that access to good civic services digitally is so crucial in democracy. So that's where the name comes from - running experiments that illustrate the power of technology and how these emerging innovations can help us envision the possibilities in govtech.

But SB evolved over the course of this fellowship. I came into thinking that I wanted to run experiments on things like DMVs - what does a better car licence renewal process look like? what does liquid democracy look like if the election results are being livestreamed while you can still change your vote? Running an immigration scheme at my birthday and giving people different types of passports and visa types and credentials - what sort of conversations did that spark?

And the experimentation went beyond bureaucratic processes - I ran the Cohort Reunion as a spy game - in order to circumvent the fact that nobody in my cohort really wanted to answer the question of "how is your prototype going?" so would there be a way I could get these two groups of people to interact with each other in a way that was "Sparkly" and fun and engaging so they could get to know each other's expertise, projects, and socialize? It was a resounding success because competition and games help lower the bar to social interactions.

I started hosting clawclubs in response to the sudden influx of the technology in the beginning of this year - and because I had been using and testing it in ways that were exciting and I wanted a community with whom I could share ideas, learn what they were building, and be excited. It was also a success - at times lightly attended 10-12 people, other times we had up to 60 people in the hall, all building and sharing, or even selling (lol) their hardware for open claw.

But I found myself reflecting on the prototype. While I love both these communities - the one that cares about tech for good, the one that cares about emerging technologies and building like open claw, and many of the communities I met in the Hall throughout this fellowship - the RightsCon folks, the Protocol for Publishers, the vTaiwan delegation, the Technologist Journalists, etc (we can pull some more from the events list at newspeak.house), I found that they helped me identify, collect, and narrow the characteristics of the community that I wanted to build.

In parallel, the fellowship and the prototype were also about building confidence. I had to grapple with the questions of why me? (in that why am I the person to do this?) and why now? (as why is this the time that yet another community/network must be built) and why not the others (as in there are so many great networks in London doing things around AI so what makes mine so special or me so unique that I must start a new thing). I have yet been able to answer this question to a degree of confidence, but I've learned that perhaps for now - since Hannah O'Rourke says that legitimacy is constructed - I must borrow the answers of those around me who have reflected to me that the overlap of sparkle x bureaucracy is unique, that I _am_ and seem to be an expert in AI when I speak about agents at events.

The thing that I can say with some confidence is that now is the window of opportunity to close the gap. As models become sufficiently more advanced, and we move our agents to the cloud, operate them remotely, and compute starts to become cheaper (in the long run), this gap will inevitably widen and most of the population will use AI tools that they don't even understand. I would like them to understand what they're using and I would like certain communities, those aligned with civic tech, tech for good, open source, gov tech, to be prepared for what's coming and how they can use these tools to continue to build new possibilities for themselves.

It's why I was so excited to be part of the AI & Campaigning conference. While campaigners weren't "my people" per se, working with Hannah on the contextualization of their problems and building them Campaign Factory - helped me realize how important it is to show people the power of these tools. Someone I met told me that Campaign Factory built for her what it would take her 5 months to research.

Similarly, my keynote at the Charity Evaluators Festival 2026 on Evaluation 2030 focused on how to shift your thinking from being prompt based to systems based in lieu of agents coming soon to you. Before evaluators are presented with vendors asking them to purchase things, I'd like them to understand how to articulate their work into a system and then plug agents into that system. There were like 15 people lined up to tell me their specific stories, how they work, and ask advice for where they could implement agents.

In the moment, these engagements perhaps didn't fit into a single mandate, but reflecting back on this past year, I think they were all pieces of this puzzle (and there's more of them in the pivotal moments that we can fit into this story and/or into the writeup).

In building all these projects, engaging with all these people, and considering these implications of this technology (through things like our AI safety study group where we read and discussed various materials on AI safety and invited people from neighboring institutions to join us), I believe I have a clearer vision for what SB is.

However, I don't know if "sparkle" "bureaucracy" is the right name. It used to be about making bureaucracy more sparkly, and I do believe that's my long-term life's mission, but as long as this window of gap is open for AI, I want to be here. I want to be someone who understands the technology sufficiently enough, who tests the latest technology, and who can build the bridge from the tech to the people that I'm speaking to in order to translate what that technology does, what it means for their work, and how they could utilize it to amplify themselves.

But I don't yet know what the new name of this network will be, so for this prototype, I present to you the accumulation of experiments, projects, and ideas that led me here and the roadmap towards what's next.

---

#### Roadmap seeds (same session)

In that roadmap, we can add things like an AI agents for good hackathon sometime in Aug/Sept, a brainstorming session with selected faculty on what Sparkle BB's flagship project will be - Peyman's idea of "what can AI do for me?" is an interesting one where maybe an agent shadows you for work, RescueTime-style, and identifies places for efficiency.

And running some sort of ongoing community, either with events or social experiment type events.

<a id="doc-awards-showcase-script"></a>

> **Source:** supplied by Fatima, 7 August 2026 — her full script for the Political Technology Awards showcase (end of March 2026) · group: Essays & captures

### Political Technology Awards — Fatima's Script
*Slide markers in [brackets]. Directions in italics. Placeholders in CAPS.*

---

#### 7:00–7:05 | WELCOME & THE BRIEF (Fatima — 5 min)

**[Slide: Title — Political Technology Awards, Cohort 25/26]**

Hello everyone, welcome. We are so excited to have you here for the Political Technology Awards Showcase — thank you so much for joining us this evening.

We're really excited to share the work that we've been doing as a committee, and also to share with you the winner of the awards. We also have a little surprise planned for you at the midpoint, and we'll have some food at eight o'clock.

We're going to walk you through the process today — how we approached the problem, what we built, and what we learned along the way.

**[Slide: The Challenge — 321 projects, 1 winner, open process]**

So everything starts here with the project brief.

We were given a list of 321 political technology projects, and our task was to evaluate them and select a winner. And because we're political technologists who care about openness and transparency, we're not just selecting a winner — we're also making our entire evaluation process open and visible. Alongside our decision, we're publishing the algorithms we've used, putting them all in public so you can review them as well.

The Political Technology Awards is an open evaluation exercise run by the fellowship cohort. We're building a public, inspectable ranking of civic and political technology projects — the kind of tools that help citizens understand institutions, participate in democracy, and hold power to account.

Our evaluation process is iterative and transparent. We use a scoring algorithm that has evolved over time, and each version applies a different heuristic and produces a ranked list. The algorithm lives in a public GitHub repo — you can inspect the code, inspect our pull requests, and review the rationale for every change.

Rankings are political. Scoring is political. By making our process transparent and iterative, we hope to surface both strong projects and the trade-offs that are inherent in any evaluation framework. A simple tool that empowers a marginalised community might score higher than a technically impressive platform that reinforces existing power structures. Political technology evaluation is inherently political, because different values lead to different conclusions — and that's exactly how it should be. So tonight, you'll hear about the values we implemented and the conclusions we got to.

---

#### 7:05–7:15 | WARM-UP EXERCISE (Audience — 10 min)

**[Slide: How do you rank 321 political technology projects? — QR code]**

Now, before we show you what we did, we want to give you a moment to try this yourselves.

On your tables you'll see the long list of 321 projects. We'd like you to rank them however you feel makes sense — you can look them up, make a list of what you care about, rank based on that. And as you're doing this, start noting down how you're making that decision, because that'll come in handy when you hear how we made ours — and when we ask you for it later in the evening.

You've got ten minutes. It's just gone 7:05, so I'll see you back here at 7:15.

*[10-minute pen and paper exercise]*

---

#### 7:15–7:20 | THREE PILLARS (Fatima — 5 min)

**[Slide: Can AI meaningfully evaluate political technology projects?]**

Welcome back. I hope that was an interesting exercise — I'm really looking forward to hearing how you approached it.

So, the question we're holding throughout tonight is: *can AI meaningfully evaluate political technology projects?*

When we started doing this, we tried a lot of different approaches — some more manual, based on human judgment, some more structured and experimental using AI. But something we kept coming back to is that every system we built had three core components.

**[Slide: Three Pillars — Data / Values / Facilitation]**

The first is **data** — what do we actually know about these projects, and how do we assess and verify that it's accurate? In our case, we had only a list of URLs, and we had to think about how to collect data in a way that feels meaningful, can be verified, and doesn't introduce bias. Because data has trade-offs: some projects have the capacity to provide more data, others can't or don't — and how do we measure those fairly without just rewarding whoever shows up better digitally?

The second pillar is **values** — political technology evaluation is inherently political because different values lead to different conclusions. Articulating those values, writing them down, agreeing on a set — that's important work, and harder than it sounds.

And the third is **facilitation** — how do we actually apply those values to the data and make decisions about scoring? This is the lens we're going to use to walk you through everything we built.

The question we're holding is: can AI meaningfully evaluate political technology projects? My answer is: maybe. What I'd like to do is show you all of the attempts at solving this problem, the different systems we built, and what happened when we tried to operationalise each one. And then maybe you can answer alongside us.

---

#### 7:20–7:55 | TIMELINE OF ITERATIONS (35 min)

##### V1–V4: What are we evaluating? (Fatima — 5 min)

**[Slide: V1 — Random scoring / V2 — Exclusion keyword bonus / V3 — Keyword clusters / V4 — AI governance body bonus]**

So let's start at the beginning — versions one to four.

We started in a very intuitive place. We had URLs, scraped content, keyword-based approaches, some human-based scoring. And we kept running into the same limitation: a URL, or the scraped content of a page — which sometimes you can get reliably and sometimes you really can't — doesn't actually tell you enough about a project. It doesn't tell you its impact, its context, or how it should be compared to anything else. So our first realisation was: the data we have is not enough.

We started building out dossiers for each project — which meant deciding on a taxonomy. We pulled together the civic tech field guide taxonomy and collected those fields for every project. We brought in databases like OpenAlex for academic citations and ProPublica for nonprofit financials. We ran a verification pass across everything. And even then, when we looked through the dossiers manually, we found issues — overlapping data, unrelated citations. But it was significantly more data than we'd started with, and that was a real step forward.

---

##### V5–V7: Articulating values (Davit via video — 5 min, then Gamithra — 10 min)

**[Slide: V5 — Three-agent ITN/A deliberation / V6 — Six-jury ITN/A deliberation]**

So if the data isn't enough on its own, the next question is: what is it that we're actually trying to measure? And this is where we had to start articulating our values.

To talk about how he took his own lens and turned it into a structured taxonomy for scoring, I'm going to hand over to Davit — he wasn't able to join us tonight but has recorded a video.

---

**[DAVIT VIDEO — 5 min]**

---

Once you have values articulated, the challenge is finding a way to apply them at scale — and for that I'm going to hand over to Gamithra, who built a framework that reflected her values and let her systemise the scoring. She'll talk for around 10 minutes on the ITN/A framework and the multi-jury AI system she built.

---

**[GAMITHRA — 10 min]**

---

##### V8–V10: Decision systems (Alexandra — 5 min, then Jamie — 5 min)

**[Slide: Evaluation Frameworks — Heterogenous criteria aggregation vs. Shared criteria]**

So now we have data and values — but we still need a way to make decisions. Do we define one set of criteria and have multiple judges apply it? Or do we let everyone bring their own value system and aggregate? These produce very different outcomes — one more consistent, one more pluralistic.

To talk about what those systems look like in practice and the trade-offs you encounter, I'm going to hand over to Alexandra — five minutes on consensus versus aggregation.

---

**[ALEXANDRA — 5 min]**

---

After this we made a bit of a leap. If we want to allow multiple value systems and aggregate them — a question emerges: what if the judges *are* the system? Before we can show you how we built that, I want to hand over to Jamie to lay the groundwork on synthetic users — what they are, how they can be useful, and how you measure whether they work.

---

**[JAMIE — 5 min]**

---

*We now have agents, data, and scoring systems.*

---

#### 7:55–8:00 | PEOPLE'S CHOICE AWARD (Fran via video — 5 min)

Before we show you what happened when we ran the full system, we have a challenge for you. We've built a People's Choice Award system — you'll submit your own criteria and an agent will generate a ranking of the shortlisted projects based on your values. To explain how it works, here's committee member Fran.

---

**[FRAN VIDEO — 5 min]**

---

**[Slide: QR code — form closes at HH:MM]**

The QR is on screen now. Please fill in the form before we come back from the break — that gives us enough time to run the analysis before we announce. And if the shortlist posters are up around the room, take a look while you're up.

---

#### 8:00–8:20 | BREAK (20 min)

**[Slide: Break]**

*[People's Choice form open during break. QR stays on screen.]*

---

#### 8:20–8:50 | SYNTHETIC SYSTEMS & FINAL RESULTS (30 min)

##### Project Mirror (Fatima — 5 min)

**[Slide: Project Mirror — agents, inferred values, rankings and reasoning]**

Welcome back. I hope you enjoyed the pizza.

So at this point we have a verified dataset, a dossier on all of the projects, a set of values we've been working with, multiple scoring approaches — and, as Jamie set up for us, agents.

What I built at this point is something we're now calling Project Mirror.

In my ideal world, all 18 members of the cohort would have been involved with the awards committee. I would have sat with all of them, gotten their values, and we would have ranked these projects together. But a system like that doesn't scale. And so — as a very tech person — I went and built an agent for each of them.

Project Mirror is an evaluator-estimator workflow. It estimates how individual cohort members might evaluate the 321 projects, based on each person's public record and bio. This was a research prototype — it doesn't claim to reconstruct people's actual beliefs or simulate cognition. It takes public records and tries to turn them into a ranking and scoring system.

The research question I really cared about was: can AI infer a usable evaluative constitution for a person from their public record of work? And if so, which project rankings stay stable when the decision procedure varies — across inference, scoring, aggregation, and deliberation? What does this reveal about AI systems as political and evaluative tools?

The pipeline worked in stages. A swarm of ten agents went through researching people's public profiles, verifying them, collecting evidence, and building a constitution for each cohort agent. The constitution included not just scoring criteria, but value modifiers — things that would boost or reduce a score based on inferred preferences. Things like community ownership or governance getting a boost, under-resourced civic contexts getting a boost, inclusive developer communities getting a boost. And things like VC funding getting a reduction — because the agent inferred that the person it was modelling would not look favourably on that. There was also a popularity discount, to counterbalance the fact that well-known projects exist heavily in LLM training data and might score higher simply because the model "knows" them better.

After all of that, we ended up with 18 different agents, each with a different constitution, each producing a different scored ranking — all with rationale for every score.

To give you some insight into what it actually feels like to have an AI agent built for you based on your public record, I'd like to invite committee member Asil — she has some reflections on looking at AI infer your values, and what that means for how we think about these tools.

---

**[ASIL — 5 min]**

---

##### Final Deliberation + Results (Gamithra — 5 min, then Fatima — 5 min)

**[Slide: v11 / aggregation, pairwise, dashboard — gamithra.com/awards/ranking]**

So — what actually happened when we ran the full system through deliberation? We aggregated scores across all the agents using different methods — pairwise comparison, averaging — to see which projects performed consistently across all of these different value systems.

To walk you through versions 10 and 11, where Gamithra mapped the scores from all the agents to find the answer, I'll hand back over to her.

---

**[GAMITHRA — 5 min]**

---

**[Slide: v14–v15 — outcomes, data plots, social choice]**

And from version 14 to 15 — let me tell you about the last thing I tried.

I had 18 agents with constitutions, and I kept coming back to a question: what if these agents sat together as a grand jury and actually had a discussion? What would they decide?

We've been learning about social choice theory here in the fellowship, and I thought it would be interesting to run the agent committee through a deliberation using those rules. I took all 16 of the synthetic agents, had them rank 38 candidates under Borda count — where each agent applies a different mathematical rule to the same preference data. Given that each agent knows its honest standings and can reason strategically, what would the committee choose?

Borda count is useful to test with because it's the most standard social choice rule for ranked preferences, and theoretically the most susceptible to manipulation. If an honest result is stable under Borda with strategic reasoning, it's likely stable under anything.

And the winner of the social choice round — unchanged from honest voting, with every agent independently concluding that the 18-point lead was too large to overcome — was **Liquid Feedback**. There were 13 arguments in deliberation. No revisions. No strategic manipulation that held. And that says something about the agent committee: across constitutions built from very different people's values, no one could find grounds to overturn it.

---

##### Winners (Fatima — 5 min)

**[Slide: And the winner is…]**

So — I've slightly spoiled it — but the winner of the cohort awards is **Liquid Feedback**.

*[ADD: short description of Liquid Feedback from the website before the event]*

And the one you've all been waiting for — the People's Choice winner, which Hoda has been running in the background for us: **[PEOPLE'S CHOICE WINNER — live on the night]**.

---

##### Closing Reflections (Fatima — 5 min)

What this shows us is not that AI can definitively evaluate projects. But that we now have tools that can help us articulate values, apply them at scale, and explore how different systems produce different outcomes.

All of this still depends on the quality of your data, how clearly you define your values, and ultimately how you design your system. Those are all political decisions, made all the way through.

I want to end with this.

*[Show image of screen with 18 agents running in parallel]*

This is a picture of my screen at home, running all 18 agents at the same time — all of them ranking, scoring, running through the research. And I remember feeling so completely delighted, because all 18 of my cohort were with me on one screen, working on this project together.

It's a little bittersweet, because these are synthetic representations. But I think where we are is the cusp of something. If I was able to design a system where synthetic versions of the cohort could work with me — then maybe the next step is not just synthetic versions of us, but us managing those versions. Technology that keeps us in the judgment loop and lets us work together better. And that, to me, is really exciting.

The story of this project is: with good data, clearly articulated values, and a system for applying them, you can score 321 political technology projects. But each of those pieces introduces trade-offs — around data quality, value definition, and evaluation design. And when you're using emerging technology, knowing that these tools can help us make changes in really big ways is the thing worth taking home tonight.

Thank you.

---

*[Reflections / Q&A / close]*

---

A working research log from Fatima's fellowship at Newspeak House (Cohort 25/26).

Structure and flow in progress.

---

#### What is Sparkle Bureaucracy

Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network of people prototyping optimistic organisational futures for the age of AI.

The idea is pretty simple: what if the systems we're stuck with — the forms, the queues, the approvals, the checkpoints — didn't have to feel hostile? What if you could keep all the structure and change what it feels like to go through it? Same rituals, different intent.

I'm not trying to fix policy. I'm trying to run experiments that show what's possible and leave something behind — a pattern, a prototype, an artifact that gets hardened into something useful inside the systems it touches. The outputs are meant to illustrate, not prescribe.

The name matters: "bureaucracy" carries weight — it signals that this is serious, not just playful. "Sparkle" signals that it doesn't have to be miserable. That's the whole bet.

What makes it different from other things in this space: **no prescribed outcome**. I don't arrive with an answer. I show up with curiosity, run the experiment, publish what happened, and see what crystallises.

---

#### Why I care about this

AI reminds me of what happened when immigrants came to the US and took low-paid jobs — taxi drivers, gas stations — despite being physicists and doctors whose degrees didn't hold up in a new country. That displacement of skill and dignity is what AI is going to do to people, at scale, quickly.

I care about the environment and water. I care about these things. And I also know how fast this is moving in industry. The options feel like:

- **(a)** The math argument — model inference uses a measurable amount of water and energy; the comparison to prompt-level costs matters
- **(b)** The values argument — giving access to marginalised communities and giving them the power to use AI to improve services and systems
- **(c)** The honest acknowledgment — this is an unsolved issue and having a moral high ground won't stop AI from moving forward

I'm not the enemy of the communities most at risk from this technology. I have a technical gift and I care about the people for whom this doesn't come easy. That's the whole point.

---

#### What it can look like

An **experiment lab** — committing to regular experiments in clearly defined theory-of-change spaces, with a small constellation of people who are inspired by the same questions.

The experiments highlight **systems** where the friction is highest and the stakes are real:
- **DMV** — identity, queuing, classification, waiting
- **Digital identity** — verification rituals, trust, documentation
- **Synthetic voice in participatory channels** — representatives getting feedback from constituents; a system already easy to spoof by good and bad actors, needing to be hardened for a world of synthetic voice. "We have to do it anyway. Let's take joy in it."
- **Elections**
- Social Credit System
Societal systems in scope include but aren't limited to government. The credit system. Social credit systems. **Social gratitude systems** — where you build and populate the market at the same time.

##### Upcoming experiments

- **DMV** — identity, classification, queuing as ritual
- **Digital identity** — verification, trust, documentation redesigned
- **Synthetic voice + participatory channels** — hardening democratic feedback systems for the AI era

---

#### Theories of change

There are a few working models — not exclusive, potentially combinable:

**1. Event series as contagion**
Cutting-edge relevant topics, a fun and whimsical way of going about it, honest curiosity, a small set of interested parties. The network gets infected by the idea through repeated gatherings.

**2. Experiment lab as commitment**
Regular experiments with clearly stated theories of change per experiment: *how does doing X create result Y, and why does it matter?* Publishing results. Building evidence over time.

**3. Insiders as actors**
There are people inside these systems who would like them to be better and are demotivated. Enlisting them as actors on the inside — starting small — is its own theory of change.

**4. Artifacts crystallising into gems**
The prototype isn't the output. The output is what it leaves behind in an institution — a process, a pattern, a person who got infected and carries it forward.

One framing that feels right: **the theory of change is inspiration itself**. When you do the experiments with rigour and openness — with no prescribed outcome — and publish them honestly, you attract the people who want to make things better and give them something to hold.

---

#### How it gains legitimacy

- **Collaborations and alliances** — not everything built alone; portfolio includes others' work
- **Publishing results** — field notes, experiment writeups, pattern documentation
- **An amazing network** — develop it deliberately; attract a small set of deeply interested people
- **Faculty experiment design + scope** — [Faculty.ai](https://faculty.ai) as a structured collaborator for experiment design in the public-sector AI space
- **Keynotes and demos** — show the work, tell the story, make it discussable

Knight Foundation has funded broader theories of change in this space. Better-funded theories of knowledge exist and are worth building on.

---

#### What I've built so far

| Work | What it demonstrates |
|------|----------------------|
| **Sparkle Border Authority** | End-to-end bureaucratic ritual as live software; artifacts, checkpoints, admin overrides; sparkle bureaucracy as implemented pattern. |
| **Project Mirror** | Multi-agent evaluation design; constitutions + modifiers + aggregation; inferring evaluative stance; social choice deliberation under Borda count. |
| **PoliTech Awards showcase** | Open evaluation as public performance; V1–V15 methodology; [2025.newspeak.house/awards](https://2025.newspeak.house/awards). |
| **Lumina House × Ration Club** | Cross-community collaboration; social choreography at scale. |
| **Claw agents** (Moltbook, penpals, research pipelines) | Playful multi-agent systems with real outputs; range from whimsy to instrumented research. |
| **Clawclub / Clawcon** | Community building, recurring formats, convening. |
| **Community × governance writing (unpublished)** | Sustained thinking on legitimacy, permission, and shadow governance. |

---

#### For the prototype check-in — experiments to get involved with

These are the experiments currently in scope. For each one, I've noted what kind of input, collaboration, or care would actually be useful. If any of these land for you — as a research interest, a lived experience, a relevant project you know about, or something you'd want to work on — I'd love to hear it.

---

**DMV / queuing and classification rituals**
Government service encounters at their most frustrating — waiting, classification, gating, identity verification. The experiment question: can you redesign the *felt experience* of this kind of encounter without touching the underlying policy? What does a sparkle bureaucracy version of a DMV interaction look like?
*Useful input: anyone who has thought about service design in public sector contexts, experience with government digital transformation, or strong opinions about queuing.*

---

**Digital identity**
Verification rituals, trust documents, what it means to prove you are who you say you are — and who gets left out when those systems are designed without them in mind. This connects to the synthetic voice problem: if identity is already hard to verify, what happens when AI makes spoofing trivially easy?
*Useful input: anyone working on digital identity, trust infrastructure, access and exclusion in verification systems, or the ethics of proof.*

---

**Synthetic voice in participatory channels**
Representatives get feedback from constituents. That feedback is increasingly easy to flood, spoof, or manufacture — by good actors and bad ones. The system needs to be hardened for a world of synthetic voice. The Sparkle Bureaucracy angle: we have to do this anyway. Let's take joy in designing it well.
*Useful input: anyone working on democratic participation, consultation processes, deliberation, AI safety in civic contexts, or has thought about what it means to hear "the people" when the people include bots.*

---

**Experiments you'd want to run**
If you have a bureaucratic system you find maddening, fascinating, or ripe for redesign — or a community you think this kind of work should be *for* — I want to know. The experiment lab model only works if the experiments are chosen well.

---

- **Website + mailing list:** launching soon

### Prior Art Log

This log tracks tools, movements, research, and precedents that help test whether Sparkle Bureaucracy is original, non-obsolete, and methodologically sound.

Rows marked "Needs follow-up" should be verified, expanded, or discussed with the librarian / faculty.

| Project / URL | What it does | What I am learning from it | How Sparkle Bureaucracy differs | Status |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| [vTaiwan](https://vtaiwan.tw/) + [pol.is](https://pol.is/) | Participatory governance process associated with a specific deliberation tool. | A movement can gain legitimacy through repeated practice and a recognisable tool. | Sparkle Bureaucracy may need either a canonical tool or a portfolio of rituals; this is the "movement + associated tool" precedent. | Active precedent |
| [LiquidFeedback](https://liquidfeedback.org/) | Liquid democracy software for delegated voting and structured decision-making. | Delegation, revocation, and voting can be operationalised as civic infrastructure. | A Sparkle Bureaucracy version would focus on the felt ritual and trust comparison between paper, digital, and delegation, not only the software. | Active precedent |
| [Creative Bureaucracy Festival](https://creativebureaucracy.org/) | Community and festival around public-sector imagination and bureaucratic innovation. | There is an existing audience for optimistic bureaucratic reform. | Sparkle Bureaucracy is more explicitly AI-native, experimental, and prototype-led. | Active precedent |
| [OneTeamGov](https://oneteamgov.uk/) | Public-sector reform community focused on practical action. | Useful adjacent community and reform energy. | Sparkle Bureaucracy uses more theatrical, AI-native, and artifact-based experiments. | Active precedent |
| [Civic Tech Field Guide](https://civictech.guide/) | Directory and taxonomy of civic technology projects. | Helps classify the project and test novelty. | Sparkle Bureaucracy should become legible enough to be added as a civic tech pattern or project. | Needs librarian check |
| [Project Mirror](https://2025.newspeak.house/awards) | Internal precedent: synthetic evaluator constitutions and multi-agent project ranking. | Shows that values can be made explicit and tested across aggregation methods. | Future work should move from estimating people to letting people manage, correct, or contest their own synthetic evaluative agents. | Built proof point |
| [Sparkle Border Authority](https://sparkle-border-authority.vercel.app/) | Internal precedent: live border-crossing ritual for an event. | Shows that bureaucratic skeleton and emotional intent can be separated. | Future work should test transfer to less theatrical and more civic/public-service contexts. | Built proof point |
| OpenClaw / Hermes-style agents | Agentic systems for ongoing research, collaboration, or action selection. | Useful precedent for the proposed diary / collaborator scout agent. | Sparkle Bureaucracy should constrain agents to drafting, triage, and suggestion unless explicit human approval is added. | Needs source link |
| Matrix-to-blog / public field notes workflows | Publishing infrastructure for turning private notes into public research artifacts. | Publishing can be part of the research method, not only dissemination. | This project treats the diary infrastructure itself as an experiment in civic research legibility. | Needs implementation |
| Public consultation and constituent feedback tools | Existing processes for gathering civic input from residents or constituents. | The synthetic voice experiment needs to understand failure modes in real feedback channels. | Sparkle Bureaucracy would focus on legibility, uncertainty, verification ritual, and access trade-offs under synthetic voice conditions. | Needs follow-up |
| Digital identity and verification tools | Systems for proving identity, eligibility, or credentials. | Important prior art for any identity-focused prototype. | A Sparkle Bureaucracy version would foreground exclusion, discomfort, and ritual rather than only verification success. | Needs follow-up |
| [PipResist](https://pipresist.uk/) — Tyrone, Campaign Lab | Voice notes turned into government consultation responses. | Lowering the barrier to civic voice is a live, adjacent build — and a natural companion to consultation-channel redesign. | SB's synthetic-voice work (Public Voice Customs) focuses on the receiving side: making volume, substance, and uncertainty legible without authenticity policing. | Pointed to by Hannah at the Previews (2 Apr 2026) |

---

### Reading list — books read because of Sparkle Bureaucracy

*Started as a stub 31 July 2026; filled 7 August 2026 with the books people handed me during the fellowship. Honest status: a few chapters into each so far — inspiration doesn't require completion. For each: who suggested it, and what it changed (blurbs TBD, Fatima).*

#### Handed to me during the fellowship

- ***The Utopia of Rules: On Technology, Stupidity, and the Secret Joys of Bureaucracy*** — David Graeber. From Anouk Ruhaak. The canonical SB text by title alone. — *what it changed: TBD*
- ***Radical Help*** — Hilary Cottam. From Chris Owen (cohort). — *what it changed: TBD*
- ***Incorruptible*** — Eric Ries. From Theodore Keloglou; also the house Book Club's pick on 16 June. — *what it changed: TBD*
- ***Who Thought This Was a Good Idea?*** — Alyssa Mastromonaco. From Hannah. — *what it changed: TBD*
- ***The Employees*** — Olga Ravn. From Six Silberman. — *what it changed: TBD*
- ***The Majority Myth: How Voting Really Works and Why Elections Must Change*** — Edmund Lewis Maklouf. Met the author at the book launch at the house; talking about SB, he pointed me toward reading on the immigrant mindset — what the literature calls the **model minority myth**: the belief that doing good and following the rules gets you noticed and rewarded. It made me examine my true motivations for this project. — *what it changed: that question.*

#### Earlier seeds from the field notes (to confirm)

*Seeing Like a State* · *Reinventing Organizations* · *Anarchic Agreements* · Jo Freeman, "The Tyranny of Structurelessness" — confirm which were actually read and add what they changed.
