### Technical Notes

This file records architecture, data, privacy, and implementation notes for the existing prototypes and proposed diary infrastructure.

---

#### Existing Prototype: Sparkle Border Authority

Source summary from [research-inventory.md](#doc-research-inventory).

##### Stack

- React.
- TypeScript.
- Vite.
- Browser storage for local state continuity.

##### Core Flow

1. Guest enters a 4-character code.
2. System confirms identity.
3. Guest completes purpose / declaration forms.
4. Screening logic produces a decision or secondary review path.
5. System assigns visa class and privileges.
6. A6 visa sticker can be printed.
7. Checkpoint handoff and arrival tracking complete the ritual.

##### Operational Surfaces

- Applicant flow.
- Manual visa creation.
- Visitor signup.
- Checkpoint assistance.
- Admin overrides.
- Reprints.
- Live stats dashboard.

##### Technical Notes To Add

- [ ] Link to source repo if public.
- [ ] Add screenshots.
- [ ] Add data model summary.
- [ ] Add print flow notes.
- [ ] Add edge cases encountered during the live event.

---

#### Existing Prototype: Project Mirror

Source summary from [research-inventory.md](#doc-research-inventory) and [synthesis.md](#doc-synthesis).

##### Core Flow

1. Research public profiles / records for cohort members.
2. Build an inferred evaluator constitution for each person.
3. Score 321 political technology projects.
4. Produce ranked outputs with rationale.
5. Aggregate rankings.
6. Run social-choice deliberation under Borda count with strategic reasoning.

##### Key Design Choices

- Constitutions describe evaluative stance, not claimed beliefs.
- Value modifiers make normative assumptions inspectable.
- Popularity discount attempts to counterbalance LLM familiarity bias.
- Aggregation method variation is treated as evidence, not noise.

##### Technical Notes To Add

- [ ] Link to methodology/code if public.
- [ ] Add constitution schema.
- [ ] Add aggregation method notes.
- [ ] Add failure cases.
- [ ] Add ethical guardrails around inferred representation.

---

#### Proposed Prototype Infrastructure: Agent-Assisted Diary

##### Goal

Use an agent to help maintain a template-compliant prototype diary by drafting structured artifacts from raw notes, while keeping publication and interpretation under human control.

##### Inputs

- Matrix field notes export.
- GitHub issues or comments.
- Faculty feedback.
- Links and prior-art notes.
- Event notes.
- Prototype screenshots.
- Manual reflections.

##### Outputs

- Weekly diary drafts.
- Experiment cards.
- Prior-art candidates.
- Engagement log updates.
- Faculty feedback prompts.
- Collaborator cards.
- Open questions.

##### Human Approval Boundary

The agent may draft, classify, summarise, and suggest. It should not publish, contact collaborators, represent someone else's views, or mark feedback resolved without human approval.

##### Data Model Sketch

```ts
type DiaryNote = {
  id: string;
  date: string;
  source: "matrix" | "github" | "conversation" | "event" | "manual";
  sourceUrl?: string;
  tag?: "link" | "journal" | "question" | "project" | "idea" | "field-note" | "engagement" | "prior-art" | "experiment";
  body: string;
  people?: string[];
  projects?: string[];
  experiments?: string[];
  privacy: "public" | "needs-review" | "private";
};

type ExperimentCard = {
  name: string;
  status: "idea" | "planned" | "in-progress" | "done";
  hypothesis: string;
  method: string;
  evidence: string[];
  risks: string[];
  facultyQuestions: string[];
  nextStep: string;
};

type FeedbackItem = {
  date: string;
  source: string;
  summary: string;
  response: string;
  status: "open" | "accepted" | "deferred" | "rejected";
};
```

##### Implementation Phases

| Phase | Description | Why |
| --- | --- | --- |
| V0 | Markdown-only manual workflow. | Keeps the diary moving and avoids overbuilding. |
| V1 | Script or agent prompt that classifies notes and drafts weekly entries. | Tests whether agent assistance helps. |
| V2 | GitHub issue / PR workflow for proposed diary updates. | Makes human approval explicit and auditable. |
| V3 | Optional website or dashboard for faculty-friendly review. | Useful only if markdown and Google Docs become too high-friction. |

##### Privacy and Safety Notes

- Preserve source links where possible.
- Do not publish private or sensitive notes by default.
- Treat third-party comments as requiring permission before direct quotation.
- Do not let inferred agents speak as real people.
- Label generated synthesis clearly.
- Keep raw notes separate from polished reflection.
- Keep a review step before any public update.

---

#### Open Technical Questions

- Should the diary agent run locally, in GitHub Actions, or as a separate service?
- What is the lowest-friction way for faculty to comment: GitHub, Google Docs, a form, or a lightweight website?
- Should Matrix remain the capture surface, or should new notes be captured in GitHub issues?
- How much of the agent workflow should be reproducible from committed source files?
- What evidence should be attached to weekly entries versus left in supporting logs?

> **Note (7 August 2026):** the assessed submission is [`../submission/outline.md`](#part-i) — formatted as the story rather than as this template, a deliberate choice because Sparkle Bureaucracy is a network/experiment lab rather than a single tool (the outline includes a template crosswalk). This file remains as the template-shaped working diary; its summary sections were written in June and reflect the pre-rethink framing — see the July essays and the outline for how the mission evolved.

---

#### 1. Project Summary (Living Abstract)

##### One-sentence summary

Sparkle Bureaucracy is an experiment lab for prototyping optimistic organisational futures for the age of AI, especially where civic, public-sector, and community systems rely on forms, queues, checkpoints, evaluations, and other bureaucratic rituals.

##### Political / civic community

Initial communities:
- Newspeak House fellows, faculty, and adjacent civic technology networks.
- Public-sector AI, civic tech, service design, creative bureaucracy, and participatory democracy practitioners.
- Communities experimenting with AI-native coordination, evaluation, feedback, and institutional legitimacy.

Potential later communities:
- Local government teams and public-service designers.
- Community organisers running decision processes, events, or feedback channels.
- Civic technology projects that need transparent evaluation, community validation, or trust-building rituals.

##### Core user need / problem

People are being asked to trust increasingly automated institutional systems, but many of those systems feel opaque, hostile, extractive, or illegible. The project asks whether bureaucratic procedures can be redesigned as participatory, legible, and even delightful without pretending that the underlying power dynamics disappear.

##### Core hypothesis

If bureaucratic rituals are redesigned as explicit, playful, inspectable experiments, then civic communities can better understand, contest, and reshape AI-era organisational systems. The strongest outputs may not be "policy advice"; they may be reusable patterns, artifacts, rituals, and evidence that help institutions and communities imagine different ways of working.

##### Current version of the concept

The prototype is Sparkle Bureaucracy: an experiment lab. It currently has three connected layers:

1. The lab identity and public narrative: what Sparkle Bureaucracy is, why it matters, and what kind of work belongs inside it.
2. The experiment programme: a set of candidate experiments that can test specific claims about legitimacy, participation, synthetic voice, and bureaucratic experience.
3. The research infrastructure: field notes, feedback logs, experiment cards, and potentially an agent-assisted diary that helps turn scattered notes into publishable evidence.

Candidate experiments include agentic diary, faculty feedback loop, collaborator scout, synthetic voice channels, hybrid liquid democracy, digital identity, and DMV/queue rituals. |
| Website + mailing list | Idea | Mentioned in README; not yet specified. |

##### 2.2 Risks & Unknowns

| Risk / Unknown | Mitigation |
| --- | --- |
| The experiment lab could feel too broad to assess. | Treat Sparkle Bureaucracy itself as the prototype, but make the lab assessable through a clearly documented first experiment sequence with dates, methods, risks, and evidence. |
| The diary becomes a manifesto rather than evidence of adaptation. | Keep the README narrative, but use this file plus weekly entries, engagement logs, feedback logs, and experiment logs for assessment evidence. |
| Agent-assisted diary output could blur authentic voice and synthetic interpretation. | Treat agents as drafting and triage infrastructure only. Require human approval before publishing. Preserve source links and distinguish notes from generated synthesis. |
| Faculty feedback may be scattered across conversations and comments. | Track questions, feedback, and responses in [faculty-feedback.md](#doc-faculty-feedback). |
| Civic use evidence may remain informal. | Log each demo, conversation, event, testimonial, metric, quote, and design change in [engagement-log.md](#doc-engagement-log). |
| Playful bureaucracy could trivialise coercive or serious systems. | Add an ethics/risk section to each experiment card. Ask faculty where the line is for each candidate domain. |
| The technical infrastructure could become heavier than the research needs. | Start with markdown and exported Matrix notes. Build automation only when repeated manual work creates real friction. |
| Prior art and novelty claims may be underspecified. | Maintain [prior-art.md](#doc-prior-art) and identify how each experiment differs from previous civic tech, creative bureaucracy, and participatory democracy tools. |

- GitHub repo as the canonical public diary and documentation surface.
- Existing field-note Matrix export in [assets/](#doc-field-notes-transcript).
- Existing prototypes and evidence: Sparkle Border Authority, Project Mirror, PoliTech Awards showcase, Claw agents, Lumina House x Ration Club.
- Faculty and mentor feedback.
- Google Doc feedback surface generated from [faculty-feedback-brief.md](#doc-faculty-feedback-brief).
- Community demos, interviews, and workshop opportunities.
- Optional agent tooling for summarisation, classification, prior-art scouting, and collaborator discovery.

- [x] Original feature(s) described.
- [x] Substantial original prototypes already built.
- [ ] Prior art adaptation documented for the experiment lab and selected first experiments.

##### 10.4 Not Obsolete

- [ ] Meaningful difference from similar tools documented.
- [ ] Evidence of learning from previous attempts documented.
- [ ] Predictable failure patterns in this category identified and avoided.

##### 10.5 Methodological Soundness

- [x] Foreseeable failure modes identified at the umbrella-project level.
- [ ] Failure modes identified for each concrete experiment.
- [ ] Approach adapted based on those risks.
- [ ] No clear predictable reason for failure remains.

##### 10.6 Evidence of Use

- [x] Existing demos and event use to draw from.
- [ ] Testimonials gathered in one place.
- [ ] Metrics gathered in one place.
- [ ] Activity logs or users attached.
- [ ] Community validation documented.

---

#### 11. Final Reflection

To complete at the end:
- What I set out to do.
- What I actually built.
- What I learned about the field of political technology.
- How the project changed from conception to final version.
- What I would do next with more time.

---

<a id="doc-research-longlist-readme"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/research-longlist/README.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Research corpus

### Sparkle Bureaucracy Longlist Research

This directory contains the desk-research sprint for finding the projects, organisations, directories, people, and networks that can explain what Sparkle Bureaucracy aims to be.

The research question is:

> What does each candidate teach us about Sparkle Bureaucracy?

The exercise is editorial rather than exhaustive. Interviews and event-based research are deliberately deferred.

#### Target output

Four linked, evidence-backed longlists:

- 25 projects.
- 25 organisations.
- 25 project directories or archives.
- 25 relevant people or networks.

The discovery pool may temporarily contain 40–50 projects. The named 25-project longlist is the deduplicated and enriched result, not the raw capture set.

From the project list, select:

- 8–10 projects “we’re inspired by.”
- 4–6 projects doing valuable work where Sparkle is absent or unproven.
- A private reserve list for newsletters, event programming, and later research.

Projects remain the primary research unit. Each project should link to an organisation, relevant people or networks, and the directory or source through which it was discovered.

“Projects we're inspired by” is not synonymous with “projects that satisfy every Sparkle dimension.” A project can belong because it demonstrates one ingredient exceptionally well—such as affective presentation—even when its relationship to bureaucracy or direct civic capacity is indirect. The card should name that partial relationship honestly.

#### Research lanes

1. **Bureaucracy made emotionally legible:** games, fiction, installations, performances, and speculative design.
2. **Public-service process redesign:** courts, benefits, immigration, health access, permits, identity, appointments, and casework.
3. **AI inside civic processes:** consultation analysis, case triage, document assembly, synthetic representation, agents, and decision support.
4. **Participatory governance:** voting, deliberation, budgeting, petitions, citizen assemblies, and collective prioritisation.
5. **Organisational rituals:** permission, hiring, evaluation, grants, audits, workplace governance, and invisible labour.
6. **Under-resourced civic capacity:** community-led technology, legal access, migration, mutual aid, local government, and thin-capacity contexts.

#### Classifications

##### Sparkle exemplar

Strong connection between bureaucracy, affect, technology, and civic capacity.

##### Sparkle ancestor

Powerful bureaucratic ritual and affect, but not necessarily AI or civic infrastructure.

##### Infrastructure awaiting Sparkle

Useful civic or administrative work with little affective ambition, or no evidence about how it felt.

##### Warning case

Technology, playfulness, or friendliness that makes a coercive process more palatable without improving agency or contestability.

These are research relationships, not objective judgments about the quality of the projects.

#### Affect and evidence model

Affect has two separate research questions. Do not use the second as a gate for the first.

##### Designed affect

What feeling, atmosphere, tension, orientation, or change in perception is built into the work—and how does its form produce it? Evidence can be found in the artifact itself: its interaction, visual language, pacing, ritual, spatial design, copy, mechanics, sound, or deliberate removal of friction. Maker accounts and critical analysis can clarify that reading.

A project can qualify as Sparkly through designed affect without publications, events, surveys, participant quotations, or a formal evaluation. The presentation may itself be the affective intervention.

##### Experienced response

What, if anything, do we know about how people actually responded? Distinguish:

- **Participant-reported:** stated by someone who encountered the work.
- **Observed:** reported by a journalist, critic, researcher, or evaluator.
- **Maker-reported:** an audience response described by the maker.
- **Not researched or not found:** no reception evidence was identified in this pass.

Reception evidence can confirm, complicate, or contradict the designed affect. It is required only when the website claims that people actually felt or experienced something—not for recognising affective design.

Evidence confidence:

- **High:** primary project documentation plus strong independent or participant evidence.
- **Medium:** credible primary description with partial independent support.
- **Low:** incomplete, self-promotional, indirect, stale, or ambiguous evidence.

#### Project record

Each enriched project should record:

```text
Name
Dates/status
Country or community
Organisation
People/network
Public URL
Discovery source or directory

What it does
Bureaucratic ritual or process
Technology and its actual role
Designed affect and how the form produces it
Experienced response, if researched or available
Civic problem addressed
Who benefits
Relevance to under-resourced contexts
Where power sits
Whether decisions can be contested
Durable artifact or capability

Sparkle classification
What Sparkle Bureaucracy borrows
What is missing or concerning
Evidence confidence
Website readiness
```

#### Editorial tests

##### Explanatory value

Does the candidate help a reader understand an ingredient of Sparkle quickly?

##### Evidence quality

Can the factual description and emotional claim be supported by appropriate sources?

##### Portfolio value

Does it add a process, geography, community, technology, affect, or tension missing from the rest of the selection?

The final portfolio should include pleasant and unsettling affect; fictional and real institutions; AI and non-AI references; deployed and experimental work; community-led and government-led projects; multiple geographies; and both successes and revealing near-misses.

#### Website card

Each published project should answer:

> **What is it?**
>
> **What bureaucracy does it touch?**
>
> **What feeling or perception is designed into it—and how?**
>
> **What, if anything, do we know about audience response?**
>
> **What does Sparkle Bureaucracy borrow from it?**
>
> **What remains missing or unresolved?**

#### Working files

- [`calibration.md`](#doc-research-longlist-calibration): the six starter cases, provisional definition, and questions to pressure-test.
- `discovery-*.md`: raw parallel research by lane; useful as source material, not the final curated lists.
- [`longlists.md`](#doc-research-longlist-longlists): the final four linked lists of 25; every project links to its full evidence profile.
- `discovery-*.md`: the complete project evidence profiles, retained by research lane so evidence and rejected candidates are not lost during curation.
- [`website-selection.md`](#doc-research-longlist-website-selection): the editorial website portfolio, draft cards, reserve list, and founder questions.
- [`movements-and-tactics.md`](#doc-research-longlist-movements-and-tactics): the companion framework and two founder-nominated movement cases that prompted a separate research lane.
- [`movements-deep-research.md`](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research): the sourced 25-case movement/tactic longlist, twelve deep profiles, eight-case website recommendation, private reserve, and resulting Sparkle design principles.

---

<a id="doc-research-longlist-calibration"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/research-longlist/calibration.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Research corpus

### Sparkle Calibration

Status: working hypothesis for the longlist, not a settled definition.

The six starter cases are deliberately heterogeneous. They should reveal where the written rubric conflicts with the project's current gut feeling, rather than act as six examples that the rubric must automatically validate.

#### Scale

- **0 — absent or contrary:** the dimension is not present, or the project works against it.
- **1 — adjacent or claimed:** the dimension is contextual, weak, or asserted without evidence.
- **2 — designed and observable:** the project meaningfully addresses the dimension, with some supporting evidence.
- **3 — central and evidenced:** the dimension is essential to the work and supported by strong primary or independent evidence.

Scores are discussion prompts, not a total Sparkle score. A lopsided profile may be more informative than a high aggregate.

The affect score concerns affect designed into the form or encounter. Participant research can strengthen or challenge that reading, but it is not required for a project to score strongly.

#### Calibration matrix

| Case | Emerging technology | Bureaucratic process | Affect | Civic boost | Reveals power? | Durable capability / artifact? | Initial classification |
| --- | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | ---: | --- |
| **Sparkle Border Authority** | 1 | 3 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 2 | Sparkle prototype / calibration anchor |
| **Papers, Please** | 1 | 3 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 2 | Sparkle ancestor |
| **Cart Life** | 1 | 2 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 2 | Sparkle ancestor |
| **Document Assembly Line** | 2 | 3 | 1 | 3 | 2 | 3 | Infrastructure awaiting evidenced Sparkle |
| **Talk to the City** | 3 | 3 | 1 | 2 | 2 | 3 | Possible exemplar; affect unproven |
| **Project Mirror** | 3 | 3 | 2 | 1 | 2 | 3 | Sparkle prototype with unresolved consent risk |

#### Case notes

##### Sparkle Border Authority

- **Process:** preserves declarations, screening, approval, rejection, visas, checkpoints, admin overrides, and a final handoff.
- **Technology:** a complete digital service and printing workflow, but not emerging technology in the AI sense.
- **Affect:** play, co-created fiction, anticipation, and belonging were designed into the encounter. The diary says people played along fully, but direct participant evidence has not yet been preserved in the research record.
- **Civic boost:** demonstrates a transferable design move, but the original party implementation did not solve a civic service problem.
- **Power:** the gate remained real. The experiment makes enforcement theatrical, but it does not necessarily make it more contestable.
- **Calibration tension:** if this is the origin case, neither AI nor direct civic impact can currently be absolute requirements for something to feel Sparkly.
- **Sources:** [prototype](https://sparkle-border-authority.vercel.app/); [repository account](#doc-readme).

##### Papers, Please

- **Process:** document inspection, identity verification, rule changes, classification, approval, rejection, detention, quotas, and wages.
- **Technology:** digital simulation is integral to putting the audience in the inspector's role, but it is not an AI or contemporary emerging-technology project.
- **Affect:** discomfort, complicity, pressure, empathy, and the seduction of efficient rule-following are generated through the mechanics rather than added as decoration. This is independently discussed in games scholarship.
- **Civic boost:** produces civic understanding and moral reflection, but does not create a direct service capability.
- **Power:** exceptionally strong at revealing how distant rules become intimate administrative harm.
- **Calibration tension:** establishes that Sparkle may involve unsettling affect rather than optimism in the ordinary sense.
- **Sources:** [official press kit](https://papersplea.se/presskit/); [Game Studies analysis](https://gamestudies.org/1701/articles/morrissette); [Press Start review](https://press-start.gla.ac.uk/press-start/article/view/70).

##### Cart Life

- **Process:** permits, payments, custody, court dates, timekeeping, retail administration, and the paperwork surrounding precarious work.
- **Technology:** the simulation format makes routine pressure playable, but the work is not AI-native.
- **Affect:** boredom, fatigue, frustration, isolation, precarity, and humour arise from repetitive tasks and constrained time. Independent reviews report these effects directly.
- **Civic boost:** builds understanding of precarious work but does not deliver an institutional intervention.
- **Power:** makes the interaction between administrative obligations and economic precarity palpable.
- **Calibration tension:** it is less a redesign of bureaucracy than an affective encounter with bureaucracy. Does representation count, or must Sparkle intervene in a real process?
- **Sources:** [MIT Docubase](https://docubase.mit.edu/project/cart-life/); [Destructoid review](https://www.destructoid.com/reviews/review-cart-life/).

##### Document Assembly Line

- **Process:** converts legal questions into guided interviews, assembled court forms, and increasingly simplified e-filing.
- **Technology:** open-source document automation is central; newer LITEfile work uses AI for document identification, filing-code suggestions, pre-filling, and issue spotting.
- **Affect:** accessibility and reduced complexity are explicit design intentions. The current source set does not establish how self-represented litigants actually felt.
- **Civic boost:** gives courts, legal-aid organisations, and volunteers reusable infrastructure for helping people navigate legal processes.
- **Power:** increases procedural agency without changing the authority or underlying rules of the court.
- **Calibration tension:** may be exemplary civic infrastructure while remaining emotionally neutral. Is reduced anxiety itself Sparkle if it is evidenced?
- **Sources:** [Suffolk LIT Lab](https://suffolklitlab.org/); [Document Assembly Line](https://assemblyline.suffolklitlab.org/docs/); [2025 update including LITEfile](https://suffolklitlab.org/document-assembly-line-q4-2025-update/).

##### Talk to the City

- **Process:** ingests public input, extracts arguments, clusters related views, and produces navigable reports for deliberation and decision support.
- **Technology:** LLM analysis is essential rather than decorative.
- **Affect:** the stated ambitions include helping people feel heard and understand the discourse around them. The reviewed sources mostly describe maker intent; independent participant-reported affect remains thin.
- **Civic boost:** can make large volumes of qualitative participation more legible, although the connection from report to binding decision varies by deployment.
- **Power:** makes viewpoints visible but gives the summarisation pipeline considerable representational authority.
- **Calibration tension:** technical and civic fit is strong, while the felt encounter is less clear. Does Sparkle require an experience participants themselves enter?
- **Sources:** [project overview](https://ai.objectives.institute/talk-to-the-city-1); [introduction](https://ai.objectives.institute/blog/introducing-talk-to-the-city-our-collective-deliberation-tool); [GitHub](https://github.com/AIObjectives/talk-to-the-city-reports).

##### Project Mirror

- **Process:** evaluator selection, value constitutions, scoring, ranking, aggregation, deliberation, and an inspectable decision record.
- **Technology:** synthetic evaluator agents and multi-agent deliberation are central.
- **Affect:** the showcase produced fascination and discomfort around having an evaluative stance inferred. The diary records this, but the participant reflection should be attached as primary evidence before a public affect claim is made.
- **Civic boost:** expands participation experimentally inside a cohort, but has not yet demonstrated a boost for an under-resourced civic context.
- **Power:** exposes values and aggregation rules, while creating unresolved consent and authority problems around inferred representation.
- **Calibration tension:** a process may be transparent and affectively charged while still granting AI an uncomfortable form of authority.
- **Sources:** [Political Technology Awards](https://2025.newspeak.house/awards); [repository account](#doc-readme).

#### Provisional definition

> **Sparkle is an affective intervention in a consequential procedure: it lets people feel and understand how rules and power are moving through an encounter, creates more room to participate, question, or act, and leaves behind a capability or ritual rather than a decorative veneer.**

This definition currently treats emerging technology as Sparkle Bureaucracy's chosen arena, not as the universal essence of Sparkle. That distinction is unresolved.

#### Questions the longlist must pressure-test

1. Is emerging technology part of the definition of **Sparkle**, or a programme constraint for **Sparkle Bureaucracy**?
2. Must Sparkle improve how a process feels, or can it deliberately produce dread, guilt, grief, or moral discomfort?
3. Does representing a bureaucracy count, as in *Papers, Please* and *Cart Life*, or must the project intervene in a real procedure?
4. Does “optimistic” describe the emotion, the possibility of change, or the project's underlying politics?
5. Is civic imagination a sufficient civic boost, or must users gain a concrete capability?
6. Can a project be Sparkly when the underlying rule and allocation of power remain unchanged?
7. Is legibility enough, or must meaningful appeal or contestability be added?
8. Does reduced anxiety, confusion, or humiliation count as affective design even when a project avoids theatricality?
9. Whose affect matters: the service user, the bureaucrat, the audience, or all three?
10. When the website makes a claim about actual audience response, what is the minimum acceptable evidence—and how should that remain separate from our reading of the designed affect?
11. Must a durable artifact be adopted by an institution, or is a reusable cultural pattern enough?
12. Is a one-off event Sparkly if it changes relationships but leaves no formal tool behind?
13. Can playful design become anti-Sparkle by making coercion friendlier without making it weaker or more contestable?
14. Is the priority under-resourced contexts an inclusion rule, a scoring boost, or a commitment governing Sparkle Bureaucracy's own work?
15. Does every exemplar need all dimensions, or is Sparkle best understood as a family resemblance across projects?

#### Working inferences to revisit after discovery

- Affect is broader than delight; the most useful ancestors often create productive discomfort.
- Aesthetic whimsy is neither necessary nor sufficient.
- Technology should change the encounter or capability, not merely decorate it.
- Direct civic utility distinguishes an exemplar from an ancestor, but ancestors remain essential to explaining the idea.
- Power should remain visible. A friendly interface that hides coercion is a warning case.
- Designed affect can be established through the work's form, mechanics and presentation. Claims about actual audience response remain separate and should not be made without reception evidence.

---

### Linked Longlists

Research date: 30 July 2026
Status: curated desk-research longlists

The three discovery passes produced 50 project profiles, 49 organisation candidates, 41 directories or archives, and 47 people or networks before deduplication. The lists below are the editorial cut to 25 of each.

Projects are the primary research unit. Project IDs connect the lists; the linked discovery profiles contain the fuller evidence record, including designed affect, any available reception evidence, power, contestability, risks, and source confidence.

#### 25 projects

| ID | Project | Core ritual and Sparkle lesson | Relationship | Linked organisation / people | Evidence profile |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| P01 | [*Papers, Please*](https://papersplea.se/presskit/) | Border inspection, document checking, quotas, stamps and refusal become moral pressure. | Sparkle ancestor | O13 · N01 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |
| P02 | [*Cart Life*](https://docubase.mit.edu/project/cart-life/) | Permits, stock, rent, time and precarious work become exhaustion, tenderness and frustration. | Sparkle ancestor | N02 · D11 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |
| P03 | [New World Summit / People’s Parliament of Rojava](https://www.jonasstaal.nl/projects/new-world-summit-utrecht/) | Reassigns who is authorised to occupy parliament and turns speculative diplomacy into architecture. | Sparkle ancestor, close to exemplar | O14 · N03 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |
| P04 | [Transborder Immigrant Tool](https://arte-util.org/projects/transborder-immigrant-tool/) | Combines low-cost GPS, desert survival information and poetry outside the official border ritual. | Possible exemplar; deployment caveat | O15 · N04 · D08 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |
| P05 | [Open Hiring at Greyston Bakery](https://www.greyston.org/open-hiring) | Replaces the hiring audition with a first-come list and moves support to after entry. | Low-tech Sparkle exemplar | O16 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |
| P06 | [Models All the Way Down](https://knowingmachines.org/models-all-the-way) | Makes the administrative by-products and classificatory choices behind LAION-5B felt through visual storytelling, then publishes its audit data. | Inspired project; affective-legibility exemplar, indirect bureaucracy | O17 · N24 · D09 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance) |
| P07 | [Buurtzorg](https://www.buurtzorg.com/about-us/buurtzorgmodel/) | Uses shared care technology to support autonomous nursing teams rather than managerial surveillance. | Sparkle exemplar | O18 · N14 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |

| P22 | [amica](https://amica.gov.au/) | Replaces part of the adversarial separation ritual with a calm, bounded pathway to a quality-assured agreement. | Sparkle exemplar with safety caveat | O22 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance) |
| P23 | [vTaiwan](https://info.vtaiwan.tw/) | Connects agenda-setting, pol.is opinion mapping, facilitated deliberation and point-by-point institutional response. | Sparkle exemplar | O23 · N10 · N11 · N18 | [Full record](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance) |

| O22 | [National Legal Aid / Legal Services Commission of South Australia](https://nationallegalaid.org.au/) | Operates a safety-bounded digital pathway for separation and property agreements. | P22 |

| O24 | [Stanford Deliberative Democracy Lab](https://deliberation.stanford.edu/) | Produces unusually strong evidence about deliberation, knowledge, empathy, trust and opinion change. | P24 |
| O25 | [Estonian Cooperation Assembly](https://kogu.ee/en/) | Connects assemblies, citizen initiatives and evidence about what happens when institutional closure fails. | P25 |

This list intentionally mixes makers with practitioner networks. Sparkle Bureaucracy is interested in both individual design intelligence and the social infrastructure that lets projects persist.

#### Coverage check

- **Affect:** relief, dignity, belonging, moral discomfort, frustration, trust, empathy, suspense, certainty and productive disagreement.
- **Forms of institution:** courts, borders, benefits offices, hospitals, hiring, grants, planning, immigration, assemblies and care organisations.
- **Technology:** no-tech and low-tech process design, mobile and SMS, open digital infrastructure, foundation-dataset investigation, automation, LLM synthesis, generative imagery and multi-agent/collective-intelligence research.
- **Geography:** Europe, North America, Latin America, South Asia, Australia, Aotearoa New Zealand and transnational/crisis contexts.
- **Power:** projects that remove gates, share allocation, make interpretation inspectable, create appeal, preserve an asymmetric status quo, or fail at the institutional handoff.

The longlist deliberately retains evidence gaps, but missing audience research is not missing Sparkle. Designed affect is assessed through the artifact and encounter; reception evidence is recorded separately when available. A project belongs in “infrastructure awaiting Sparkle” only when affective design itself is weak, absent, or inaccessible—not merely because no survey or participant quotation was found.

<a id="doc-research-longlist-website-selection"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/research-longlist/website-selection.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Research corpus

### Website Selection

Status: editorial draft from the 25-project longlist
Research date: 30 July 2026

This is a proposed portfolio, not a claim that the selected projects call themselves Sparkle Bureaucracy. The aim is to explain the idea through a constellation of projects that each reveal a different ingredient.

#### Definition after the longlist

> **Sparkle is the designed charge in a consequential procedure: rules and power become possible to feel and understand, people gain meaningful room to participate, question, or act, and the encounter leaves behind civic capability rather than a decorative veneer.**

This is still provisional. The longlist currently suggests that emerging technology is Sparkle Bureaucracy's chosen material and historical context, while the deeper quality called Sparkle can also be found in low-tech forms, games, architecture, organisational design, and the deliberate removal of a bureaucratic ritual.

The strongest cross-case findings are:

- Affect is broader than delight. Relief, dignity, moral discomfort, certainty, trust, suspense, empathy and productive disagreement can all be relevant.
- Affect can reside in the presentation itself: visual language, pacing, interaction, ritual and mechanics can make an otherwise inaccessible system felt. Publications, events, surveys and participant testimony are not prerequisites for Sparkle.
- Status is affective infrastructure. Acknowledgement, tracking and closure often matter as much as a friendly front door.
- Sparkle may remove a ritual rather than decorate it.
- Technology often works best behind a trusted person or shared object, not as the sole civic interface.
- Staff relief is useful evidence, but it is not evidence that a resident felt heard.
- The institutional handoff is part of the experience. Invitation without response can reduce trust.
- The clearest warning pattern is affective legitimacy without recourse: a beautiful or friendly process that leaves power untouched and unchallengeable.

#### Proposed: 10 projects “we’re inspired by”

##### 1. *Papers, Please* — Lucas Pope

**What it is:** A 2013 game that puts the player behind an authoritarian border-inspection desk. **Bureaucracy:** documents, changing rules, quotas, approval, detention and refusal. **What people felt:** critics and games researchers describe moral discomfort, complicity, tension and the pressure to become ruthlessly efficient. **What SB borrows:** policy should arrive as a felt interface, with every mundane stamp connected to a human consequence. **Unresolved:** the migrant has almost no agency; the game diagnoses the institution rather than improving it. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals)

##### 2. *Models All the Way Down* — Christo Buschek and Jer Thorp

**What it is:** A visual investigation that follows how 5.8 billion image–text pairs became LAION-5B, an influential foundation dataset. **Bureaucratic relationship:** indirect. The dataset is an administrative artifact left by organised acts of crawling, classification, standardisation, threshold-setting and release; it is not itself a public bureaucracy. **What is the affect:** the presentation is the affect—scrolling image grids, accumulation, repetition and abrupt numerical thresholds turn an incomprehensible data supply chain into awe, alarm and suspicion. **What SB borrows:** make scale bodily comprehensible, trace mundane classifications to public consequences, and publish the evidence beneath the experience. **Unresolved:** it reveals and audits the pipeline but does not give people represented in the data a route to appeal or correction. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance)

##### 3. New World Summit / People’s Parliament of Rojava — Jonas Staal and partners

**What it is:** Alternative parliaments co-created with political movements excluded from dominant diplomatic forums, including a permanent parliament in Rojava. **Bureaucracy:** authorisation, representation, agenda-setting, translation, speech and institutional space. **What people felt:** participant and observer accounts describe solidarity, embodiment and a temporary displacement of helplessness, while also questioning the limits of symbolic recognition. **What SB borrows:** redesign the room, symbols and authorised participants together; allow speculative administration to harden into infrastructure. **Unresolved:** visibility and legitimacy do not automatically create political leverage, and curatorial power remains. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals)

##### 4. Project Re:form — Civilla and Michigan MDHHS

**What it is:** A statewide redesign of the Michigan benefits application and the staff practice around it. **Bureaucracy:** household disclosure, eligibility intake, evidence and caseworker processing. **What people felt:** applicants reported relief and confidence; staff described less-flustered applicants and gaining an extra minute to be human. **What SB borrows:** a form can become a respectful invitation without theatricality or AI, and frontline-worker affect can reinforce the resident experience. **Unresolved:** eligibility power remains with the state, and the evidence is hosted by the project maker. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 5. JustFix Letter of Complaint

**What it is:** A free tool that helps New York tenants generate and send a formal repair-demand letter. **Bureaucracy:** guided intake, official artifact, certified handoff, status, follow-up and escalation. **What people felt:** participant accounts describe the tool as accessible and capable of producing action where informal requests failed. **What SB borrows:** the full pipeline matters; a person should leave with an artifact that changes how they can approach power. **Unresolved:** a strong letter cannot by itself correct housing scarcity, retaliation or weak enforcement. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 6. Haqdarshak

**What it is:** An Indian entitlement-discovery and application service combining AI-supported matching with trained local agents, many of them women. **Bureaucracy:** scheme discovery, eligibility, documents, application and follow-up. **What people felt:** participant satisfaction and agent-empowerment evidence suggest the hybrid relationship is part of the intervention, not merely distribution. **What SB borrows:** let AI strengthen a trusted neighbour as the civic interface; build local capability and livelihoods alongside service access. **Unresolved:** outcome and affect claims need continued independent scrutiny, and the agent relationship can introduce new forms of mediation and dependence. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 7. OpenCRVS

**What it is:** Open, configurable civil-registration infrastructure for births and deaths, designed for low-connectivity contexts. **Bureaucracy:** local declaration, verification, registration, status updates and receipt of a foundational identity certificate. **What people felt:** implementation evidence includes families appreciating a more streamlined encounter, although qualitative affect evidence remains limited. **What SB borrows:** acknowledgement and status are emotional design; an institution should show that it has seen a person and where their case now sits. **Unresolved:** identity systems can deepen surveillance or exclusion unless correction, consent and recourse remain visible. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 8. Buurtzorg

**What it is:** A Dutch home-care organisation built around small, self-managing nursing teams and a shared digital care system. **Bureaucracy:** assessment, scheduling, allocation, record-keeping, team decisions and performance management. **What people felt:** research reports high employee and client satisfaction alongside warmer, more continuous relationships. **What SB borrows:** technology can remove managerial layers and support professional discretion rather than automate judgement; organisational structure itself can be affective design. **Unresolved:** international adaptations vary, and satisfaction does not replace scrutiny of labour intensity, costs or client appeal. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals)

##### 9. vTaiwan

**What it is:** A Taiwanese process connecting public agenda-setting, pol.is opinion mapping, facilitated deliberation and government response. **Bureaucracy:** issue intake, position mapping, consensus formation, stakeholder meetings and institutional disposition. **What people felt:** published research and participant accounts support the experience of seeing agreement across apparent camps, although participation is not representative by default. **What SB borrows:** make disagreement navigable, separate consensus from popularity, and require a visible response from the institution at the end. **Unresolved:** the process depends on volunteer capacity and political uptake; participation and consensus can still exclude quieter publics. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance)

##### 10. Habermas Machine — Google DeepMind and collaborators

**What it is:** A research prototype in which an AI drafts common-ground statements, receives participant criticism and revises them. **Bureaucracy:** individual positions become a mediated, challengeable collective artifact. **What people felt:** participants rated AI-generated statements as clearer, more informative and less biased than human-mediator statements in the published experiments. **What SB borrows:** synthesis should be iterative and contestable; measure perceived clarity and bias instead of declaring neutrality. **Unresolved:** preference for wording is not democratic legitimacy, minority disagreement can be smoothed away, and no public institution is required to act. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance)

#### Proposed companion section: movements we're inspired by

Movements should sit beside projects rather than inside the same rubric. Their authorship is collective and their durable artifact may be a replicable tactic, symbol, role or emotional repertoire.

> **Movement Sparkle is an affective civic tactic that makes institutional power possible to feel, gives people an inhabitable way to confront it, and leaves behind a repertoire others can reuse—without disguising the stakes or transferring attention away from those taking the risk.**

##### Mexico's Glitter Revolution / #NoMeCuidanMeViolan

Pink glitter turned a material associated with celebration and femininity into an adhesive mark on police authority during protests against sexual violence and institutional impunity. **What SB borrows:** let the material behave politically and mark power rather than merely decorating participants. **Unresolved:** media framing can detach the image from the violence and demands that produced it. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### Operation Inflation / Portland's inflatable anti-ICE protesters

Frogs and other oversized animals placed absurd, soft bodies against the armed seriousness of immigration enforcement. Shared costumes made the action into a role and helped make contemporary footage difficult to confuse with old Portland protest imagery. **What SB borrows:** create roles rather than mascots; design the public record as well as the live encounter. **Unresolved:** spectacle can attract attention away from people directly harmed by detention and deportation, while costumed participants still face real physical and legal risk. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### LASTESIS / *Un violador en tu camino*

A short, learnable choreography turned sexual violence from an individualised burden into a synchronised accusation of police, courts and the state. Its score travelled through local translation rather than fixed central control. **What SB borrows:** design a protocol people can embody, repeat and alter without losing the institutional target. **Unresolved:** repetition can become a decontextualised cultural event and participation can expose survivors to retaliation. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### Otpor!'s Milošević barrel

Passers-by paid to hit a barrel bearing the Serbian president's face; police could ignore the public ridicule or arrest the object and complete the joke. **What SB borrows:** map the institution's response options and design an encounter in which disproportion becomes self-describing. **Unresolved:** dilemma actions depend on context; repression is not always reputationally costly to the institution or physically tolerable to participants. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### Mothers of Plaza de Mayo

White headscarves made from children's nappies and a weekly circuit of the square turned enforced movement into a counter-procedure for names and cases the dictatorship tried to disappear. **What SB borrows:** when an institution withholds status, use garment, place and cadence to create a public register that returns. **Unresolved:** the language of motherhood can narrow whose grief is legible, and the organisations' later histories cannot be flattened into a single symbol. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### ACT UP

SILENCE=DEATH, die-ins and the 1988 FDA action joined graphic urgency and grief to deep procedural knowledge about trials, approvals, funding and insurance. **What SB borrows:** attention should lead into counter-expertise capable of contesting the rule, not stop at a memorable interface. **Unresolved:** famous imagery can erase the care, study and internal organisation that made the visible action consequential. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### Women of Liberia Mass Action for Peace

Christian and Muslim women wore white and sat together, then escalated from witness to physically preventing peace negotiators leaving without an agreement. **What SB borrows:** a counter-uniform can hold a coalition together, but it becomes civic capacity through an escalation path into leverage and later participation. **Unresolved:** popular retellings often reduce a complex movement to a “sex strike” and over-credit a single tactic for the peace outcome. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

##### Hīkoi mō te Tiriti and the parliamentary haka

Hīkoi, waiata and haka brought embodied Māori political protocol into direct contact with the Treaty Principles Bill; in Parliament, Hana-Rāwhiti Maipi-Clarke tore the document before leading the haka. **What SB borrows:** make the contested artifact tangible and bring living protocol into official procedure. **Unresolved:** culturally grounded forms are sources of principles, not free-floating formats an organisation may copy. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research)

[Read the complete 25-case movement and tactic longlist](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research), including reserves and the Holograms for Freedom warning study.

#### Proposed: 6 valuable projects where Sparkle is absent or unproven

##### 1. Document Assembly Line / Court Forms Online / LITEfile

An exemplary shared legal infrastructure: guided interviews, assembled forms, e-filing and newer AI assistance help courts and legal-aid groups serve self-represented litigants. It has durable artifacts, open-source maintenance and obvious civic value. What is missing from the public record is the felt experience of litigants themselves: whether the process reduced fear or humiliation, increased agency, or merely made a demanding court ritual technically completable. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 2. Talk to the City

This open LLM tool makes large bodies of testimony explorable without forcing people into survey boxes. Its reports retain links from clusters to underlying source material, which is an important contestability pattern. Yet the strongest affect claim—helping contributors feel heard—is still primarily maker intent. A clear map of public voice can also make the model's omissions appear authoritative, and the final decision handoff varies by deployment. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance)

##### 3. Pathfinder

Pathfinder converts unstable immigration-court data into alerts and case intelligence for nonprofit attorneys and legal-service providers. Practitioners report greater clarity and operational confidence, which can indirectly benefit clients in high-stakes cases. The missing evidence is the immigrant's own experience and agency: the technology currently sparkles most clearly for the professional intermediary. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 4. HRC Explorer Grants

The Health Research Council of New Zealand uses a short anonymous application, threshold review and random allocation among qualified proposals. It is a rigorous and refreshingly honest response to uncertainty and prestige bias, backed by applicant survey evidence. Its affect is not explicitly designed, however, and acceptance of the lottery is strongly influenced by whether an applicant won. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals)

##### 5. Clear My Record

Code for America's work helped transform criminal-record clearance from an individual petition into government-initiated bulk relief. It may be Sparkly precisely because a humiliating ritual disappears and the procedural burden moves back to the institution. But the project has little published evidence about whether people knew relief had occurred or how it felt; invisible automation without notice or correction can become another opaque system. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-public-civic)

##### 6. Helsinki Summer Streets with UrbanistAI

Residents, businesses and planners used generative imagery to turn words and rough drawings into shared street proposals, then discussed and tested their feasibility. The workshop format is visually vivid and plausibly lowers professional-language barriers. The reviewed sources do not publish independent participant testimony or before/after affect measures, so claims about excitement, ownership or empowerment should remain explicitly unproven. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-ai-governance)

#### Private reserve list

These projects remain useful for newsletters, events, future essays, or substitution if a selected card fails deeper verification.

| Project | Why retain it | Why not in the first website set |
| --- | --- | --- |
| [*Cart Life*](https://docubase.mit.edu/project/cart-life/) | Repetition, scarce time and mundane administrative work produce exhaustion, tenderness and empathy around precarious survival. | Moved to reserve to make room for *Models All the Way Down*, which adds an AI-native visual investigation while *Papers, Please* retains the game-based ancestor role. [Evidence profile](#doc-research-longlist-discovery-affect-rituals) |
| [Transborder Immigrant Tool](https://arte-util.org/projects/transborder-immigrant-tool/) | An exceptional combination of accessible emerging technology, poetry and literal survival information. | Real-world deployment and intended-user experience need stronger verification. |
| [Open Hiring](https://www.greyston.org/open-hiring) | A memorable reversal of the hiring gate with a durable operating history. | Technology is not central; the first set already contains several strong low-tech structural cases. |
| [Cobudget](https://cobudget.com/) | Makes shared authority over money tangible and social. | Better held for an organisational-governance essay or event with direct practitioner input. |
| [mRelief](https://www.mrelief.com/) | Strong combination of dignity, channel choice, participant testimony and causal uptake evidence. | Overlaps with Project Re:form in the first public portfolio; excellent replacement if more emerging technology is desired. |
| [Upsolve](https://upsolve.org/) | Gives a rigorous court procedure a clear emotional horizon: relief and a fresh start. | Overlaps with JustFix and Document Assembly Line in a legal-access-heavy selection. |
| [Refugee.Info / Signpost](https://www.signpost.ngo/) | Reciprocal, multilingual humanitarian navigation with trusted local moderators. | AI/data governance and attribution across deployments require careful project-specific wording. |
| [amica](https://amica.gov.au/) | Calm, bounded redesign of an emotionally charged legal ritual. | Safety, coercion and suitability exclusions deserve a deeper review before public praise. |
| [America in One Room](https://deliberation.stanford.edu/news/america-one-room) | Unusually strong evidence for empathy, value and reduced hostility inside deliberation. | Its institutional handoff is advisory, and vTaiwan/Habermas Machine already cover deliberative encounters. |
| [Rahvakogu](https://kogu.ee/en/activity/peoples-assembly/) | The strongest warning that a good invitation can worsen trust when the institution fails to close the process. | Better used as an explicit warning essay or tension card than placed among inspirations. |

#### Questions requiring a founder decision

The desk research cannot settle these by itself:

1. Is emerging technology part of the definition of **Sparkle**, or a programme constraint for **Sparkle Bureaucracy**?
2. Can moral discomfort and grief count as Sparkle, or must the final emotional direction be hopeful?
3. Must a project transfer or contest power, or is making power legible enough?
4. Can the disappearance of a procedure be Sparkle, or must there be a redesigned encounter?
5. Is under-resourced civic use a hard inclusion rule, a scoring boost, or a commitment governing only Sparkle Bureaucracy's own projects?
6. Should the public website distinguish **designed affect** from **evidenced affect**, even if that makes some project cards visibly inconclusive?
7. Should Sparkle Border Authority and Project Mirror appear alongside outside inspirations as calibration anchors, or remain only under “our projects”?

<a id="doc-research-longlist-movements-and-tactics"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/research-longlist/movements-and-tactics.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Research corpus

### Movements and Tactics We're Inspired By

Status: companion research framework and founder-nominated seed cases
Research date: 30 July 2026

The subsequent deep research run is in [`movements-deep-research.md`](#doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research). It contains the calibrated movement rubric, a sourced 25-case longlist, twelve full evidence profiles, an eight-case website recommendation, private reserves, and the resulting design principles. This file retains the two cases that prompted the run and the reasoning for treating movements separately from authored projects.

This is a companion to the project longlist, not a fifth project classification. Movements often have diffuse authorship, changing names, contested boundaries and no single deliverable. Treating them as products would erase the collective action that makes them powerful.

The website-facing title can be **Movements we're inspired by**. Internally, research both the movement and the specific tactic: glitter, costume, humour, song, ritual, occupation, mutual aid, counter-documentation or another affective device.

#### Why this belongs in Sparkle Bureaucracy

Movements can reveal a different form of Sparkle:

- Affect is a method of civic action, not a decorative communications layer.
- Humour, beauty, camp, absurdity, grief or rage can change who feels able to enter a confrontation.
- A memorable material or image can interrupt the state's preferred account of an event.
- The tactic can make coercive institutions visible while refusing their emotional script.
- Replication can leave behind a distributed capability even when there is no organisation, platform or formal publication.

These cases should not be scored against the complete project rubric. Research them through:

```text
Movement and tactic
Date and place
Institutional power confronted
Material or affective device
Feeling or perception designed into the action
How the tactic changes participation, visibility or narrative
How it circulates or becomes replicable
Who is taking the risk
Power, safety, appropriation and media-framing cautions
What Sparkle Bureaucracy borrows
Sources and attribution limits
```

#### Starter cases

##### 1. Mexico's Glitter Revolution / #NoMeCuidanMeViolan

- **Date and place:** Mexico City and other Mexican cities, August 2019 onward.
- **Movement/tactic:** Feminist protests against violence toward women, institutional impunity and alleged police sexual violence used pink glitter as a collective symbol and direct intervention. Protesters threw glitter at Mexico City's police chief; it also became part of signs, bodies and the wider visual language of the demonstrations.
- **Institutional power confronted:** Police violence, the handling of complaints and investigations, official credibility, and the wider failure of the state to prevent or respond to gender-based violence and femicide.
- **Designed affect:** Glitter holds celebration, femininity and apparent frivolity while being invasive, adhesive and extremely difficult to contain. Against uniforms, offices and police procedure, it turns rage into a visible contamination of official authority. Its sparkle does not soften the demand; it makes the refusal harder to control or forget.
- **How it changes participation and narrative:** The material is inexpensive, portable and recognisable. It creates an image that can travel beyond a march while rejecting the expectation that political anger must look solemn or respectable to count.
- **Durable capability:** A replicable protest language rather than a formal organisation or tool.
- **What SB borrows:** Choose a material whose cultural meaning conflicts with the institution's emotional script; let the affective device mark power rather than merely decorate participants; create symbols that can be copied without central permission.
- **Cautions:** Do not reduce demands about violence and impunity to an attractive visual identity. Credit the protesters and political conditions, not the publication that later explained the tactic. Glitter also carries environmental and safety concerns that should not be casually reproduced.
- **Source:** Lauren Cocking, [“Mexico's Glitter Revolution Is Taking Aim at Violence Against Women”](https://www.teenvogue.com/story/mexico-protest-glitter-explainer), *Teen Vogue*, 28 August 2019.

##### 2. Operation Inflation and Portland's inflatable anti-ICE protesters

- **Date and place:** Portland, Oregon; the inflatable-costume tactic expanded during anti-ICE demonstrations in 2025 and continued as a recognisable protest form into 2026.
- **Movement/tactic:** After an inflatable frog appeared outside Portland's ICE facility, protesters adopted frogs and other oversized animal costumes. The distributed effort became associated with **Operation Inflation**, which helped make costumes available to more participants.
- **Institutional power confronted:** Immigration detention and enforcement, armed federal presence, policing of protest, and official narratives portraying opposition as inherently threatening or disorderly.
- **Designed affect:** Absurdity, humour, joy and ridicule are placed directly against uniforms, weapons and institutional seriousness. The soft, ungainly bodies make it difficult to sustain a purely menacing visual account of the crowd. The costumes do not deny danger; they refuse to let the state determine the encounter's emotional register.
- **How it changes participation and narrative:** A costume supplies an immediately legible role, a shared visual identity and a tactic that others can repeat. Its media visibility helped the image travel beyond one protest and turn a lone frog into a distributed repertoire.
- **Durable capability:** A recognisable, low-instruction protest format and a growing shared supply of costumes rather than a conventional project artifact.
- **What SB borrows:** Use absurdity to expose the theatricality already present in state power; create roles people can inhabit quickly; design a visual contradiction that survives hostile framing; let a symbol become collective rather than keeping it as a branded mascot.
- **Cautions:** Spectacle must not eclipse people directly harmed by immigration enforcement. Costumes do not remove physical or legal risk, and reporting about the tactic should not be mistaken for evidence about the movement's full demands or organisation.
- **Sources:** [Oregon Public Broadcasting reporting on the adoption of Operation Inflation costumes](https://news.google.com/rss/articles/CBMilAFBVV95cUxQTXNOaXREZzVxZFFiUUFQVkRqR2k5UkttNE1NTzZqUktOOFFDUmxSaDlkN0xLNm5kdVZGR19XcGt0cHhQWkxvVkU3aENpMElTZW9xdHlzbjdLTEtuSnVRTi02ZERGQzRqS3pQcmNOSDhvZFQtYzR1dUExZzQ4dDJzQ2trTFJUUzM1UktncEtYS2hOcXlL?oc=5), 30 March 2026; additional reporting should be source-audited before publication.

#### Editorial guardrails

- Attribute collective tactics carefully; do not invent a singular designer.
- Separate the movement's political demands from the tactic through which it became visually legible.
- Ask who is exposed to arrest, retaliation, surveillance or misrepresentation.
- Do not treat virality as civic impact.
- Do not praise “playfulness” when it trivialises harm or recentres spectators over affected communities.
- Record what the tactic changes: participation, morale, narrative, visibility, material support or institutional response.
- Prefer movements whose affect reveals or contests power, rather than making coercion more pleasant.

#### Discovery directions used in the deep run

The private research pool is broad, while the proposed public section remains small and globally varied. The search included:

- feminist and queer protest aesthetics;
- carnivalesque and absurdist resistance;
- protest puppetry, inflatables, projection and light;
- songs, chants, mourning rituals and collective silence;
- counter-mapping, witnessing and citizen documentation;
- disability-led access tactics and mutual-aid rituals;
- movements that appropriate forms, uniforms, receipts, permits or official language;
- cases where a compelling aesthetic was co-opted or obscured the underlying demand.

Eight well-contextualised cases are recommended for the first website set. The 25-case research list remains private infrastructure for future writing and programming.

---

<a id="doc-research-longlist-movements-deep-research"></a>

> **Source:** `lore/research-longlist/movements-deep-research.md` · inlined verbatim, 7 August 2026 · group: Research corpus

### Movements and Affective Tactics: Deep Research Run

Status: evidence-backed longlist and editorial recommendation
Research date: 30 July 2026
Scope: collective tactics that make institutional power felt, interrupt its emotional script, and give people a reusable way to act

#### Outcome

The two founder-nominated anchors—Mexico's Glitter Revolution and Portland's inflatable anti-ICE protests—show that this is not simply a search for “creative protest.” The useful research unit has two layers:

1. **The movement:** the people, demand, political conditions and institutional power being confronted.
2. **The tactic:** the material, role, ritual, choreography or image through which that confrontation becomes felt and repeatable.

Treating only the second layer as the work would turn political struggle into an aesthetic reference board. Treating only the first would miss the designed affect that makes these cases relevant to Sparkle Bureaucracy.

##### Provisional definition

> **Movement Sparkle is an affective civic tactic that makes institutional power possible to feel, gives people an inhabitable way to confront it, and leaves behind a repertoire others can reuse—without disguising the stakes or transferring attention away from those taking the risk.**

This is a companion to the main Sparkle definition, not a replacement for it. A movement tactic does not need AI, a formal organisation, a publication, or reception research to be instructive. Its durable artifact may be a symbol, score, role, gesture, chant, garment or repeated procedure.

#### Calibrated research rubric

The original project rubric still contributes two questions: what bureaucracy or consequential process is touched, and what affect is designed? Movement cases need five further tests.

1. **Institutional target:** Which law, office, police power, administrative failure, negotiation or public narrative is being confronted?
2. **Affective mechanism:** What does the material or action do—not merely signify—to alter fear, grief, ridicule, dignity, solidarity or attention?
3. **Inhabitable role:** Does the tactic give participants something comprehensible to wear, do, repeat, carry, sing or make?
4. **Strategic change:** Is there evidence that it altered participation, public narrative, movement morale, institutional response or material capacity?
5. **Distributed durability:** Can it travel without a central author, and what knowledge or infrastructure survives the moment?
6. **Power legibility:** Does it expose coercion and open room for agency, or merely make confrontation attractive to spectators?
7. **Risk and attribution:** Who faces arrest, violence, surveillance, appropriation or misrepresentation, and whose authorship is being simplified?

##### Four separate evidence claims

Do not collapse these into one “impact” score:

- **Designed affect:** readable in the form and supported by maker or critical accounts.
- **Experienced response:** reported by participants or observers; useful but not required to recognise affective design.
- **Strategic effect:** a change in participation, framing, morale, replication or institutional behaviour.
- **Political outcome:** a law, decision, negotiation or allocation changed. Attribution will almost always be shared and contested.

Virality is evidence of circulation, not evidence of civic impact. An attractive image without an institutional target or participatory role belongs in a visual reference folder, not this list.

#### Six discovery lanes

1. **Material transformation:** glitter, cloth, umbrellas, brooms and everyday objects whose ordinary meaning is turned against authority.
2. **Counter-uniforms and roles:** costumes or dress codes that let people inhabit a collective civic character.
3. **Choreography and collective voice:** repeated movement, song, silence, call-and-response and sound.
4. **Witnessing, grief and counter-archives:** names, bodies, memorial forms and rituals that refuse administrative disappearance.
5. **Counter-procedure:** citizen-made assemblies, vigils, marches and negotiations that answer a failed or exclusionary official process.
6. **Spectacle and dilemma:** humour, absurdity or technology that makes repression reputationally costly and inaction permissive.

#### 25-case longlist

`Website` marks the recommended first public set. `Deep reserve` means there is enough substance for a future card or newsletter. `Research reserve` means the case is useful but needs tighter attribution, outcome or safety research. `Warning study` means the unresolved tension is itself valuable.

| ID | Movement and tactic | Place / dates | Institution or process confronted | Affective device and civic mechanism | Disposition | Starting evidence |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| M01 | Glitter Revolution / `#NoMeCuidanMeViolan` | Mexico, 2019– | Police sexual violence, complaint handling and state impunity | Pink glitter changes apparent frivolity into adhesive rage: it marks officials and travels as a decentralised protest language. | **Website** | [Teen Vogue explainer](https://www.teenvogue.com/story/mexico-protest-glitter-explainer); [research on the material and digital “cyborg movida”](https://www.tandfonline.com/doi/full/10.1080/01436597.2025.2456839) |
| M02 | Operation Inflation / Portland inflatable anti-ICE protests | United States, 2025–26 | Immigration detention, federal force, protest policing and official claims of disorder | Soft, unwieldy animal bodies refuse the state's menacing visual script; shared costumes turn a lone frog into a role others can enter. | **Website** | [OPB interview with Operation Inflation](https://www.opb.org/article/2026/03/30/think-out-loud-operation-inflation-animal-costumes/); [AP reporting](https://apnews.com/article/7017b62b45eab1cac82a169a763af359) |
